\displaystyle=(-1)^{|{x}^{\vee}_{j}|+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\geq 1\\
1\leq i_{l},\ldots,i_{1}\leq k\end{subarray}}c_{i_{l},\ldots,i_{1}}^{j}{(x_{i_{l}}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{i_{1}})}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|{x}^{\vee}_{j}|+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\geq 1\\
1\leq i_{l},\ldots,i_{1}\leq k\end{subarray}}c_{i_{l},\ldots,i_{1}}^{j}(-1)^{\sum_{1\leq s<t\leq l}|{x}^{\vee}_{i_{s}}||{x}^{\vee}_{i_{t}}|}~{x}^{\vee}_{i_{1}}\ldots{x}^{\vee}_{i_{l}}.

Remark 3.15
.

The Koszul dual
A^{!}
is indeed a dg algebra. Abstractly speaking, the dual of the operator
\Delta
is the ordinary product
{\Delta}^{\vee}:{T(\bar{A}[1])}^{\vee}\otimes{T(\bar{A}[1])}^{\vee}\to{T(\bar{A}[1])}^{\vee}
. Dualizing
\mu_{A}^{2}=0
gives
({\mu}^{\vee}_{A})^{2}=0
and dualizing the co-Leibniz rule for
\mu_{A}
with respect to
\Delta
gives the Leibniz rule for
\mu_{A}
with respect to
{\Delta}^{\vee}
. The signs can be checked by hand.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
A
be an augmented
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Let
M
and
N
be graded vector spaces and
f:M\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])\to N
a linear map. Then the
Koszul transform
of
f
is the partial dual map
{f}^{\vee}:M\to N\otimes A^{!}
. It is a graded linear map with characterizing property

\forall m\in M,~a\in\,T(\bar{A}[1]):\quad\langle{f}^{\vee}(m),a\rangle=f(m\otimes a).

Here
\langle-,-\rangle
liberally denotes the standard pairing between
{T(\bar{A}[1])}^{\vee}
and
T(\bar{A}[1])
, in this case as map

\langle-,-\rangle:(N\otimes{T(\bar{A}[1])}^{\vee})\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])\to N.

We copy
Definition 2.2
and adapt it slightly to the dg case.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
D
be a dg algebra. Then the category
\operatorname{Add}D
is the category of formal shifted sums of copies of
D
with hom spaces given as spaces of matrices with entries
D
. The differential
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}D}
and product
\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}
are the linear and bilinear extension of differential and product of
D
, with a sign change. On single matrix entries, the sign change is as follows:

\displaystyle\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}D}(a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-l}d_{D}a,\quad\forall a:D[k]\to D[l],

\displaystyle\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}(a,b)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|a|_{D}(k-l)}ab,\quad\forall a:D[l]\to D[m],~b:D[k]\to D[l].

Definition 3.18
.

Let
D
be a dg algebra. Then a twisted complex over
D
is an element
X\in\operatorname{Add}D
together with an element
\delta\in\operatorname{End}_{\operatorname{Add}D}^{1}(X)
such that
\delta
is upper triangular and satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation:

\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}D}(\delta)+\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}(\delta,\delta)=0.

The differential and product on
\operatorname{Tw}D
follow the sign rule

\displaystyle\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Tw}D}(f)

\displaystyle=\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}D}(f)+\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}(\delta,f)+(-1)^{|f|+1}\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}(f,\delta),

\displaystyle\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Tw}D}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}(f,g).

The product
\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}
is comparable to a matrix product and similarly
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}D}
acts as entry-wise differential. As preparation for the Koszul duality functor, we prove a few properties regarding the Koszul transform. We show that the Koszul transform of
f\circ g
agrees with the matrix product
\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}
of
{f}^{\vee}
and
{g}^{\vee}
and that taking the Koszul transform of
f\circ\mu_{A}
amounts to taking the entry-wise differential
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}D}
of
{f}^{\vee}
. The precise statement is as follows:

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional augmented
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Let
f:M\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])\to N\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])
and
g:L\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])\to M\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])
be coderivations. Then

\displaystyle\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}({f}^{\vee}_{0},{g}^{\vee}_{0})

\displaystyle={(f\circ g)_{0}}^{\vee}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}D}({f}^{\vee}_{0})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|f|+1}{(f\circ\mu_{A})_{0}}^{\vee}.

(3.4)

Proof.

The simplest way to evaluate both statements is by choosing bases for
A
,
L
,
M
and
N
. Let
X,Y\in T(\bar{A}[1])
be basis elements homogeneous with respect to both tensor degree and
\|\cdot\|_{\bar{A}[1]}
. Let
\varepsilon_{L}\in L
and
\varepsilon_{M}\in M
and
\varepsilon_{N}\in\,N
be further basis elements. Let
f
and
g
be given coderivations. They are determined solely by their first component
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
. Since the statement is linear in
f
and
g
, we may assume that
g_{0}(\varepsilon_{L}\otimes X)=\varepsilon_{M}
and
f_{0}(\varepsilon_{M}\otimes Y)=\varepsilon_{N}
and that
f_{0}
and
g_{0}
vanish on all other basis elements for
L\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])
and
M\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])
. The Koszul transforms of
f
,
g
read

\displaystyle{f}^{\vee}(\varepsilon_{M})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}\otimes{Y}^{\vee},

\displaystyle{g}^{\vee}(\varepsilon_{L})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{M}\otimes{X}^{\vee}.

Let us now show (
3.3
). We note that
(f\circ g)_{0}
vanishes on all basis elements, except

(f\circ g)_{0}(\varepsilon_{L}\otimes X\otimes Y)=(-1)^{|g|\|Y\|}f(g(\varepsilon_{L}\otimes X)\otimes Y)=(-1)^{|g||{Y}^{\vee}|}\varepsilon_{N}.

Therefore
{(f\circ g)}^{\vee}
and
{f}^{\vee}\cdot{g}^{\vee}
vanish on all basis elements of
L
, except

\displaystyle{(f\circ g)}^{\vee}(\varepsilon_{L})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|g||{Y}^{\vee}|}\varepsilon_{N}\otimes{(X\otimes Y)}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|g||{Y}^{\vee}|+|{X}^{\vee}||{Y}^{\vee}|}\varepsilon_{N}\otimes{Y}^{\vee}{X}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(|\varepsilon_{L}|-|\varepsilon_{M}|)|{Y}^{\vee}|}\varepsilon_{N}\otimes{Y}^{\vee}{X}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(|\varepsilon_{L}|-|\varepsilon_{M}|)|{Y}^{\vee}|}({f}^{\vee}\cdot{g}^{\vee})(\varepsilon_{L})

\displaystyle=\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}D}({f}^{\vee},{g}^{\vee})(\varepsilon_{L}).

In the third row we have used that
|g|+|\varepsilon_{L}|=|{X}^{\vee}|+|{X}^{\vee}|
. This proves (
3.3
).

We now prove (
3.4
). It is our aim to compute the composition
(f\circ\mu_{A})_{0}=f_{0}\circ\mu_{A}
. Since
f_{0}
vanishes on all basis elements except
\varepsilon_{M}\otimes Y
and (
3.4
) is linear in
\mu_{A}
itself, we may simply assume that
\mu_{A}(\varepsilon_{M}\otimes Z)=\varepsilon_{M}\otimes Y
for some basis element
Z\in\,T(\bar{A}[1])
and
\mu_{A}
vanishes on all other basis elements. Then
(f\circ\mu_{A})_{0}
vanishes on all basis elements of
L\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])
, except

(f\circ\mu_{A})_{0}(\varepsilon_{M}\otimes Z)=f_{0}(\varepsilon_{M}\otimes Y)=\varepsilon_{N}.

We see that
{(f\circ\mu_{A})_{0}}^{\vee}
vanishes on all basis elements of
M
, except

\displaystyle{(f\circ\mu_{A})_{0}}^{\vee}(\varepsilon_{M})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{N}\otimes{Z}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|{Y}^{\vee}|+1}\varepsilon_{N}\otimes d{Y}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|f|+1}\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}D}({f}^{\vee})(\varepsilon_{M}).

In the last row, we have used that
|\varepsilon_{M}|+|f|=|\varepsilon_{N}|+|{Y}^{\vee}|
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

In
Lemma 3.19
, it is essential that
f
and
g
be coderivations. For instance,
\mu_{A}:M\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])\to M\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])
is not a coderivation on its own and in fact its zeroth component
\mu_{M,0}
vanishes, while a composition
f\circ\mu_{A}
may again have nonvanishing zeroth component and therefore nonvanishing Koszul transform.

In
Corollary 3.21
, we recall the Koszul duality functor between modules over
A
and twisted complexes over
A^{!}
. The idea is to apply “Koszul transform” the action map of every module. The construction is functorial, therefore gives rise to a dg functor.

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional augmented
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Then the following defines a dg functor:

\displaystyle F:\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}\operatorname{Tw}A^{!},

\displaystyle(M,\mu_{M})

\displaystyle\xmapsto{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}(M\otimes A^{!},{\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0}),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}{f}^{\vee}_{0}.

We call
F
the
Koszul duality functor
of
A
.

Proof.

There are three items to check. First, we show that
{\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0}
satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation. Then, we check that
F
preserves differential and product. We start with the Maurer-Cartan equation:

\displaystyle\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}A^{!}}({\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0})+\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}A^{!}}({\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0},{\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0})

\displaystyle={(\mu_{M,0}\circ\mu_{A})}^{\vee}+{(\mu_{M,0}\circ\mu_{M})}^{\vee}

\displaystyle={(\mu_{M}\circ\mu_{A}+\mu_{M}\circ\mu_{M}+\mu_{A}\circ\mu_{M})_{0}}^{\vee}=0.

In the second row, we have used that
(\mu_{A}\circ\mu_{M})_{0}=0
. Next, for every coderivation
f:M\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])\to N\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])
we have

\displaystyle F(\mu^{1}(f))

\displaystyle={((\mu_{N}+\mu_{A})\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ(\mu_{M}+\mu_{A}))_{0}}^{\vee}

\displaystyle={(\mu_{N}\circ f)_{0}}^{\vee}-(-1)^{|f|}{(f\circ\mu_{M})_{0}}^{\vee}-(-1)^{|f|}{(f\circ\mu_{A})_{0}}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}A^{!}}({\mu}^{\vee}_{N,0},{f}^{\vee}_{0})-(-1)^{|f|}\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}A^{!}}({f}^{\vee}_{0},{\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0})+\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Add}A^{!}}({f}^{\vee}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}}(F(f)).

If additionally
g:L\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])\to M\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])
is a coderivation, then

F(\mu^{2}(f,g))=F(f\circ g)={(f\circ g)_{0}}^{\vee}=\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}A^{!}}({f}^{\vee},{g}^{\vee})=\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}}(F(f),F(g)).

This shows that
F
is a dg functor and finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

Strictly speaking, the object
F(M)=(M\otimes A^{!},{\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0})
only becomes a twisted complex upon choice of a graded basis for
M
. Furthermore,
{\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0}
need not be an upper triangular matrix. However, if
\bar{A}
is concentrated in positive degrees, then sorting the basis elements of
M
in order of descending degree makes
{\mu}^{\vee}_{M,0}
upper triangular.

3.3
Classical Koszual duals

In this section, we comment on the relations of the modern with the classical Koszul dual construction. Classical Koszul duality is namely a phenomenon known for ordinary algebras and we recall here its typical properties: First, the double Koszul dual
(A^{!})^{!}
is
A
again. Second, the Koszul dual algebra is formal. Third, the Koszul algebra is the Ext algebra of its simple module
\mathbb{C}=A/\bar{A}
. In the present section, we recall these statements in the classical context and recall how they translate to modern Koszul duality. A valuable source is
[
28
]
.

Koszul duality has classically been a correspondence between Koszul algebras, a class of ordinary algebras with quadratic relations:

Ordinary algebra
\frac{V\otimes V}{R}=\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle X,Y\rangle}{(XY-YX)}
Ordinary algebra
\frac{{V}^{\vee}\otimes{V}^{\vee}}{R^{\perp}}=\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle X,Y\rangle}{(X^{2},Y^{2},XY+YX)}
Koszul

The relations on either side are the “orthogonal complement” of the relations on the other side along the pairing
(V\otimes V)\otimes({V}^{\vee}\otimes{V}^{\vee})\to\mathbb{C}
. In particular, classical Koszul duality is an involution from the beginning.

Koszul duality for
A_{\infty}
-algebras is not a one-way street either. If
A
is an augmented finite-dimensional
A_{\infty}
-algebra, we regard the double dual
(A^{!})^{!}
. It is possible that this dg algebra is quasi-isomorphic to
A
itself. However, the double Koszul dual construction applies vector space duals twice so that finiteness conditions are required to match
A
exactly with
(A^{!})^{!}
.

A sufficient finiteness criterion can be formulated if one assumes that the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
has an additional grading, also referred to as
Adams grading
. The direct sum decomposition
A=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}\oplus\bar{A}
is supposed to be compatible and the products
\mu^{k}
are assumed to be homogeneous with respect to the Adams grading. One then says that
A
is
Adams connected
if the homogeneous part of
\bar{A}
with respect to any Adams degree
j\in\mathbb{Z}
is finite-dimensional and vanishes either for all
j\leq 0
or all
j\geq 0

[
28
]
. This connectedness assumption is a sufficient finiteness criterion and ensures that the double Koszul
(A^{!})^{!}
is quasi-isomorphic to
A
again:

Theorem 3.23

(
[
28
]
)
.

Let
A
be an augmented
A_{\infty}
-algebra. If
A
is Adams connected, then
(A^{!})^{!}
and
A
are quasi-isomorphic as
A_{\infty}
-algebras.

The classical Koszul dual is automatically an ordinary graded algebra, without the need to pass to cohomology. From a modern perspective, Koszul duals are dg algebras and only passing to cohomology
\operatorname{H}A^{!}
and forgetting the
A_{\infty}
-structure gives an ordinary graded algebra. Koszul algebras are certain types of algebras distinguished by the property that their Koszul dual
A^{!}
tends to be a formal dg algebra. This way, one can forget the higher structure on
\operatorname{H}A^{!}
and recover classical Koszul duality. For sake of completeness, we recall the definition here:

Definition 3.24

(
[
4
]
)
.

A graded associative algebra
A
is
Koszul
if it is positively graded
A=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}A^{i}
, we have
A^{0}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}
and
\mathbb{C}=A/\bigoplus_{i>0}A^{i}
as an
A
-module has a resolution of graded
A
-modules

\ldots\to P^{2}\to P^{1}\to P^{0}\to A\to 0

in which every
P^{i}
is generated by its degree
i
component:
P^{i}=AP_{i}^{i}
.

In
[
28
]
a precise criterion was given for formality:

Theorem 3.25

(
[
28
, Corollary 2.7]
)
.

Let
A
be an
(a,b)
-generated Koszul algebra in the sense of
[
28
, Definition 2.6]
. Then we have an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism
A^{!}\cong\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
.

Remark 3.26
.

Classical Koszul duality is full of correspondences between types of algebras. Folklore statements include that Koszul algebras correspond to Koszul algebras, Gorenstein corresponds to Gorenstein, Artin-Schelter regular corresponds to Frobenius
[
28
, Corollary D, E]
.

Remark 3.27
.

The classical analog of
\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
is
\operatorname{Perf}\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
. This is the reason why Koszul duality is classically formulated as triangulated equivalence between categories of the form
\operatorname{D}\operatorname{Mod}A
and
\operatorname{Perf}\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
, see for instance
[
28
, Theorem B]
. These classical Koszul duality functors are typically complicated for the reason that they do not take the possible higher structures on
\operatorname{H}A^{!}
into account. In the classical world, this is “solved” by restricting to Koszul algebras. Thanks to modern Koszul duality, it is possible to recover classical Koszul duality from the functor
\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}A\to\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}
. The idea is to replace
A^{!}
by its zeroth cohomology, which is an ordinary algebra. Abstractly, we aim for a functor
\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}\to\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
in order to precompose it with the Koszul duality functor
\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}A\to\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}
. We follow this route in
section 3.8
.

Another classical statement of Koszul duality is that
A^{!}
can be interpreted as Ext algebra of the simple module
\mathbb{C}=A/\bar{A}
of
A
. As we recall in
Lemma 3.28
, this is also the case if
A
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. One can alternatively use this description of
A^{!}
as defining property. With regards to notation, let
A
be an augmented
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Then we denote by
\mathbb{C}
the simple right
A
-module
\mathbb{C}=A/\bar{A}
. Its action map
\mathbb{C}\otimes T(\bar{A}[1])\to\mathbb{C}
is simply zero. In these terms,
A^{!}
is just the dg algebra
\operatorname{Hom}_{rModfdA}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})
:

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
A
be an augmented
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Then we have an isomorphism of dg algebras

A^{!}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}).

Proof.

This follows easily from
Corollary 3.21
. Indeed, the Koszul duality functor
F
sends the simple module
\mathbb{C}
to the twisted complex
(A^{!},0)\in\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}
and therefore establishes a map between the two dg endomorphism algebras. It is easy to see that
F
is fully faithful. Since the endomorphism algebra of
(A^{!},0)
is just
A^{!}
, we are done.
∎

Remark 3.29
.

Similar to
Lemma 3.28
, let
(M,\mu_{M})
be a finite-dimensional right
A
-module. Then we have an isomorphism of right
A^{!}
-modules

M\otimes A^{!}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}}(\mathbb{C},M).

The differential on
M\otimes A^{!}
is induced from induced from
\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A
and the action of
A^{!}
is by (signed) right-multiplication. The action of
A^{!}
on
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}}(\mathbb{C},M)
is by composing with
A^{!}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C})
on the right.

3.4
Calabi-Yau algebras

In this section, we recall Calabi-Yau algebras. This class of algebras is now widely recognized as a noncommutative analog of Calabi-Yau manifolds. The definition brings several technical difficulties and correspondingly a wide range of adaptations and variants have been introduced in the literature. In the present section, we follow the original definition of Ginzburg
[
19
]
, in particular we focus on the dg case.

We start by recalling opposite and enveloping algebras.

Definition 3.30
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra. Then the
opposite algebra

A^{\operatorname{op}}
of
A
is obtained by setting
A^{\operatorname{op}}=A
as vector space and defining

d_{A^{\operatorname{op}}}(a)=-d_{A}(a),\quad a\cdot_{A^{\operatorname{op}}}b=(-1)^{|a||b|}ba.

The
enveloping algebra

A^{\operatorname{e}}=A\otimes A^{\operatorname{op}}
is the tensor product of
A
and
A^{\operatorname{op}}
. It is an dg algebra itself with product
(a\otimes b)(c\otimes d)=(-1)^{|b||c|}ac\otimes bd
and differential
d(a\otimes b)=da\otimes b+(-1)^{|a|}a\otimes db
.

Definition 3.31
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra. Then an
A
-bimodule
is the same as an
A^{\operatorname{e}}
-module. The space
A
becomes naturally a bimodule over
A
by putting
(a\otimes b)x=(-1)^{|b||x|}axb
for
a\otimes b\in A^{\operatorname{op}}
and
x\in\,A
. The space
A^{\operatorname{e}}
is also an
A
-bimodule, but in two different ways. The default action is the outer action given by

(a\otimes b).(x\otimes y)=(-1)^{|b||x|}ax\otimes yb,\quad a\otimes b\in A^{\operatorname{e}},x\otimes y\in A^{\operatorname{e}}.

The alternative is the inner action given by

(a\otimes b).(x\otimes y)=(-1)^{|b||x|+|a||b|+|a||x|}xb\otimes ay,\quad a\otimes b\in A^{\operatorname{e}},x\otimes y\in A^{\operatorname{e}}.

The dg category of dg modules over a dg algebra can be defined similar to the module category over an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. If
D
is a dg algebra, we denote this dg category by
\operatorname{Mod}D
. It is not the same as the ordinary category with hom spaces the morphisms of dg modules, but rather the differential measures failure to be a dg morphism. To define
\operatorname{Mod}D
, one turns the dg algebra into an honest
A_{\infty}
by means of the sign flip
\mu^{1}(a)=(-1)^{|a|}
and
\mu^{2}(a,b)=(-1)^{|b|}ab
and then forms
\operatorname{Mod}D
according to the recipe presented in
section 3.1
. One may express the result more explicitly which we will not attempt here.

In particular, we can form the category
\operatorname{Mod}A^{\operatorname{e}}
. When
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}
, we can regard the hom space

M^{\mathrm{D}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}A^{\operatorname{e}}}(M,A^{\operatorname{e}}).

For us, it is most important that
M^{\mathrm{D}}
is again a dg
A^{\operatorname{e}}
-module, often called the dual bimodule of
M
. Its differential is the differential
\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{Mod}A^{\operatorname{e}}}
and the
A^{\operatorname{e}}
-action on
M^{\mathrm{D}}
is the inner action on the codomain
A^{\operatorname{e}}
. It is elementary to check that
M^{\mathrm{D}}
is indeed a dg module. According to Kontsevich and Soibelman, the bimodule
A^{\mathrm{D}}
is to be viewed as “inverse dualizing bibundle”
F\mapsto F\otimes K_{X}^{-1}[-\operatorname{dim}X]
of the noncommutative manifold defined by
A

[
22
, Definition 8.1.6]
.

When
M
is a dg module, the
n
-th left shift
M[n]
also becomes an object of
\operatorname{Mod}A^{\operatorname{e}}
. Reflecting the definition of Calabi-Yau manifolds in the commutative world, the dg algebra
A
is called Calabi-Yau if the dual bimodule
A^{\mathrm{D}}
is quasi-isomorphic to a shift of
A
:

Definition 3.32
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra. Then
A
is
Calabi-Yau
of dimension
n\geq 1
(CY
n
) if
A^{\mathrm{D}}[n]
and
A
are quasi-isomorphic in the category
\operatorname{Mod}A^{\operatorname{e}}
.

Remark 3.33
.

The original definition
[
19
, Definition 3.2.3]
requires that the quasi-isomorphism is a self-dual morphism. Van den Bergh has shown in
[
34
, Proposition C.1]
that this condition is typically automatic.

The definition of the hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})
takes the degrees of the chosen resolution of
A
and the degrees of
A^{\operatorname{e}}
into account. In particular, requiring
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})
and
A[n]
to be quasi-isomorphic cannot be easily translated into a property regarding resolutions on
A
. If
A
is however an ordinary algebra (concentrated in degree zero), then the definition simplifies as follows:

Lemma 3.34

(
[
19
, (3.2.5)]
)
.

Let
A
be an associative algebra which has a finite projective
A
-bimodule resolution of finitely generated bimodules. Then
A
is Calabi-Yau of dimension
n\geq 1
if and only if

\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})\cong\begin{cases}A&\text{if }k=n,\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

(3.5)

Here
\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})
is equipped again with the inner
A
-bimodule action and the isomorphism is meant as
A
-bimodules.

Proof.

By definition
\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})
is the cohomology of
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})
, together with the additional
A
-bimodule action. We now prove both directions.

If
A
is CY
n
then
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})
and
A[n]
are quasi-isomorphic as objects of
\operatorname{Mod}A^{\operatorname{e}}
. There exist closed morphisms
f
and
g
between them and a morphism
h
of degree
-1
such that
fg=\mathrm{id}+d(h)
. In particular
f
and
g
define quasi-inverse morphisms of complexes and we conclude the cohomology of
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})
is
A
, concentrated in degree
n
.

Conversely, assume (
3.5
) holds. The conclusion is a general statement regarding complexes. Let
P^{\bullet}
be a complex with homology concentrated in a single degree, namely
\varphi:A\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{H}^{k}P
. Then there is a quasi-isomorphism
\psi:A\to P^{\bullet}
of complexes given by
a\mapsto\varphi(a)\in\operatorname{H}^{k}P\subseteq P^{k}
. This map is indeed a map of chain complexes since the differential on
A
and on the image
\psi(A)
vanishes. The map is a quasi-isomorphism since on cohomology this map is just
\varphi
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.35
.

In contrast to dg algebras, two
A_{\infty}
-algebras can not be tensored easily to form a tensor
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Instead, the construction is difficult and has attracted various literature
[
27
,
29
]
. This includes an advanced construction of the tensor product
A\otimes A^{\operatorname{op}}
as
A_{\infty}
-algebra.

This way,
A\otimes A^{\operatorname{op}}
itself becomes an
A
-bimodule. This construction should not be confused with the naive bimodule action of
A
mentioned in
[
30
, Theorem 1.8]
. In this naive action,
A
acts on the two tensor components of the graded vector space
A\otimes A^{\operatorname{op}}
separately. In the present section, we avoid all difficulties by restricting to the case of
A
being a dg algebra whenever we need the algebra
A^{\operatorname{e}}
or
A\otimes A^{\operatorname{op}}
as
A
-bimodule.

In the definition of a category of modules
\operatorname{Mod}A
, one is not limited to choosing the specific resolution
T(A[1])\otimes M
of
M
. Instead one may choose an arbitrary “projective replacement”. In case
A
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra, this is a topic of research, but if
A
is a dg algebra, then the correct notion of “projective replacement” is to be K-projective:

Definition 3.36

(
[
28
, Section 4.2]
)
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra. Then a dg module
P
is
K-projective
if the dg hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}A}(P,Q)
is acyclic whenever
Q
is an acyclic dg module. A
K-projective replacement
of
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}A
is a K-projective dg module
P
together with a quasi-isomorphism
P\to M
.

Remark 3.37
.

Keller shows in
[
21
, Section 3.2]
that K-projective replacements can be found by resolving a module into a complex of so-called dg-projective modules. A module
P
is called dg-projective if it is a cofibrant object with respect to the projective model structure on the category of dg categories. Explicitly,
P
is dg-projective if for every surjective quasi-isomorphism
L\twoheadrightarrow M
, every morphism
P\to M
factors through
L
.

Remark 3.38
.

The hom spaces in
\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}A
enjoy various names in the literature, thanks to the fact that their cohomology can also be built as derived hom functor. Generally, the cohomology hom spaces
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}A}(M,N)
may be denoted
\operatorname{RHom}_{A}^{\bullet}(M,N)
or
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(M,N)
. When
M=N
, the dg hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}A}(M,M)
is actually a dg algebra itself and its minimal model becomes an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. This
A_{\infty}
-algebra is commonly denoted
\operatorname{RHom}_{A}^{\bullet}(M,M)
as well, despite the fact that classical derived hom functors do not retain homotopy information.

Remark 3.39
.

If
A
is a dg algebra, then the Hochschild cohomology
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet}(A,M)
with coefficients in an
A
-bimodule
M
is by definition equal to
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Mod}A^{\operatorname{e}}}(A,M)
.

3.5
Van den Bergh and Serre duality

In this section, we recall Van den Bergh and Serre duality for ordinary (non-dg) Calabi-Yau algebras. As it turns out, the Calabi-Yau property for ordinary algebras is closely related to having a self-dual bimodule resolution. This fact has been observed and exploited in
[
9
]
.

Definition 3.40

(
[
9
]
)
.

Let
A
be an algebra and
0\to P^{n}\to\ldots\to P^{0}\to A
be a projective
A
-bimodule resolution of
A
by finitely generated bimodules. Then
P^{\bullet}
is a
self-dual
resolution if
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(P^{\bullet},A^{\operatorname{e}})\cong P^{n-\bullet}
as complexes of
A
-bimodules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(P^{0},A^{\operatorname{e}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(P^{n},A^{\operatorname{e}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

Lemma 3.41
.

If
A
has a selfdual projective bimodule resolution of length
n\geq 1
then
A
is CY
n
.

Proof.

Let
P^{\bullet}
be a selfdual bimodule resolution. By definition, the Hochschild cohomology
\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,A^{\operatorname{e}})
is the homology in degree
k
of the complex
\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(P^{\bullet},A^{\operatorname{e}})
. By selfduality, this homology is just the homology in degree
n-k
of
P^{\bullet}
, which is
A
if
k=n
and zero otherwise. This shows that
A
is CY
n
.
∎

A bimodule resolution of
A
gives rise to resolutions for all left and right
A
-modules. The idea is to tensor the resolution on the right or left side with
M
, respectively:

Lemma 3.42

(
[
7
, Lemma 2.4]
)
.

Let
A
be an algebra and
P^{\bullet}\to A
a projective bimodule resolution. Let
M
be a left
A
-module. Then
P^{\bullet}\otimes_{A}M\to M
is a resolution for
M
.

Van den Bergh duality is a Poincaré-style theorem for Hochschild homology and cohomology of CY
n
algebras. This duality was first observed in
[
8
]
and we recall it as follows:

Theorem 3.43

(
[
8
]
)
.

Let
A
be a CY
n
algebra. If
A
has a finite projective resolution of finitely generated bimodules, then

\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,M)\cong\operatorname{HH}_{n-k}(A,M).

Proof.

We recall here the proof in the easy case where
A
has a selfdual resolution
P^{\bullet}
. We compute

\displaystyle\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,M)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{H}^{k}\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(P^{\bullet},M)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{H}^{k}\big(\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(P^{\bullet},A^{\operatorname{e}})\otimes_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}M\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{H}^{k}(P^{n-\bullet}\otimes_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}M)=\operatorname{HH}_{n-k}(A,M).

∎

As we recapitulate in
Lemma 3.44
, a CY
n
algebra has the property that the
n
-th shift is a Serre functor for its derived category. In
[
9
]
, CY
n
algebras were precisely defined by this characteristic property. It is unclear to which extent the definitions are equivalent.

Lemma 3.44

(
[
7
, Proposition 2.3]
)
.

Let
A
be an algebra with a finite projective resolution of finitely generated bimodules. If
A
is CY
n
, then for all finite-dimensional
A
-modules
M,N
there are natural isomorphisms of graded vector spaces

{\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(M,N)}^{\vee}\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{n-\bullet}(N,M).

Proof.

We recall here the proof from
[
7
]
. The first step of the proof is to realize that thanks to
Lemma 3.42
,
\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{A}(M,N)
equals the Hochschild cohomology
\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,N))
:

\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{A}(M,N)=\operatorname{H}^{k}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P^{\bullet}\otimes M,N)=\operatorname{H}^{k}\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(P^{\bullet},\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,N))=\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,N)).

Here
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,N)
is the
A
-bimodule with the left factor acting on
N
and the right factor acting on
M
:
(a\varphi b)(m)=a\varphi(bm)
.

The second step is to show
\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,{M}^{\vee})\cong{\operatorname{HH}_{k}(A,M)}^{\vee}
for finite-dimensional
A
-bimodules
M
. Here the vector space dual
{M}^{\vee}
is also an
A
-bimodule by letting the left factor act from the right on
M
and the right factor act from the left on
M
, explicitly
(a\varphi b)(m)=\varphi(bma)
.
We compute

\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,{M}^{\vee})=\operatorname{H}^{k}\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}(P^{\bullet},\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,\mathbb{C}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(P^{\bullet}\otimes_{A^{\operatorname{e}}}M,\mathbb{C})={\operatorname{HH}_{k}(A,M)}^{\vee}.

Combining the first two steps with Van den Berg duality, we conclude

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{k}(M,N)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(M,N))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{HH}^{k}(A,{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(N,M)}^{\vee})

\displaystyle\cong{\operatorname{HH}_{k}(A,\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(N,M))}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\cong{\operatorname{HH}^{n-k}(A,\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(N,M))}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\cong{\operatorname{Ext}^{n-k}_{A}(N,M)}^{\vee}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.45
.

The duality between the two Ext spaces can also be interpreted as a pairing

\langle-,-\rangle:\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(M,N)\times\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{n-\bullet}(N,M)\to\mathbb{C}.

This pairing can also be expressed by means of the traces
\operatorname{Tr}_{M}
,
\operatorname{Tr}_{N}
on
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(M,M)
and
\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(N,N)
as

\langle x,y\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}_{N}(x\circ y)=(-1)^{|x||y|}\operatorname{Tr}_{M}(y\circ x),\quad x\in\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(M,N),\quad y\in\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{n-\bullet}(N,M).

Here
\circ
denotes the composition of extensions, equivalently the product of
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Mod}A
. The trace
\operatorname{Tr}_{M}:\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{\bullet}(M,M)\to\mathbb{C}
is induced from the trace on the complex
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P^{\bullet}\otimes M,P^{\bullet}\otimes M)
which computes
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}(M,M)
. The correct signs were computed in
[
9
, Appendix]
.

3.6
Jacobi algebras

In this section, we recall characterizations of ordinary (non-dg) CY3 algebras. The core idea is that most CY3 algebras are the Jacobi algebra of a quiver with superpotential. Conversely, most quivers with superpotential gives rise to a CY3 algebra. In the present section, we recall the precise criteria from
[
9
]
. In particular, we recall superpotentials and their associated Jacobi algebras as well as a candidate bimodule resolution for Jacobi algebras. This section serves as a direct preparation for
section 6
.

We start by fixing terminology for cyclic elements of quiver algebras as follows:

Definition 3.46
.

Let
Q
be a quiver. A path in
Q
is a cycle if it starts and ends at the same vertex. If
p
is a cycle in
Q
, we denote by
p_{\operatorname{cyc}}\in\mathbb{C}Q
the sum of its cyclic permutations. We extend this assignment linearly to
\mathbb{C}Q
and denote it by
p\mapsto p_{\operatorname{cyc}}
as well. An element
W\in\mathbb{C}Q
is
cyclic
if it lies in the image of this map. Explicitly,
W
is cyclic if it is a linear combination of cycles whose coefficients are invariant under cyclic permutation:

W=\sum_{\text{cycles }a_{k}\ldots a_{1}}\lambda_{a_{k}\ldots a_{1}}a_{k}\ldots a_{1},\quad\text{with}\quad\forall i=1,\ldots,k:~~\lambda_{a_{k}\ldots a_{1}}=\lambda_{a_{i-1}\ldots a_{i+1}a_{i}}.

A superpotential on a quiver
Q
is defined as a cyclic element
W\in\mathbb{C}Q
which is a linear combination of paths of length at least two:

Definition 3.47
.

A
superpotential
is a cyclic element
W\in\mathbb{C}Q_{\geq 2}
. Its
relations
are the elements

\partial_{a}W=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{paths }a_{k}\ldots a_{1}\\
\text{with }a_{k}=a\end{subarray}}\lambda_{a_{k}\ldots a_{1}}a_{k-1}\ldots a_{1},\quad a\in Q_{1}.

Its
Jacobi algebra
is given by

\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)=\frac{\mathbb{C}Q}{(\partial_{a}W)}.

Here
(\partial_{a}W)
denotes the two-sided ideal generated by the partial derivatives
\partial_{a}W
for
a\in Q_{1}
.

Remark 3.48
.

A typical assumption in the literature is that the paths are of length at least three. In fact, length two term gives rise to a single arrow being contained in one relation
\partial_{a}W
. The effect is that this arrow is killed in the Jacobi algebra.

The original paper of Ginzburg
[
9
]
formulated the expectation that all CY3 algebras “appearing in nature” are Jacobi algebras of quivers with superpotential. This expectation was largely verified in
[
9
]
, with the core result that a quiver algebra with graded relations which is CY3 is necessarily of the form
\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)
:

Theorem 3.49

(
[
9
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Let
Q
be a quiver and let
A=\mathbb{C}Q/I
be the quotient by a finitely generated graded ideal
I\subseteq\mathbb{C}Q_{\geq 2}
. If
A
is CY3, then there exists a superpotential
W
such that
\mathbb{C}Q/I\cong\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)
.

The idea of the proof is to explore the structure of
I
in terms of resolutions for the simple modules of
A
. These first bits of these resolutions are standard and do not depend on the ideal
I
. In contrast, the last bits depend on
I
but can be guessed by consideration on the dimension of the Ext spaces based on the assumption that
A
is CY3.

Conversely, not every Jacobi algebra is CY3. There is however a precise criterion due to
[
9
]
as well. The criterion is formulated in terms of a “candidate” bimodule resolution for
A
. We start with the following notation:

Definition 3.50
.

Let
W\in\mathbb{C}Q_{\geq 2}
be a superpotential. Then the
\mathbb{C}Q_{0}
-bimodule generated by
W
in
\mathbb{C}Q
is denoted

W=\mathbb{C}Q_{0}WQ_{0}=\bigoplus_{v\in Q_{0}}\mathbb{C}vWv\subseteq\mathbb{C}Q.

The relations space is denoted

R=\operatorname{span}\{\partial_{a}W~|~a\in Q_{1}\}.

To introduce the candidate bimodule resolution, let
Q
be a quiver,
W\in\mathbb{C}Q_{\geq 3}
a superpotential. Let us temporarily write
A=\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)
for the Jacobi algebra. The candidate bimodule resolution has the shape

0\to A\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{C}Q_{0}}}W\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{C}Q_{0}}}A\overset{g_{1}}{\to}A\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{C}Q_{0}}}R\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{C}Q_{0}}}A\overset{g_{2}}{\to}A\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{C}Q_{0}}}\mathbb{C}Q_{1}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{C}Q_{0}}}A\overset{g_{3}}{\to}A\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{C}Q_{0}}}A\to A\to 0.

(3.6)

Remark 3.51
.

The maps in the sequence (
6.1
) are described as follows:

•

For the map
g_{1}
, let
w=\sum_{i\in I}r_{i}a_{i}=\sum_{j\in J}b_{i}r_{j}\in W
, where all
a_{i}
an
b_{i}
are arrows and the
r_{i},r_{j}\in R
are compatible elements. The map
g_{1}
sends the element
1\otimes w\otimes 1
to

g_{1}(1\otimes w\otimes 1)=\sum_{i\in I}1\otimes r_{i}\otimes a_{i}-\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}\otimes r_{j}\otimes 1.

•

For the map
g_{2}
, let
r\in R
and for
d\geq 0
write
r=\sum_{i\in I_{d}}p_{i}^{(d)}a_{i}^{(d)}q_{i}^{(d)}\in R
, where
p_{i}^{(d)}
are paths of length
d
and
a_{i}^{(d)}
are arrows. The map
g_{2}
sends the element
1\otimes r
to

g_{2}(1\otimes r\otimes 1)=\sum_{d\geq 0}\sum_{i\in I_{d}}p_{i}^{(d)}\otimes a_{i}^{(d)}\otimes q_{i}^{(d)}.

•

The map
g_{3}
is given by
1\otimes a\otimes 1\mapsto a\otimes 1-1\otimes a
.

•

The fourth map is simply the contraction map.

The sequence (
6.1
) is clearly a chain complex. Whether or not it is exact is an indicator on whether
A
is CY3 or not:

Theorem 3.52

(
[
9
, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

Let
Q
be a quiver and
W\in\mathbb{C}Q_{\geq 3}
a superpotential. Then the algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)
is CY3 if and only if the sequence (
3.6
) is a bimodule resolution for
A
.

3.7
Cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras

In this section, we recall the correspondence of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras and Calabi-Yau dg algebras via Koszul duality. In
Lemma 3.44
and
3.28
, we have already seen that
A^{!}
is the endomorphism algebra of the simple module
\mathbb{C}
and that modules over a CY
n
algebra enjoy Serre duality of degree
n
. This is a strong indication that
\operatorname{H}A^{!}
has a cyclic structure of degree
n
. As we recall in this section, this is the norm and cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras correspond to Calabi-Yau algebras under Koszul duality:

A
Cyclic
A^{!}
Calabi-Yau
Koszul

In this section, we start by recalling the notion of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras. Then we explain that their Koszul duals are so-called deformed dg-preprojective algebras, which are Calabi-Yau. This explains the correspondence of cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras and Calabi-Yau dg algebras via Koszul duality. Our primary reference is
[
34
]
. We start by recalling cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras, a generalizations of Frobenius algebras:

Definition 3.53
.

Let
A
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Then
A
is
cyclic
of degree
n
if it is equipped with a bilinear nondegenerate pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:A\times A\to\mathbb{C}
of degree
-n
such that
\langle x,y\rangle=(-1)^{|x||y|}\langle y,x\rangle
and

\langle\mu^{k}(a_{k+1},\ldots,a_{2}),a_{1}\rangle=(-1)^{\|a_{k+1}\|(\|a_{k}\|+\ldots+\|a_{1}\|)}\langle\mu^{k}(a_{k},\ldots,a_{1}),a_{k+1}\rangle.

Remark 3.54
.

The pairing is a noncommutative and categorical manifestation of a symplectic form. This is one of the reasons that cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras are dominant in the theory of Fukaya categories. In our context, the notion of cyclic Fukaya categories is reserved for the A-side of mirror symmetry, while Calabi-Yau algebras are reserved for the B-side of mirror symmetry.

When discussing Koszul duality for cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras, we frequently need to choose a basis compatible with the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
. We recall this piece of linear algebra as follows:

Lemma 3.55
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional graded vector space over
\mathbb{C}
and
\langle-,-\rangle:V\times V\to\mathbb{C}
a nondegenerate bilinear form of degree
-n
with
\langle x,y\rangle=(-1)^{|x||y|}\langle y,x\rangle
. Then there is a graded basis for
V
in which
\langle-,-\rangle
takes the form

\displaystyle\langle-,-\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\begin{matrix}&I_{k}\\
\pm I_{k}&\end{matrix}&&\\
\hline\cr&I_{l}&\\
\hline\cr&&\begin{matrix}&-I_{m}\\
I_{m}&\end{matrix}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\pm I_{k}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{diag}((-1)^{(n+1)|x_{1}|},\ldots,(-1)^{(n+1)|x_{k}|}).

Here
k=\operatorname{dim}V_{<n/2}
and the first
k
basis elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
can be freely chosen. The matrix
I_{l}
appears only if
n
is even and
n/2
is even. The symplectic part consisting of
I_{m}
and
-I_{m}
only appears if
n
is even and
n/2
is odd. In these cases we have
l=\operatorname{dim}V_{n/2}
and
m=\operatorname{dim}V_{n/2}/2
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
be a given graded basis for
V_{<n/2}
. The restricted pairing
\langle-,-\rangle:V_{<n/2}\times V_{>n/2}\to\mathbb{C}
gives an isomorphism
\varphi:V_{>n/2}\xrightarrow{\sim}{V}^{\vee}_{<n/2}
and the basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
gives rise to a dual basis
{x}^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,{x}^{\vee}_{i}\in{V}^{\vee}_{<n/2}
. Pick
x_{j}^{*}\in V_{>n/2}
such that
\varphi(x_{j}^{*})={x}^{\vee}_{j}
. Then

\langle x_{i},x_{j}^{*}\rangle=\varphi(x_{j}^{*})(x_{i})={x}^{\vee}_{j}(x_{i})=\delta_{ij},\quad\langle x_{j}^{*},x_{i}\rangle=(-1)^{(n-|x_{j}|)|x_{i}|}\delta_{ij}=(-1)^{(n+1)|x_{i}|}\delta_{ij}.

Picking
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*}
as first part of the basis sets up the first diagonal block of the matrix. If
n
is odd, then
V=V_{<n/2}\oplus V_{>n/2}
and we are done. If
n
is even and
n/2
is even, then
\langle-,-\rangle
is a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form on
V_{n/2}
and we can find a basis in which
\langle-,-\rangle:V_{n/2}\times V_{n/2}\to\mathbb{C}
is the identity matrix. If
n
is even and
n/2
is odd, then
\langle-,-\rangle:V_{n/2}\times V_{n/2}\to\mathbb{C}
is a symplectic form on
V_{n/2}
and we can choose a symplectic basis. This finishes the proof.
∎

Definition 3.56
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional graded vector space over
\mathbb{C}
and
\langle-,-\rangle:V\times V\to\mathbb{C}
a nondegenerate bilinear form of degree
-n
. Choose a basis for
V
in which
\langle-,-\rangle
takes the shape (
3.55
):

\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*}

\displaystyle\text{if }n\text{ odd},

\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{k+l}

\displaystyle\text{if }n\text{ even},~n/2\text{ even},

\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{k+m},x_{k+1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k+m}^{*}

\displaystyle\text{if }n\text{ even},~n/2\text{ odd.}

Then we call the pair of sequences

\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*}

\displaystyle\text{if }n\text{ odd},

\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{k+l}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*},x_{k+1}^{*}\coloneqq x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{k+l}^{*}\coloneqq x_{k+l}

\displaystyle\text{if }n\text{ even},~n/2\text{ even},

\displaystyle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{k+m}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k+m}^{*}

\displaystyle\text{if }n\text{ even},~n/2\text{ odd}

a
basis with dual basis
for
V
via
\langle-,-\rangle
.

Remark 3.57
.

For any
i
we have
|x_{i}^{*}|=n-|x_{i}|
, regardless of whether
n
is odd or even.

Van den Bergh investigated the correspondence between cyclic
A_{\infty}
-algebras and Calabi-Yau dg algebras via Koszul duality in
[
34
]
. The technical core result is a structure characterization of Calabi-Yau dg algebras. Roughly speaking, a dg algebra is CY
n
if it is weakly equivalent to a so-called deformed dg-preprojective algebra
\Pi(Q,n,W)
for some quiver
Q
and superpotential
W\in\mathbb{C}Q_{\geq 3}
. Here
W
is required to satisfy
\{W,W\}=0
with respect to the so-called necklace bracket
\{-,-\}
, on which we will not elaborate here. We recall the construction of the algebras
\Pi(Q,n,W)
as follows:

Definition 3.58
.

Let
Q
be a graded quiver,
n\geq 3
an integer and
W\in\mathbb{C}Q_{\geq 3}
a superpotential homogeneous of degree
-n+3
with respect to the grading on
Q
. Assume that
W
is “graded cyclic”, instead of cyclic. Assume
\{W,W\}=0
with respect to the necklace bracket. Let
\tilde{Q}
be the double quiver, obtained from
Q
by inserting for every arrow
a
an arrow
a^{*}
in opposite direction. The arrow
a^{*}
is assigned degree
-n+2-|a|_{Q}
. In the special case where an arrow
a
is a loop with odd degree
|a|_{Q}=(2-n)/2
, no arrow
a^{*}
is adjoined and one puts
a^{*}=a
. Let
\bar{Q}
be the quiver obtained from
\tilde{Q}
by additionally inserting on every vertex a loop
z_{i}
of degree
1-n
. Then the
deformed dg-preprojective algebra

\Pi(Q,n,W)=(\widehat{\mathbb{C}\tilde{Q}},d_{\Pi})

is the dg algebra modeled on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}\tilde{Q}}
with the following differential:

\displaystyle d_{\Pi}(a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(|a|+1)|a^{*}|}\partial_{a^{*}}W,

\displaystyle d_{\Pi}(a^{*})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|a|+1}\partial_{a}W,

\displaystyle d_{\Pi}(z_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{h(a)=i}[a,a^{*}].

We recall Van den Bergh’s structure result as follows:

Theorem 3.59

(
[
34
, Theorem 10.2.2]
)
.

Let
Q
be a graded quiver,
n\geq 3
an integer and
W\in\mathbb{C}\tilde{Q}_{\geq 3}
a superpotential with
\{W,W\}=0
. Assume
W
is homogeneous of degree
-n+3
with respect to the grading on
Q
and all arrows in
Q
lie in degree range
[-n+2,0]
. Then
\Pi(Q,n,W)
is CY
n
.

Van den Bergh also shows the converse statement that every CY
n
dg algebra under suitable finiteness conditions is equivalent to some deformed dg-preprojective algebra
\Pi(Q,n,W)
. It is an easy consequence of
Theorem 3.59
that the Koszul dual of a minimal cyclic
A_{\infty}
-category is CY
n
:

Corollary 3.60
.

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional augmented minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra concentrated in non-negative degrees with
A^{0}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}
. Assume
A
is cyclic of degree
n
. Then
A^{!}
is a deformed dg-preprojective algebra and is therefore CY
n
.

Proof.

The idea is to represent
A^{!}
as a deformed dg-preprojective algebra
\Pi(Q,n,W)
by letting the arrows of
Q
stand for basis elements of
A
and letting the superpotential
W
record the product
\mu
. We ignore signs.

The first step is to choose a basis for
A
by means of
Lemma 3.55
. Since
A^{0}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}
, we can include the element
\mathrm{id}
in the the basis and obtain a basis with dual basis of the following form:

A=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}\oplus\operatorname{span}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}\oplus\operatorname{span}\{x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*}\}\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}^{*}.

Of course, we have to make adaptations in case
n
is even: There may be self-dual basis elements of degree
n/2
. This is not a problem and is adequately reflected in the definition of
\Pi(Q,n,W)
by the intricate extra condition on loops. We shall for simplicity proceed with the assumption that
n
is odd.

We define the quiver
Q
to be a single vertex with
k
arrows, such that the double quiver
\bar{Q}
and the extended quiver
\tilde{Q}
satisfy

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{C}\tilde{Q}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\langle\!\langle{x}^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,{x}^{\vee}_{k},{(x_{1}^{*})}^{\vee},\ldots,{(x_{k}^{*})}^{\vee}\rangle\!\rangle,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{C}\bar{Q}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\langle\!\langle{x}^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,{x}^{\vee}_{k},{(x_{1}^{*})}^{\vee},\ldots,{(x_{k}^{*})}^{\vee},{(\mathrm{id}^{*})}^{\vee}\rangle\!\rangle.

In these graded algebras, the variables
{x}^{\vee}_{i}
and
{(x_{i}^{*})}^{\vee}
have degrees
1-|x_{i}|
and
1-|x_{i}^{*}|
, respectively. The variable
{(\mathrm{id}^{*})}^{\vee}
has degree
1-n
.

We define the superpotential
W\in\widehat{\mathbb{C}\tilde{Q}}
by Koszul transforming the
A_{\infty}
-structure on
\mu
:

W=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\leq k\\
2^{l+1}\text{ star options}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\langle\mu(x_{i_{l}}^{[*]},\ldots,x_{i_{1}}^{[*]}),x_{i_{0}}^{[*]}\rangle}_{\in\mathbb{C}}\cdot\underbrace{{(x_{i_{l}}^{[*]})}^{\vee}\ldots{(x_{i_{1}}^{[*]})}^{\vee}{(x_{i_{0}}^{[*]})}^{\vee}}_{\in\mathbb{C}\tilde{Q}}.

Here we sum over choices of indices
i_{0},\ldots,i_{l}
as well as choices of starred or non-starred basis elements and variables. Minimality of
A
ensures that
W
lies in
\mathbb{C}Q_{\geq 3}
and cyclicity of
A
ensures that
W
is graded cyclic. The
A_{\infty}
-relations for
A
ensure that
\{W,W\}=0
.

The quiver
Q
, the cyclicity degree
n
and the superpotential
W
now yield a deformed dg-projective algebra
\Pi(Q,n,W)
. Explicitly, its differential reads

\displaystyle d_{\Pi}{x}^{\vee}_{i}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(|{x}^{\vee}_{i}|+1)|{(x_{i}^{*})}^{\vee}}\partial_{{(x_{i}^{*})}^{\vee}}W,

\displaystyle d_{\Pi}{(x_{i}^{*})}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|{x}^{\vee}_{i}|+1}\partial_{{x}^{\vee}_{i}}W,

\displaystyle d_{\Pi}{(\mathrm{id}^{*})}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{x}^{\vee}_{i}{(x_{i}^{*})}^{\vee}-{(x_{i}^{*})}^{\vee}{x}^{\vee}_{i}.

In the remainder of the proof, we check that
\Pi(Q,n,W)
equals
A^{!}
. In fact, we have already named all the variables
2k+1
variables in
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q}
precisely the way in which variables for
A^{!}
are named.

To see that also the differentials
d_{\Pi}
and
d_{A^{!}}
agree, note that the derivative
\partial_{{x_{i}}^{\vee}}W
precisely records the
x_{i}^{*}
coefficient of all possible products
\mu(\ldots)
of which the input is not the identity
\mathrm{id}
or the co-identity
\mathrm{id}^{*}
. In principle, the differential of
A^{!}
also records those products
\mu(\ldots)
which contain a co-identity, however those products vanish:

|\mu^{l}(x_{i_{l}}^{[*]},\ldots,\mathrm{id}^{*},\ldots,x_{i_{1}}^{[*]})|\geq(2-l)+(l-1)+n=n+1.

Here we have used the assumption that
A
is concentrated in non-negative degrees and the co-idenity is in degree
n
. We draw the conclusion that such products vanish because
A
is limited to degrees at most
n
. Ultimately,
d{(x_{i}^{*})}^{\vee}=\partial_{{x}^{\vee}_{i}}
agrees with the differential of
{(x_{i}^{*})}^{\vee}
in
A^{!}
.

Also on the variable
{(\mathrm{id}^{*})}^{\vee}
, the differentials of
\Pi(Q,n,W)
and
A^{!}
agree. Indeed, the differential of
A^{!}
records appearances of
\mathrm{id}^{*}
as result of products
\mu(\ldots)
. Thanks to cyclicity, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mu^{l\geq 3}(a_{l},\ldots,a_{1}),\mathrm{id}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\mu^{2}(a,b),\mathrm{id}\rangle+\langle\mu^{2}(b,\mathrm{id}),a\rangle+\langle\mu^{2}(\mathrm{id},a),b\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

When plugging in
a=x_{i}
and
b=x_{j}^{*}
, we precisely obtain
d_{A^{!}}({(\mathrm{id}^{*})}^{\vee})=d_{\Pi}({(\mathrm{id}^{*})}^{\vee})
. Ultimately, we conclude that
A^{!}=\Pi(Q,n,W)
. Thanks to
Theorem 3.59
, the algebra
\Pi(Q,n,W)
is CY
n
and therefore
A^{!}
is CY
n
itself. This finishes the proof.
∎

3.8
Cho-Hong-Lau roadmap

In this section, we motivate the Cho-Hong-Lau construction from the perspective of Koszul duality. The idea is to pass the dg algebra
A^{!}
to cohomology. Regarded as ordinary algebra, the cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
need not be a Calabi-Yau algebra itself. Under grading assumptions on
A
, it is however a Jacobi algebra and therefore a candidate to be CY3. We explain how to drop the grading requirements so that one can start from
A
being
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded. The Koszul duality functor only survives this drop of requirements when we replace the actual cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
by a surrogate. At the end of the section, we provide this tweaked Koszul duality functor which comes close to the Cho-Hong-Lau construction.

Our first starting point is an
A_{\infty}
-category
A
which is cyclic of degree
n
, concentrated in non-negative degree and has degree zero part
A^{0}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}
:

A=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}\oplus A^{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus A^{n-1}\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}^{*}.

Our interest lies in the Koszul duality functor
\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A\to\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}
which we recalled in
section 3.2
. The essential step leading to the Cho-Hong-Lau construction consists of replacing the dg algebra
A^{!}
by its zeroth cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
, which is an ordinary associative algebra. In this section, we provide a simple explanation why this works particularly well if
A
is cyclic of degree 3.

We start with the observation that passing to the minimal model is unproblematic if
A
is concentrated in non-negative degree. We denote the minimal model of the dg algebra
A^{!}
by
\operatorname{H}A^{!}
. Its degree zero part is
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
.

Lemma 3.61
.

Let
A
be a non-negatively graded augmented
A_{\infty}
-algebra such that
A^{0}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}
. Then there is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism
A^{!}\to\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
.

Proof.

By definition of the minimal model
\operatorname{H}A^{!}
, there is an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism
A^{!}\to\operatorname{H}A^{!}
. In the remainder of the proof, we show that the projection
\pi_{0}:\operatorname{H}A^{!}\to\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism. Regard homogeneous elements
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\operatorname{H}A^{!}
. Writing out the definition of
\mu_{\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}}(\pi_{0}a_{k},\ldots,\pi_{0}a_{1})
, it is our task to show

\pi_{0}\mu_{\operatorname{H}A^{!}}(a_{k},\ldots,a_{1})=\begin{cases}\mu_{\operatorname{H}A^{!}}(\pi_{0}a_{2},\pi_{0}a_{1})&\text{if }k=2\\
0&\text{if }k\neq 2.\end{cases}

(3.10)

The basic observation is that
A^{!}
is concentrated in non-positive degrees because
\bar{A}
is concentrated in positive degrees. The cohomology
\operatorname{H}A^{!}
is then also concentrated in non-positive degrees. To check (
3.10
), we distinguish four cases according to the value of
k
and the degrees of the input elements
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
.

If
k=1
, both sides vanish. If
k=2
and
a_{1},a_{2}
are both of degree zero and (
3.10
) holds. If
k=2
and one of
a_{1},a_{2}
is of negative degree, then
\mu_{\operatorname{H}A^{!}}(a_{2},a_{1})
is of negative degree and both sides of (
3.10
) vanish. If
k\geq 3
, then
\mu_{\operatorname{H}A^{!}}(a_{k},\ldots,a_{1})
has degree at most
2-k\leq-1
and therefore both sides vanish as well. This finishes the proof.
∎

We borrow the following notation from
Definition 6.32
.

Definition 3.62
.

Let
V
be a vector space and
R\subseteq V
be a subspace. Then
I(R)_{\widehat{T(V)}}
is the ideal of
\widehat{T(V)}
given by the image of the map
\widehat{T(V)}\widehat{\otimes}R\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{T(V)}\to\widehat{T(V)}
. Explicitly,
I(R)_{\widehat{T(V)}}
consists of all elements that can be written as a series

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}p_{i}r_{i}q_{i},\quad p_{i}\in V^{\otimes\to\infty},\quad r_{i}\in R,\quad q_{i}\in V^{\otimes\to\infty}.

Lemma 3.63
.

Let
A
be an augmented
A_{\infty}
-algebra concentrated in non-negative degrees with
A^{0}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}
. Then we have an algebra identification

\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}=\frac{\widehat{T({(A^{1})}^{\vee})}}{I(\{d_{A^{!}}x~|~x\in{(A^{2})}^{\vee}\})_{\widehat{T({(A^{1})}^{\vee})}}}.

Proof.

Since
\bar{A}
is concentrated in positive degrees, its left-shift
\bar{A}[1]
is concentrated in non-negative degrees. We can write

A^{!}=\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(\underbrace{{(A^{1})}^{\vee}}_{\text{deg }0}\oplus\underbrace{{(A^{2})}^{\vee}}_{\text{deg }-1}\oplus\ldots)^{\otimes i}

In the remainder of the proof, we determine the degree zero cocycles and degree zero coboundaries of
A^{!}
.

For the degree zero cocycles, we note that the degree zero part of
A^{!}
is
{(A^{1})}^{\vee}
and the degree one part of
A^{!}
is zero. Together, the degree zero cocyles of
A^{!}
are just
{T(A^{1})}^{\vee}
.

For the degree zero coboundaries, we determine first the degree
-1
part of
A^{!}
. In fact, the part of degree
-1
consists of products of degree zero elements with precisely one degree
-1
element, more precisely

\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{i}{(A^{1})}^{\vee}{}^{\otimes j}\otimes{(A^{2})}^{\vee}\otimes{(A^{1})}^{\vee}{}^{\otimes i-j}.

Now the degree zero coboundaries of
A^{!}
are precisely the differentials of elements in this space. Since
d_{A^{!}}x=0
for
x\in{(A^{1})}^{\vee}
, we immediately see that for
x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}\in{(A^{1})}^{\vee}
and
y\in{(A^{2})}^{\vee}
we have

d_{A^{!}}(x_{i}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{i-j+1}\otimes y\otimes x_{i-j}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{1})=x_{i}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{i-j+1}\otimes d_{A^{!}}y\otimes x_{i-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes x_{1}.

We conclude that the degree zero coboundaries of
A^{!}
are precisely elements of
I(\{d_{A^{!}}y~|~y\in{(A^{2})}^{\vee}\})
. This finishes the proof.
∎

We shall continue writing
I
for the ideal defined in
Lemma 3.63
:

Definition 3.64
.

Let
A
be an augmented
\mathbb{Z}
-graded finite-dimensional
A_{\infty}
-algebra cyclic of degree
n\geq 3
. Denote by
A^{1}
and
A^{2}
the homogeneous subspaces of degree
1
and
2
, respectively. Then we denote by
I
the ideal

I=I(\{d_{A^{!}}x~|~x\in{(A^{2})}^{\vee}\})_{\widehat{T({(A^{1})}^{\vee}}}\subseteq\widehat{T({(A^{1})}^{\vee})}.

It is in fact possible to say more about the ideal in case
A
is cyclic of degree
3
:

Lemma 3.65
.

Let
A
be an augmented finite-dimensional
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Assume
A
is cyclic of degree
3
and concentrated in non-negative degrees with
A^{0}=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}
:

A=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}\oplus A^{1}\oplus A^{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}^{*}.

Then we have a Koszul duality functor
\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A\to\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
. Upon choosing a basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
for
A^{1}
, the algebra
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
can be written as a Jacobi-type algebra and is naturally a candidate to be CY3:

\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}=\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\!\langle{x}^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,{x}^{\vee}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle}{I(\partial_{{x}^{\vee}_{i}}W)_{\mathbb{C}\langle\!\langle{x}^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,{x}^{\vee}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle}}.

(3.11)

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps. In the first step, we explain the Koszul duality functor. In the second step, we examine the algebra
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
. In the third step, we comment on CY3-ness.

For the first step, pick the Koszul duality functor
\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A\to\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}
from
Corollary 3.21
. Thanks to
Lemma 3.61
, we have an additional morphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras
A^{!}\to\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
, which induces an
A_{\infty}
-functor
\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}\to\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
. Composing these two, we obtain the desired Koszul duality functor

\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A\to\operatorname{Tw}A^{!}\to\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}.

For the second step of the proof, the starting point is the description
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}=\widehat{T({(A^{1})}^{\vee})}/I
from
Lemma 3.63
. It is our task to examine the ideal
I
. Choose a basis
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
for
A^{1}
and denote by
x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{n}^{*}
the corresponding dual basis for
A^{2}
via
\langle-,-\rangle
. We construct the superpotential
W\in\widehat{T({(A^{1})}^{\vee})}
as follows:

W=\sum_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k},i_{0}\leq n}\langle\mu(x_{i_{k}},\ldots,x_{i_{1}}),x_{i_{0}}\rangle{x}^{\vee}_{i_{0}}{x}^{\vee}_{i_{1}}\ldots{x}^{\vee}_{i_{k}}.

This specific pairing has the chance of not vanishing only because
n=3
. In fact, the degree of
\mu(\ldots)
is
2
while the degree of
x_{i_{0}}
is
1
. The superpotential
W
is cyclic since
\mu
is assumed to be cyclic. We now claim that
\partial_{{x}^{\vee}_{i_{0}}}W=d_{A^{!}}{(x_{i_{0}}^{*})}^{\vee}
. Indeed,

\partial_{{x}^{\vee}_{i_{0}}}W=\sum_{1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\leq n}\langle\mu(x_{i_{k}},\ldots,x_{i_{1}}),x_{i_{0}}\rangle{x}^{\vee}_{i_{1}}\ldots{x}^{\vee}_{i_{k}}=d_{A^{!}}{(x_{i_{0}}^{*})}^{\vee}.

This proves the desired description of
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
. For the third part of the proof, we comment on the non-technical statement regarding the claimed CY3 candidate status of
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
. As we have shown in the second part of the proof, the algebra
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
is the quotient of a noncommutative power series ring by derivatives of a superpotential. This does technically not imply that
\operatorname{H}^{0}A^{!}
is CY3. However, it is known that if the number of variables and the degree of the superpotential are high enough, then the typical superpotential does turn the quotient into a CY3 algebra
[
9
, Corollary 4.4]
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.66
.

It is essential that the cyclicity degree of
A
is
3
: Assume
A
is cyclic of degree
n
instead. In order to represent
d{x}^{\vee}
as derivative of a superpotential
W\in\widehat{T({(A^{1})}^{\vee})}
, the only natural way is by
d{x}^{\vee}
being either the derivative
\partial_{{x}^{\vee}}W
or
\partial_{{(x^{*})}^{\vee}}W
. However, the variable
{x}^{\vee}
has degree
-1
and the variable
{(x^{*})}^{\vee}
has degree
1-(n-2)=3-n
. We conclude that only for
n=3
any of the variables, namely
{(x^{*})}^{\vee}
, has a chance of appearing in
W
. This shows that cyclicity in degree
n=3
is favorable for passing
A^{!}
to cohomology.

The reduction of the Koszul dual to zeroth cohomology in
Lemma 3.65
makes room for further weakening of the assumptions on the side of the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A
. As we show in
Lemma 3.68
, we can drop the requirement that
A
be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded and concentrated in non-negative degrees. The idea is to circumvent
A^{!}
and work directly with the quotient algebra displayed on the right-hand side of (
3.11
). When we restrict
\mu_{A}
or
\mu_{M}
to
T(\bar{A}^{1})
, we shall use the letter
m
instead of
\mu
:

Definition 3.67
.

Let
A=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}\oplus\bar{A}^{1}\oplus\bar{A}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}^{*}
be an augmented
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded finite-dimensional
A_{\infty}
-algebra cyclic of odd degree. We denote the restriction to
T(A^{1})
of the map
\mu_{A}:M\otimes T(\bar{A})\to M\otimes T(\bar{A})
and its partial dual
{\mu}^{\vee}_{A}
by

\displaystyle m_{A}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle M\otimes T(\bar{A}^{1})

\displaystyle\to M\otimes T(\bar{A}^{1}),

\displaystyle{m}^{\vee}_{A,0}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\to M\otimes{T(\bar{A}^{1})}^{\vee}.

Let
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A
be a finite-dimensional right
A
-module with action map
\mu_{M}:M\otimes T(A[1])\to M\otimes T(A[1])
. Then we denote the restriction to
T(A^{1})
of
\mu_{M}
and
{\mu}^{\vee}_{M}
by

\displaystyle m_{M}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle M\otimes T(\bar{A}^{1})

\displaystyle\to M\otimes T(\bar{A}^{1}),

\displaystyle{m}^{\vee}_{M,0}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\to M\otimes{T(\bar{A}^{1})}^{\vee}.

We now formulate the Koszul duality where
A
is not required to be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded. From the standpoint of Koszul duality, this statement is the closest to the Cho-Hong-Lau construction that lies within the framework of finite-dimensional modules and twisted complexes:

Lemma 3.68
.

Let
A=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}\oplus\bar{A}^{1}\oplus\bar{A}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}^{*}
be an augmented
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded finite-dimensional
A_{\infty}
-algebra cyclic of odd degree. Write
J=\widehat{T({(A^{1})}^{\vee})}/I
. Then we have a Koszul duality functor

\displaystyle F:\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}\operatorname{Tw}J,

\displaystyle(M,\mu_{M})

\displaystyle\xmapsto{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}(M\otimes J,{m}^{\vee}_{M,0}),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\xmapsto{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}{f}^{\vee}.

Proof.

We only provide a glimpse of the proof here, since the more general version is treated in
section 7
. For instance, it is instructive to explain why
(M,{m}^{\vee}_{M,0})
is a twisted complex over
J
.

In order to recognize
(M,{m}^{\vee}_{M,0})
as twisted complex over
J
, we have to check its Maurer-Cartan identity. Since
M
is a module over
A
, we have
(m_{M}\circ m_{A})_{0}+(m_{M}\circ m_{M})_{0}=0
. The image of
{(m_{M}\circ m_{A})_{0}}^{\vee}
lies in
M\otimes I
. This means that modulo
M\otimes I
, we have

\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}J}({m}^{\vee}_{M,0},{m}^{\vee}_{M,0})={(m_{M}\circ m_{M})_{0}}^{\vee}=0.

This shows that
{m}^{\vee}_{M,0}
satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation in
\operatorname{Add}J
and
(M\otimes J,{m}^{\vee}_{M,0})
is indeed a twisted complex.
∎

Remark 3.69
.

The functor
F
in
Lemma 3.68
can be made explicit upon choice of basis for
A
. Choose a basis of a consisting of elements
x_{i}
in odd degree and the dual elements
x_{i}^{*}
in even degree:

A=\underbrace{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}}_{\text{even}}\oplus\underbrace{\operatorname{span}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}}_{\text{odd}}\oplus\underbrace{\operatorname{span}\{x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{k}^{*}\}}_{\text{even}}\oplus\underbrace{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{id}^{*}}_{\text{odd}}.

The algebra
J
takes on the form

J=\frac{\mathbb{C}\langle\!\langle{x}^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,{x}^{\vee}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle}{I(\partial_{{x}^{\vee}_{i}}W)_{\mathbb{C}\langle\!\langle{x}^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,{x}^{\vee}_{k}\rangle\!\rangle}}.

Formulated in terms of the basis, the fact that
{m}^{\vee}_{M}
squares to zero is based on the observation that

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\geq 0\\
1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\leq k\end{subarray}}(\mu\cdot\mu)(m,x_{i_{l}},\ldots,x_{i_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\geq 0\\
1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\leq k\\
0\leq s\leq r\leq l\end{subarray}}\mu(m,x_{i_{l}},\ldots,\mu(x_{i_{r}},\ldots,x_{i_{s+1}}),\ldots)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\geq 0\\
1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\leq k\\
0\leq r\leq l\end{subarray}}\mu(\mu(m,x_{i_{l}},\ldots,x_{i_{r+1}}),\ldots,x_{1}).

Note that there are no signs since all
x_{i}
are odd. When dualizing the above equality, the first summand on the second row lands in the ideal
I(\partial_{{x}^{\vee}_{i}}W)_{\mathbb{C}\langle\!\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\rangle\!\rangle}
and vanishes in
J
. Meanwhile, the second summand on the second row dualizes to
({m}^{\vee}_{M,0})^{2}
. We conclude that
({m}^{\vee}_{M,0})^{2}=0
, in other words
(M\otimes J,{m}^{\vee}_{M})
is a twisted complex.

The construction of
Lemma 3.68
leads directly to the Cho-Hong-Lau construction. The idea is to extend the construction of the Koszul duality functor
\operatorname{Mod}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{\mathrm{right}}A\to\operatorname{Tw}J
to the case of non-augmented algebras. On the side of the Koszul dual, the required adaption consists of passing from the twisted completion to matrix factorizations. In
section 7
, we recall this Cho-Hong-Lau construction and in particular amend all signs to the rules for
A_{\infty}
-categories.

4
Preliminaries on mirror symmetry

In this section, we recollect preliminaries on mirror symmetry of punctured surfaces from
[
12
]
.

4.1
Gentle algebras

In this section, we recall the
A_{\infty}
-gentle algebra from
[
12
]
and its deformed version from
[
13
]
. In particular, we explain the philosophy of
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
as a discrete relative Fukaya category.

Definition 4.1
.

A
punctured surface
is a closed oriented surface
S
with a finite set of punctures
M\subseteq S
. We assume that
|M|\geq 1
, or
|M|\geq 3
if
S
is a sphere.

The assumptions on
|M|
are cosmetical and explained in
[
24
]
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
(S,M)
be a punctured surface. An
arc
in
S
is a not necessarily closed curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to S
running from one puncture to another. An
arc system
on a punctured surface is a finite collection of arcs such that the arcs meet only at the set
M
of punctures. Intersections and self-intersections are not allowed. The arc system satisfies the
no monogons or digons
condition
[NMD]
if

•

No arc is a contractible loop in
S\setminus M
.

•

No pair of distinct arcs is homotopic in
S\setminus M
.

The arc system satisfies the
no monogons or digons in the closed surface
condition
[NMDC]
if

•

No arc is a contractible loop in
S
.

•

No pair of distinct arcs is homotopic in
S
.

An arc system is
full
if the arcs cut the surface into contractible pieces, which we call
polygons
.

Definition 4.3
.

A
dimer

Q
is a full arc system on a punctured surface such that every polygon is bounded by at least three arcs and the arcs along the boundary of a polygon are all oriented in the same direction. The letter
Q_{0}
denotes the set of punctures of the dimer, the letter
Q_{1}
denotes the set of arcs, and
|Q|
denotes the closed surface.

Remark 4.4
.

All polygons in a dimer are either bounded entirely clockwise, or entirely anticlockwise. A polygon neighboring a clockwise polygon is anticlockwise, and the other way around. A dimer can be interpreted as a quiver embedded in a surface, therefore quiver terminology applies and we may for instance refer to paths of
Q
.

The
gentle algebra

\operatorname{Gtl}Q
is an
A_{\infty}
-category defined as follows: Its objects are the arcs
a\in Q_{1}
. A basis for the hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(a,b)
is given by the set of all angles around punctures from
a
to
b
. This includes
empty angles
, which are the
identities
on the arcs. The hom spaces of
\operatorname{Gtl}Q
are not finite-dimensional, in contrast to what is classically called a gentle algebra. The
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-grading on
\operatorname{Gtl}Q
is given by declaring all interior angles of polygons to have odd degree. The differential
\mu^{1}
is plainly set to zero, and the product
\mu^{2}(\alpha,\beta)
of two angles
\alpha,\beta
is defined as the concatenation
\alpha\beta
of
\alpha
and
\beta
if both angles wind around the same puncture and
\alpha
starts where
\beta
ends:

\mu^{1}\coloneqq 0,\quad\mu^{2}(\alpha,\beta)\coloneqq(-1)^{|\beta|}\alpha\beta.

The higher products
\mu^{\geq 3}
on
\operatorname{Gtl}Q
are defined in terms of what we will call discrete immersed disks. Roughly speaking, a discrete immersed disk may either be a polygon, or a sequence of polygons stitched together. We make this precise by regarding immersions of the standard polygon
P_{k}
, depicted in
1(a)
:

Definition 4.5
.

Let
Q
be dimer. A
discrete immersed disk
in
Q
consists of an oriented immersion
D:P_{k}\to|Q|
of a standard polygon
P_{k}
into the surface, such that

•

The edges of the polygon are mapped to a sequence of arcs.

•

The immersion does not cover any punctures.

The immersion mapping itself is only taken up to reparametrization. The sequence of
interior angles
of
D
is the sequence of angles in
Q
given as images of the interior angles of
P_{k}
under the map
D
. An angle sequence
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}
is a
disk sequence
if it is the sequence of interior angles of some discrete immersed disk.

3
2
2
1
1
5
5
4

\alpha_{1}

\mu^{6}_{q}

Figure 4.1
:
Illustration for
\operatorname{Gtl}Q
and
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q

We can now describe the higher products
\mu^{\geq 3}
on
\operatorname{Gtl}Q
as follows:

Definition 4.6
.

Let
Q
be a dimer. Then the
gentle algebra

\operatorname{Gtl}Q
of
Q
is the
A_{\infty}
-category with objects the arcs
a\in Q_{1}
, hom spaces spanned by angles, and
A_{\infty}
-product
\mu
defined by
\mu^{1}=0
and
\mu^{2}(\alpha,\beta)=(-1)^{|\beta|}\alpha\beta
. To define
\mu^{k\geq 3}
, let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}
be any disk sequence, let
\beta
be an angle composable with
\alpha_{1}
, i.e.
\beta\alpha_{1}\neq 0
, and let
\gamma
be an angle post-composable with
\alpha_{k}
, i.e.
\alpha_{k}\gamma\neq 0
. Then

\mu^{k}(\beta\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{1})\coloneqq\beta,\quad\mu^{k}(\alpha_{k},\ldots,\alpha_{1}\gamma)\coloneqq(-1)^{|\gamma|}\gamma.

The higher products vanish on all angle sequences other than these.

In
[
13
]
we defined a deformation
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
of
\operatorname{Gtl}Q
, hoping it to provide a discrete relative Fukaya category. In
[
24
]
, we confirmed this hope. The starting point for the definition of
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
is a dimer which satisfies the [NMDC] condition. The category
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
is a curved
A_{\infty}
-deformation of
\operatorname{Gtl}Q
over the deformation base
B=\mathbb{C}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
, which has one deformation parameter per puncture. We denote the maximal ideal by
\mathfrak{m}=(Q_{0})\subseteq\mathbb{C}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
.

The curvature
\mu^{0}_{q}
of
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
is defined as follows: For every puncture
q\in Q_{0}
, denote by
\ell_{q}
the sum of all full turns around
q
, summed over all arc ends at
q
. The total curvature
\mu^{0}_{q}
of
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
is defined as the sum over all puncture contributions:

\mu^{0}_{q}\coloneqq\sum_{q\in Q_{0}}q\ell_{q}.

The product
\mu^{1}_{q}
still vanishes and the product
\mu^{2}_{q}\coloneqq\mu^{2}
is not deformed. The higher products
\mu^{\geq 3}_{q}
however count discrete immersed disks which are now also allowed to cover punctures, weighting the result of every disk with the product
\in\mathbb{C}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
of the punctures covered. The definition of
\mu^{0}_{q}
and
\mu^{\geq 3}_{q}
is depicted in
Figure 4.1
.

4.2
Zigzag paths

In this section, we recall the notions of zigzag paths and geometric consistency for dimers. An exhaustive reference is
[
11
]
. More information is also found in
[
24
]
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
Q
be a dimer. A
zigzag path

L
is an infinite path
\ldots a_{2}a_{1}a_{0}a_{-1}a_{-2}\ldots
of arcs in
Q
together with an alternating choice of “left” or “right” for every
i\in\,\mathbb{N}
such that

•

a_{i+1}a_{i}
lies in a clockwise polygon if
i
is assigned “right”,

•

a_{i+1}a_{i}
lies in a counterclockwise polygon if
i
is assigned “left”.

We also say that
L

turns left
at
a_{i}
if
i
is assigned “left” and
turns right
if
a_{i}
is assigned “right”. Two zigzag paths are identified if their paths including left/right indications differ only by integer shift.

a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{6}
a_{1}

Figure 4.2
:
A zigzag path
L

Slightly simplified, a zigzag path
L
is a path in
Q
that turns alternatingly maximally right and maximally left in
Q
. The typical shape of a zigzag path is drawn in
Figure 4.2
. If every puncture of
Q
has valence at least 4, then the left/right indication is a superfluous datum. In this case, the left/right indication for zigzag paths is a superfluous part of the datum of a zigzag path. For other dimers
Q
, the left/right indication is very important. An example is the
M
-punctured sphere
Q_{M}
which we will recall in
section 5
. Our definition deviates slightly from the definition of
[
12
]
.

Geometric consistency is a specific instance of various consistency conditions which can be imposed on dimers. To define it, we denote by
\tilde{Q}
the lift of the arc system
Q
to the universal cover of the closed surface
|Q|
. We define the auxiliary notion of zigzag rays as follows, depicted in
3(a)
:

Definition 4.8
.

Let
a\in\tilde{Q}_{1}
be an arc. Then the four zigzag rays starting at
a
are the sequences of arcs
(a_{i}^{1})_{i\geq 0}
,
(a_{i}^{2})_{i\geq 0}
,
(a_{i}^{3})_{i\geq 0}
and
(a_{i}^{4})_{i\geq 0}
in
\tilde{Q}
determined by
a_{0}^{1}=a_{0}^{2}=a_{0}^{3}=a_{0}^{4}=a
and the following properties:

•

The sequences
(a_{i}^{1})
and
(a_{i}^{2})
satisfy
h(a_{i}^{1/2})=t(a_{i+1}^{1/2})
.

•

The sequences
(a_{i}^{3})
and
(a_{i}^{4})
satisfy
t(a_{i}^{3/4})=h(a_{i+1}^{3/4})
.

•

The path
a_{i+1}^{1/2}a_{i}^{1/2}
lies in the boundary of a counterclockwise polygon when
i
is odd/even, and clockwise when
i
is even/odd.

•

The path
a_{i}^{3/4}a_{i+1}^{3/4}
lies in the boundary of a counterclockwise polygon when
i
is odd/even, and clockwise when
i
is even/odd.

a_{2}^{3}
a_{1}^{3}
a
a_{1}^{2}
a_{2}^{2}
a_{1}^{1}
a_{2}^{1}
a_{1}^{4}
a_{2}^{4}

(a)

Zigzag rays starting at
a\in\tilde{Q}_{1}

(b)

Not geometrically consistent

Figure 4.3
:
On consistency

A dimer is geometrically consistent if the zigzag rays starting with an arc
a
in the universal cover intersect nowhere, except at
a
itself. The precise definition reads as follows:

Definition 4.9
.

Let
Q
be a dimer. Then
Q
is
geometrically consistent
if for every
a\in\tilde{Q}_{1}
the four zigzag rays
(a_{i}^{1})
,
(a_{i}^{2})
,
(a_{i}^{3})
and
(a_{i}^{4})
satisfy the following property: Whenever
a_{i}^{k}=a_{j}^{l}
, then
i=j
and
k=l
, or
i=j=0
.

Remark 4.10
.

Many dimers are geometrically consistent. In contrast, a dimer on a sphere is never geometrically consistent. A dimer which contains the pattern sketched in
3(b)
is also not geometrically consistent. A geometrically consistent dimer satisfies the [NMDC] condition.

4.3
Matrix factorizations

In this section, we recall the notion of matrix factorizations. After recalling the definition, we focus on matrix factorization categories. Matrix factorizations serve as B-side in mirror symmetry. As such, our standard reference is
[
12
]
.

Matrix factorizations go back to 20th century work of Buchweitz and others. In a nutshell, the observation is as follows: Let
A
be an algebra and
\ell\in\,Z(A)
a central element. Such a pair
(A,\ell)
is also called a
Landau-Ginzburg model
. If
\ell
is prime in
A
, there is no nontrivial factorization
\ell=ab
in
A
. However, there may be modules
M,N\in\operatorname{Mod}A
with maps
f:M\to N
and
g:N\to M
such that both
g\circ f:M\to M
and
f\circ g:N\to N
are multiplication by
\ell
. In other words, for typical
\ell
there are more factorizations on the module level than in the algebra itself. This gives rise to the following definition:

Definition 4.11
.

Let
A
be an associative algebra and
\ell\in A
a central element. A
matrix factorization
of
(A,\ell)
is a pair of finitely generated projective
A
-modules
(P,Q)
together with
A
-module morphisms
f:P\to\,Q
and
g:Q\to P
such that
f\circ g=\ell\mathrm{id}_{Q}
and
g\circ f=\ell\mathrm{id}_{P}
.

Remark 4.12
.

There is an alternative definition: A matrix factorization is a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded projective
A
-module
M
together with an odd
A
-module map
\delta:M\to M
such that
\delta^{2}=\ell\mathrm{id}_{M}
. This definition is equivalent to
Definition 4.11
. Given
(M,\delta)
, simply put
P\coloneqq M^{\operatorname{even}}
and
Q\coloneqq M^{\operatorname{odd}}
. The odd map
\delta
then automatically splits into two maps
f:P\to Q
and
g:Q\to P
. Conversely given
\mathord{\hbox to74.29pt{\vbox to15.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.98051pt\lower-7.77745pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
