We will group the collection of all summands into cyclic orbits. Namely, regard any term
\lambda x_{e_{k+1}}\ldots x_{e_{1}}
appearing in this sum. Recall that by the cyclicity assumption of
Convention 7.6
we have

\lambda=\langle\mu^{k}(X_{e_{k+1}},\ldots,X_{e_{2}}),X_{e_{1}}\rangle=\langle\mu^{k}(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}),X_{e_{k+1}}\rangle,

(7.6)

This means the permuted version
\lambda x_{e_{k}}\ldots x_{e_{1}}x_{e_{k+1}}
also appears in the sum (
7.5
), with equal coefficient
\lambda
. This renders
\langle\mu^{k}(b,\ldots,b),b\rangle
cyclic. Summing over
k\geq 1
, we derive that the entire superpotential
W
is cyclic.

The second part of the proof is to check that
R_{e}=\partial_{x_{e}}W
. Usually, derivatives of a cyclic superpotential are written in terms of those paths starting with the given variable
x_{e}
. Since
W
is already cyclic, it suffices to extract all paths which instead end on
x_{e}
. To avoid double indexing by
e
, we will write
x_{f}
in the expansion of
\langle\mu^{k}(b,\ldots,b),b\rangle
. Ultimately, we calculate within
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
that

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{e}}W

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{e}}\left\langle\sum_{k\geq 1}\mu^{k}(b,\ldots,b),\sum_{f}x_{f}X_{f}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{e}}\left\langle\ell\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{L}}+\sum_{f}R_{f}Y_{f},\sum_{f}x_{f}X_{f}\right\rangle=\partial_{x_{e}}\left(\sum_{f}R_{f}x_{f}\right)=R_{e}.

The third part of the proof is to check that
\ell\in\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
is central. In this part of the proof, we shall regard
\ell
interchangeably as element of
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
or
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q},W)
. We start with the observation that within
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L})
we have

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{l\geq 1}\mu^{k}(b,\ldots,\mu^{l}(b,\ldots,b),\ldots,b)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mu^{k}(b,\ldots,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{i}\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}}+\sum R_{e}Y_{e},\ldots,b).

We have essentially performed a reordering of the double sum which is legitimate since the path lengths regarding
Q^{\mathbb{L}}
encountered in
\mu(b,\ldots,b)
increase as the number of inputs increases. We now claim that apart from
\mu^{2}(b,\ell_{i}\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}})
and
\mu^{2}(\ell_{i}\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}},b)
, the entire sum on the right-hand side of the equation lies in
\overline{(\partial_{x_{e}}W)}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L})
. Indeed, all terms with identities vanish for
k\geq 3
and
k=1
and the products involving
Y_{e}
all embrace relations
R_{e}
. We caution that the expression does in general not lie in
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}\operatorname{span}(\partial_{x_{e}}W)\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
, see
Remark 6.53
for an illustration. Ultimately, we conclude within
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L})
that

0=\mu^{2}(b,\ell\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{L}})+\mu^{2}(\ell\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{L}},b).

Let
1\leq i,j\leq N
and
e\in E_{ij}
. Then extracting the
X_{e}
-component gives

0=x_{e}\ell-\ell x_{e}\text{ within }\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W).

We conclude that
\ell
commutes with all arrows in
Q^{\mathbb{L}}
and hence with all finite paths. Let now
x\in\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
be an arbitrary element. If one wants to take Krull-continuity of the projection
\pi:\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}\to\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
for granted, simply choose a sequence
(x_{n})\in\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
with
x_{n}\to x
, then
0=\pi(x_{n})\ell-\ell\pi(x_{n})\to\pi(x)\ell-\ell\pi(x)
, hence
x\ell=\ell x
. If not, write
x=\sum_{k\geq 0}x_{k}
where
x_{k}
is homogeneous of length
k
. Then

\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}\pi(x_{k})\right)\ell=\pi\left(\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}x_{k}\right)\ell\right)=\pi\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}x_{k}\ell\right)=\pi\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}\ell x_{k}+z_{k}\right)=\ell\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}\pi(x_{k})\right).

Here
z_{k}\in\overline{(\partial_{x_{e}}W)}
denotes the difference of
x_{k}\ell
and
\ell x_{k}
when regarded as elements of
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
. Note that
z_{k}
has length at least
k
since
x_{k}
does, hence
\sum z_{k}\in\overline{(\partial_{x_{e}}W)}
. We conclude that
\ell
commutes with any element
x\in\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
and hence
\ell\in Z(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W))
. The fourth part of the proof consists of observing that the calculations still hold, even simplify, in case
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded growth. This finishes the proof.
∎

It is time to demonstrate how one calculates in the Jacobi algebra. For instance, within
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L})
the expression (
7.3
) simplifies to

\sum_{k\geq 1}\mu^{k}(b,\ldots,b)\,=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{i}\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}}\in\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L}).

We are ready to recall the construction of the mirror functor of Cho, Hong and Lau. The idea is to tweak the Koszul duality functor for the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
A=\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{L})
. We shall construct two functors
\widehat{F}
and
F
, where
\widehat{F}
serves the general case and
F
the case where
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded growth. The domain of both functors is
\mathcal{C}
and the codomains are the matrix factorization categories
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W),\ell)
and
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W),\ell)
, respectively. We start with the explicit descriptions on the level of objects:

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(X)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)L_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(L_{i},X),\quad\delta(m)=\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{\|m\|}\mu^{k}(m,b,\ldots,b)\right),

(7.7)

\displaystyle F(X)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W)L_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(L_{i},X),\quad\delta(m)=\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{\|m\|}\mu^{k}(m,b,\ldots,b)\right).

(7.8)

Let us explain how to interpret these expressions as matrix factorizations. Recall from
Remark 4.12
that a matrix factorization can be defined as a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded module which together with an odd endomorphism which squares to the desired central element. In our case, the
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
-module
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)L_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(L_{i},X)
shall have
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-grading inherited from
\operatorname{Hom}(L_{i},X)
. Since
b
is odd, the map
\delta
itself becomes odd. The module is projective and finitely generated since
\operatorname{Hom}(L_{i},X)
is finite-dimensional by assumption. If we check that
\delta
squares to
\ell
, then
\widehat{F}(X)
is indeed a matrix factorization.

Lemma 7.17
.

For
X\in\mathcal{C}
the object
\widehat{F}(X)
is indeed a matrix factorization of
(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W),\ell)
. If
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded growth, then
F(X)
is a matrix factorization of
(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W),\ell)
.

Proof.

We merely check the case of
\widehat{F}(X)
. It is our task to show that
\delta^{2}
equates to multiplying by
\ell
. Calculating in
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},X)
, we get

\displaystyle\delta(\delta(m))

\displaystyle=\delta(\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{\|m\|}\mu^{k}(m,b,\ldots,b))

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geq 1}\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{\|m\|+\|m\|-1}\mu^{l}(\mu^{k}(m,b,\ldots,b),b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n\geq 1}(\mu\cdot\mu)^{n}(m,b,\ldots,b)+\sum_{k\geq 2}\sum_{l\geq 1}\mu^{k}(m,b,\ldots,\mu^{l}(b,\ldots,b),\ldots,b)

\displaystyle=\mu^{2}(m,\ell\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{L}})=\ell m\in\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},X).

Here we have used the
A_{\infty}
-relation
\mu\cdot\mu=0
and
\sum_{l\geq 1}\mu^{l}(b,\ldots,b)=\ell\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{L}}
. We conclude that
\delta^{2}=\ell
. This shows that
\widehat{F}(X)
is a matrix factorization. The analogous calculations show that
F(X)
is a matrix factorization when
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded growth.
∎

We are finally ready to write down the functors
\widehat{F}
and
F
. On objects, these functors map
X\in\mathcal{C}
to their associated matrix factorizations
\widehat{F}(X)
and
F(X)
defined in (
7.7
). On morphisms, the functors are intuitively constructed through viewing
\operatorname{Hom}(L_{i},X)
as a module over
\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{L})
and composing with the Koszul duality functors. We explain the origin of the functors in more detail in
Remark 7.21
.

Definition 7.18
.

The
CHL functor

\widehat{F}
is the mapping

\displaystyle\widehat{F}:\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W),\ell),

(7.9)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\xmapsto{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}\widehat{F}(X),

(7.10)

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(m)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|)\|m\|+1}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu^{k+l+1}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},m,b,\ldots,b)

(7.11)

\displaystyle\text{for }m_{i}:X_{i}\to X_{i+1},\quad m\in\widehat{F}(X_{1}).

(7.12)

In case
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded growth, the functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W),\ell)
is defined analogously.

Remark 7.19
.

Let us elaborate and make sense of the definition of
\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})
. To start with, the sequence
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
is an arbitrary sequence of morphisms
m_{i}:X_{i}\to X_{i+1}
in
\mathcal{C}
. The morphism
\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})
is supposed to be a
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
-module map
\widehat{F}(X_{1})\to\widehat{F}(X_{k+1})
. Let us make sense of its definition: The element
m
used to define this map lies in the direct sum

\widehat{F}(X_{1})=\bigoplus_{i\in Q^{\mathbb{L}}_{0}}\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)L_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(L_{i},X_{1}).

Its image
\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(m)
is defined by the products
\mu(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},m,b,\ldots,b)
, with an arbitrary amount of
b
-insertions. The result of each of these products lives in

\widehat{F}(X_{k+1})=\bigoplus_{j\in Q^{\mathbb{L}}_{0}}\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)L_{j}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(L_{j},X_{k+1}).

To see this, recall that the formal parameters
x_{e}
in
b
simply get multiplied up as we evaluate the product. Simply speaking, a product where the right-most
b
-summand
x_{e}X_{e}
is consumed lands in
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)t(e)\otimes\allowbreak\operatorname{Hom}(L_{t(e)},X_{k+1})
. Finally, note that
\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})
is indeed a module map: If
a\in\,\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
, then
F(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(am)=aF(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(m)
since the factor
a
can be pulled to the front.

The main algebraic result of Cho, Hong and Lau entails that
\widehat{F}
indeed defines an
A_{\infty}
-functor:

Lemma 7.20
.

The CHL functor is a unital
A_{\infty}
-functor
\widehat{F}:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W),\ell)
. In case
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded growth, then
F
is a unital
A_{\infty}
-functor as well.

Proof.

We have that
\widehat{F}(\mathrm{id})(m)=(-1)^{\|m\|+1}\mu^{2}(\mathrm{id},m)=m
, therefore
\widehat{F}(\mathrm{id})=\mathrm{id}
. Similarly, we have
\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})=0
whenever
k\geq 1
and one
m_{i}
is an identity. For the functor relations, we need to check

\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{j}\|}\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,\mu(m_{i},\ldots,m_{j+1}),\ldots,m_{1})\\
=\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{MF}}(\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{i+1}),\widehat{F}(m_{i},\ldots,m_{1}))+\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{MF}}(\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})).

Both sides are homomorphisms
\widehat{F}(X)\to\widehat{F}(Y)
of certain
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
-modules. In order to equate both sides, we plug in an arbitrary element
m\in\widehat{F}(X)
and start evaluating from the left-hand side. Minding the notation
\tilde{\delta}(m)=(-1)^{|m|}\delta(m)
, we get:

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq i\leq j\leq k}(-1)^{\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{j}\|}\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,\mu(m_{i},\ldots,m_{j+1}),\ldots,m_{1})(m)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq j\leq k\\
0\leq l\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{j}\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|+1)\|m\|+1}\mu^{k+l}(m_{k},\ldots,\mu(m_{i},\ldots,m_{j+1}),\ldots,m_{1},m,b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k-1\\
j,l\geq 0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\|m\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|+1)\|m\|}\mu^{k-i+l+1}(m_{k},\ldots,\mu^{i+1+l}(m_{i},\ldots,m_{1},m,b,\ldots,b),b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\geq 0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\|m\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|+1)\|m\|}\mu^{i+1}(\mu^{k+j+1}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},m,b,\ldots,b),b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\geq 0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\|m\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|+1)\|m\|}\mu^{k+i+1}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},\mu^{j+1}(m,b,\ldots,b),b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}(-1)^{\maltese}\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{i+1})(\widehat{F}(m_{i},\ldots,m_{1})(m))

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{\|m\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|+1)\|m\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|)\|m\|+1+1}\tilde{\delta}(\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(m))

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{\|m\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|+1)\|m\|+1+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|)\|\tilde{\delta}(m)\|+1}\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(\tilde{\delta}(m))

\maltese=\|m\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|+1)\|m\|+(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{i}\|)\|m\|+1+(\|m_{i+1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|)(\|\widehat{F}(m_{i},\ldots,m_{1})(m)\|)+1

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}(-1)^{(\|m_{i+1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|)(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{i}\|+1)}\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{i+1})(\widehat{F}(m_{i},\ldots,m_{1})(m))

\displaystyle\quad+\tilde{\delta}(\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(m))-(-1)^{|\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})|}\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(\tilde{\delta}(m))

\displaystyle=\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{MF}}(\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{i+1}),\widehat{F}(m_{i},\ldots,m_{1}))(m)+\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{MF}}(\widehat{F}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1}))(m).

This calculation deserves a few comments. In the first equality, we have unraveled the definition of
\widehat{F}
. In the second equality, we have used the
A_{\infty}
-relations for
\mathcal{C}
, which adds an absolute flip to the sign. In the third equality, we have reinterpreted the inner and outer
\mu
applications as
\widehat{F}
or
\tilde{\delta}
, using that
\tilde{\delta}(m)=-\sum_{l\geq 1}\mu^{l}(m,b,\ldots,b)
. In the fourth equality, we have rewritten the expressions in terms of the products
\mu_{\operatorname{MF}}
. Since
m
was arbitrary, we conclude that the
A_{\infty}
-functor relations hold. The very same calculations apply for
F
in case
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded growth.
∎

Remark 7.21
.

The approach of Cho, Hong and Lau seems to originate from Yoneda functors instead of Koszul duality. We sketch here their line of reasoning
[
16
,
17
]
, focusing on the case of a single reference object,
\mathbb{L}=\{L\}
. Let
\mathrm{Ch}
denote the dg category of chain complexes.

A typical Yoneda functor in the
A_{\infty}
-world takes the shape
F=\operatorname{Hom}(L,-):\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Ch}
and sends
X\in\mathcal{C}
to the chain complex
(\operatorname{Hom}(L,X),\mu^{1})
. The functor
F
is not strict, but has higher components
F^{\geq 1}
given by
F(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(m)=\pm\mu(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},m)
.

Twisting an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
by any element
b
gives a new possibly curved
A_{\infty}
-category. Cho, Hong and Lau simply decided to enlarge the category
\mathcal{C}
by introducing formal variables
x_{e}
for every odd basis element
X_{e}
of the hom space
\operatorname{Hom}(L,L)
. Their twist is given by the element
b=\sum x_{e}X_{e}
. This gives an enlarged and twisted category
\mathcal{C}_{b}=(\mathcal{C}\langle\!\langle x_{e}\rangle\!\rangle,b)
. Its products
\mu^{k}_{b}
are given by twisting
\mu
with
b
. This category in principle has a Yoneda functor
F_{b}:\mathcal{C}_{b}\to\mathrm{Ch}
itself, given by

\displaystyle F_{b}(X)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Hom}(L,X),\mu^{1}_{b}),

(7.13)

\displaystyle F_{b}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(m)

\displaystyle=\mu_{b}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},m).

At this point, adaptations have to be made. For instance the map
\mu^{1}_{b}
, explicitly
\mu^{1}_{b}(m)=\mu(b,\ldots,m,\ldots,b)
, is not a differential anymore because
\mathcal{C}_{b}
has curvature
\mu(b,\ldots,b)
. Before we proceed, simplify
F_{b}
slightly by inserting
b
only at the right-most positions in (
7.13
).

To make
\mu^{1}_{b}
a differential and restore the functor
F_{b}
, the crucial idea is to enforce relations among the variables
x_{e}
to at least render the curvature a multiple of the identity. This precisely explains the choice of the relations
R_{e}
. It be noted that
\mu^{1}_{b}
is still not a differential since
\mu(b,\ldots,b)
may contain an identity term
\ell\mathrm{id}_{L}
. This in mind,
\mu^{1}_{b}
however squares to
\ell
and we are in the realm of matrix factorizations. The functor
F_{q}
can be adapted accordingly. This explains the original motivation of Cho, Hong and Lau.

7.3
Deformed Landau-Ginzburg model

In this section, we start deforming the construction of Cho, Hong and Lau. The idea is as easy as it can get: We apply the Cho-Hong-Lau construction formally to the whole formal family of categories
(\mathcal{C}_{q})
. Intuitively, we obtain a family of matrix factorization categories. More precisely, we get a new algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
, a new central element
\ell_{q}\in Z(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}))
, a deformation
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
of
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)\ell)
and a functor
\widehat{F}_{q}
running from
\mathcal{C}_{q}
to
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
. In other words: Thanks to the generality the Cho-Hong-Lau construction, the mirror gets deformed when the input category
\mathcal{C}
gets deformed.

In the present section, we devote ourselves to the construction of
W_{q}
,
\ell_{q}
and the Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
. In
section 7.5
, we define the deformed matrix factorizations category and in
section 7.6
we construct our deformed mirror functor. We document our setup in the following convention:

Convention 7.22
.

The category
\mathcal{C}
, the reference objects
\mathbb{L}=\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{N}\}
, the basis elements
X_{e}
,
Y_{e}
,
\mathrm{id}^{*}_{L_{i}}
and the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
are as in
Convention 7.6
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{q}
be a deformation of
\mathcal{C}
over a deformation base
(B,\mathfrak{m})
. We assume
\mathcal{C}_{q}
is (deformed) cyclic on odd elements:

\langle\mu_{q}(X_{e_{k+1}},\ldots,X_{e_{2}}),X_{e_{1}}\rangle=\langle\mu_{q}(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}),X_{e_{k+1}}\rangle.

We assume that
\mathcal{C}_{q}
is strictly unital with the same identities
\mathrm{id}_{X}
as in
\mathcal{C}
:

\mu_{q}^{1}(\mathrm{id}_{X})=0,\quad\mu_{q}^{2}(a,\mathrm{id}_{X})=a,\quad\mu_{q}^{2}(\mathrm{id}_{X},a)=(-1)^{|a|}a,\quad\mu_{q}^{\geq 3}(\ldots,\mathrm{id}_{X},\ldots)=0.

The letter
\mathbb{L}_{q}
denotes the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{q}
given by the objects
L_{1},\ldots,L_{N}\in\mathbb{L}
.

In overview, our construction of the deformed Landau-Ginzburg model
(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
proceeds as follows: We model the algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
still via the CHL quiver
Q^{\mathbb{L}}
and relations. More precisely, we regard the enlarged version
B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
instead of
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
. The deformed superpotential
W_{q}
lives in this enlarged algebra instead of
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
. The deformed Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
is defined as quotient of
B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
instead of
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
. The deformed potential
\ell_{q}
lives in the center of
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
. We develop in parallel a variant in case
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded type. For the variant in case of bounded type, we need an additional condition on the deformed products
\mu_{q}
. We refer to this condition as being of slow growth, and the condition already implies that
\mathbb{L}
is of bounded growth. All terminology is collected in
Table 7.1
.

:
Terminology of deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction

Definition 7.23
.

\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of
slow growth
if for all morphisms
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
in
\mathcal{C}
and for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an
l_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall l\geq l_{0}:\quad\mu_{q}^{k+l}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},b,\ldots,b)\in\mathfrak{m}^{n}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},X_{k+1}).

Remark 7.24
.

If
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth, then
\mathbb{L}
is automatically of bounded growth.

We are now ready to define the deformed relations
R_{q,e}
and potential
\ell_{q}
. As the reader may expect, these are simply read off from
\mu_{q}(b,\ldots,b)
.

Definition 7.25
.

The
deformed relations

R_{q,e}\in B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
and the
deformed potential
are defined by

\sum_{k\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{k}(b,\ldots,b)=\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in E_{ij}}R_{q,e}Y_{e}.

(7.14)

The
deformed superpotential
is defined as

W_{q}=\langle\sum_{k\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{k}(b,\ldots,b),b\rangle\in B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}.

The
deformed Jacobi algebra
is defined as

\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})=\frac{B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}}{~\overline{(\partial_{x_{e}}W)}^{\otimes}~}.

The
deformed Landau-Ginzburg model
is the pair
(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
. If
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth, then
R_{q,e}
,
\ell_{q}
,
W_{q}
are regarded as elements of
B\widehat{\otimes}\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
, the deformed Jacobi algebra is defined as
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})=(B\widehat{\otimes}\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}})/\overline{(\partial_{x_{e}}W)}
, and the deformed Landau-Ginzburg model is
(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
.

Remark 7.26
.

The element
W_{q}\in B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
is cyclic, as we shall see in
Lemma 7.30
. Cyclicity for
W_{q}
is to be understood in the sense that
W_{q}\in B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}_{\operatorname{cyc}}
, where
\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}_{\operatorname{cyc}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
is the subspace of elements invariant under cyclic permutation. More explicitly,
W_{q}
is of the form
\sum m_{i}p_{i}
with
m_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}^{\to\infty}
and
p_{i}\in\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}_{\operatorname{cyc}}
. This explains the sense in which
W_{q}
is cyclic.

The derivatives
\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q}
are elements of
B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
. More precisely, the derivative
\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q}
is defined as
\lim_{k}\partial_{x_{e}}\pi_{k}(W_{q})
where
\pi_{k}
denotes the projection
\pi_{k}:B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}_{\operatorname{cyc}}\to B/\mathfrak{m}^{k}\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}_{\operatorname{cyc}}
. More explicitly, if
W_{q}=\sum m_{k}p_{k}
with
m_{k}\in\mathfrak{m}^{\to\infty}
and
p_{k}\in\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}_{\operatorname{cyc}}
, then
\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q}=\sum m_{k}\partial_{x_{e}}p_{k}
. This explains how to understand
\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q}
.

Remark 7.27
.

The definition of the relations
R_{q,e}
is understood as follows: The chunk of
R_{q,e}
read off from
\mu_{q}^{k}(b,\ldots,b)
for a certain
k\geq 0
consists of paths in
Q^{\mathbb{L}}
of length
k
, weighted with deformation parameters. More precisely, this chunk lies in
B\widehat{\otimes}\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}_{k}
. The total relation
R_{q,e}
is the sum of these chunks over
k\geq 0
. As such, the deformed relations
R_{q,e}
all lie in
B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
. As in the classical case, the relation
R_{q,e}
only contain paths running from
h(e)
to
t(e)
for every
e\in E_{ij}
.

Similarly, the potential
\ell_{q}
lies in
B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
. We shall from now on typically denote by
\ell_{q}
its projection to
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
. As in the classical case, the only paths contained in
\ell_{q}
are loops of
Q^{\mathbb{L}}
.

Remark 7.28
.

In contrast to the classical case, the category
\mathcal{C}_{q}
may also have infinitesimal curvature. This is not a problem. We simply start counting from
k=0
instead of
k=1
in (
7.14
).

Remark 7.29
.

Within the present
section 7
, we have decided to stick to the following closure notations: If
X\subseteq\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
, then
\overline{X}
is the closure with respect to the Krull topology. If
X\subseteq B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
or
X\subseteq B\widehat{\otimes}\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
, then
\overline{X}
denotes the closure with respect to the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology. If
X\subseteq B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
, then
\overline{X}^{\otimes}
denotes the closure with respect to the tensor topology. For more information on the tensor topology, we refer to
section 6.9
.

In the rest of this section, we check properties of the deformed Landau-Ginzburg model. Our first milestone is a deformed version of
Lemma 7.16
:

Lemma 7.30
.

Assume
Convention 7.22
. Then the superpotential
W_{q}\in B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
is cyclic and we have
R_{q,e}=\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q}
. The potential
\ell_{q}\in\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
is central. The analogous statements hold if
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of the classical version
Lemma 7.16
. Cautious is due when working with the completed ideals. We shall therefore spell out a few details. By comparison, the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{q}
is allowed to have curvature is technically unproblematic.

Cyclicity of
W_{q}
and the property that
R_{q,e}=\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q}
follow immediately from the cyclicity assumption in
Convention 7.22
and are unproblematic in the sense that these properties hold in
B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
.

We shall rather comment in detail on the centrality of the deformed potential
\ell_{q}
: Within
B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L})
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{0\leq l\leq k}\mu_{q}^{k-l+1}(b,\ldots,\mu_{q}^{l}(b,\ldots,b),b,\ldots,b)

(7.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{k}(b,\ldots,\mu_{q}^{l}(b,\ldots,b),\ldots,b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mu_{q}^{k}(b,\ldots,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{q,i}\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}}+\sum R_{e}Y_{e},\ldots,b).

In the first row, we have used the curved
A_{\infty}
-relations. In the second row, we have applied a reordering of the double sum with increasing path length. In the third row, we have reproduced (
7.14
). We claim that up to the two terms
\mu_{q}^{2}(b,\ell_{q,i}\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}})
and
\mu_{q}^{2}(\ell_{q,i}\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}},b)
, the entire sum lies in
\overline{\operatorname{span}(\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q})}^{\otimes}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L})
. Indeed, for every
k\geq 3
and for
k=1
the summand lies in the intersection of
B\widehat{\otimes}\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}\operatorname{span}(R_{e})\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
and
B\widehat{\otimes}\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}_{\geq k-1}
. Therefore the series converges, apart from the two special summands, to an element of
\overline{\operatorname{span}(\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q})}^{\otimes}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L})
. Within
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L})
, we conclude that

0=\mu_{q}^{2}(b,\ell_{q}\mathrm{id})+\mu_{q}^{2}(\ell_{q}\mathrm{id},b).

Let
1\leq i,j\leq N
and
e\in E_{ij}
. Then extracting the
X_{e}
-component gives

0=x_{e}\ell_{q}-\ell_{q}x_{e}\text{ within }\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}).

We conclude that
\ell
commutes with all arrows in
Q^{\mathbb{L}}
, hence with all finite paths and the image of
B\otimes\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
. Let now
x\in B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}
be an arbitrary element. We shall prove that
\pi(x)\ell=\ell\pi(x)
, where
\pi:B\widehat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}}\to\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
is the projection. The easiest way to achieve this is by approximating
x
by finite paths via the tensor topology. Pick any sequence
(x_{n})\subseteq B\otimes\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
such that
x_{n}\to x
in the tensor topology. Since
\overline{(\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q})}^{\otimes}
is closed with respect to the tensor topology, the projection
\pi
is continuous. We obtain

0=\pi(x_{n})\ell-\ell\pi(x_{n})\to\pi(x)\ell-\ell\pi(x)\text{ within }\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}).

We conclude that
\ell
commutes with any element
x\in\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
and hence
\ell\in Z(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}))
. This finishes the third part.

The fourth part of the proof consists of observing that the calculations still hold in case
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth. Indeed, in this case the double sums in the calculation (
7.15
) all become finite and apart from the two special terms lie in
\overline{\operatorname{span}(\partial_{x_{e}}W_{q})}
, noting that the closure is taken this time only with respect to the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology. The approximation of
x\in B\widehat{\otimes}\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
happens by a sequence
x_{n}\in B\otimes\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
which converges to
x
in the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology. This proves the fourth step and finishes the proof.
∎

7.4
Projectives of deformed algebras

In this section, we show that projectives of an algebra and any deformation are in one-to-one correspondence. The purpose of this section is to build a rigorous foundation for our deformed category of matrix factorizations.

Projectives of
A_{q}
Projectives of
A

The core idea of matching projectives is as follows: Projectives of
A
are direct
A
-module summands of a free
A
-module. Given such a decomposition
A^{\oplus n}=P_{1}\oplus\ldots P_{k}
into projectives, intuition says that the modules
P_{i}
can be extended to
A_{q}
-modules
P_{q,i}
such that
A_{q}^{\oplus n}=P_{q,1}\oplus\ldots\oplus P_{q,k}
. The extensions
P_{q,i}
are direct summands of
A_{q}^{\oplus n}
and therefore automatically projective. This procedure allows us to turn projectives of
A
into projectives of
A_{q}
. Conversely, our expectation is that a projective module
P_{q}
of
A_{q}
gives rise to a projective
P
of
A
by merely setting
P=P_{q}/\mathfrak{m}P_{q}
. This allows us to match projectives of
A_{q}
with projectives of
A
.

If
P
is a projective of
A
, then
B\widehat{\otimes}P
is not a projective of
A_{q}
. Instead, the module needs to be tweaked a little. This is best accomplished by realizing
P
as image of an
A
-linear idempotent
p:A^{\oplus n}\to A^{\oplus n}
and lifting
p
to an
A_{q}
-linear idempotent
p_{q}:A_{q}^{\oplus n}\to A_{q}^{\oplus n}
. We achieve this by an adaption of the classical idempotent lifting argument to the case of formal deformations:

Lemma 7.31
.

Let
A
be an algebra and
A_{q}=(B\widehat{\otimes}A,\mu_{q})
a deformation. Let
p:A^{\oplus n}\to A^{\oplus n}
be an
A
-linear idempotent in the sense that
p^{2}=p
. Then there exists an
A_{q}
-linear idempotent
p_{q}:A_{q}^{\oplus n}\to A_{q}^{\oplus n}
with leading term
p
.

Proof.

The idea is to lift
p
to an arbitrary
A_{q}
-module map and then turn it iteratively into a projection. As a first step, let
p_{0}:A_{q}^{\oplus n}\to A_{q}^{\oplus n}
be any
A_{q}
-linear continuous lift of
p
, for instance given by setting
p_{0}(e_{i})=p(e_{i})\in A^{\oplus n}\subseteq A_{q}^{\oplus n}
where
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
are the basis vectors of
A_{q}^{\oplus n}
.

As a second step, we construct a sequence of
A_{q}
-module maps
p_{k}:A_{q}^{\oplus n}\to A_{q}^{\oplus n}
such that
p_{k}^{2}-p_{k}\in\mathfrak{m}^{2^{k}}\operatorname{End}(A_{q}^{\oplus n})
and
p_{k+1}-p_{k}\in\mathfrak{m}^{2^{k}}\operatorname{End}(A_{q}^{\oplus n})
. The first item in the sequence is the already constructed map
p_{0}
. Assume for induction that the sequence has already been constructed up to
p_{k}
. Then put
\varepsilon=p_{k}^{2}-p_{k}
. By induction hypothesis,
\varepsilon:A_{q}^{\oplus n}\to A_{q}^{\oplus n}
is an
A_{q}
-module map which lies in
\mathfrak{m}^{2^{k}}\operatorname{End}(A_{q}^{\oplus n})
.

The trick is to set
p_{k+1}=p_{k}\pm\varepsilon
in order to render
p_{k+1}^{2}-p_{k+1}
of lesser order than
p_{k}^{2}-p_{k}=\varepsilon
. The correct sign is neither plus or minus in general, but differs for two parts of
\varepsilon
. More precisely, we split
\varepsilon
into a part mapping to the image of
p_{k}
and a part almost mapping to the kernel of
p_{k}
:

\varepsilon=p_{k}\circ\varepsilon+(p_{k}-\mathrm{id})\circ\varepsilon.

The right sign for the first part is
-1
, while the sign for the second part is
+1
. In consequence, we put

p_{k+1}=p_{k}-p_{k}\circ\varepsilon+(\mathrm{id}-p_{k})\circ\varepsilon.

Since
p_{k}
is a
A_{q}
-linear, so is
p_{n+1}
. Noting that
\varepsilon
commutes with
p_{k}
by definition, we get

\displaystyle p_{k+1}^{2}-p_{k+1}

\displaystyle=(p_{k}-p_{k}\varepsilon+(\mathrm{id}-p_{k})\varepsilon)^{2}-(p_{k}-p_{k}\varepsilon+(\mathrm{id}-p_{k})\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=(p_{k}^{2}-p_{k})-2p_{k}^{2}\varepsilon+2(p_{k}-p_{k}^{2})\varepsilon+p_{k}\varepsilon-(\mathrm{id}-p_{k})\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=p_{k}\varepsilon+(\mathrm{id}-p_{k})\varepsilon-2p_{k}^{2}\varepsilon+2(p_{k}-p_{k}^{2})\varepsilon+p_{k}\varepsilon-(\mathrm{id}-p_{k})\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=4(p_{k}-p_{k}^{2})\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

In the calculation, we have written
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
for any expressions containing an
\varepsilon^{2}
factor. We conclude that
p_{k+1}\in(\mathfrak{m}^{2^{k}})^{2}\operatorname{End}(A_{q}^{\oplus n})=\mathfrak{m}^{2^{k+1}}\operatorname{End}(A_{q}^{\oplus n})
. This finishes the construction of
p_{k+1}
and thereby the inductive construction of the sequence
(p_{k})
.

Finally, the sequence
(p_{k})
converges to a limit map
p_{q}:A_{q}^{\oplus n}\to A_{q}^{\oplus n}
. It can be explicitly described as limit of
p_{k}
in every matrix entry, where matrix representation is taken with respect to the direct sum description
A_{q}^{\oplus n}
. We conclude that
p_{q}
is an
A_{q}
-linear map with
p_{q}^{2}=p_{q}
. Since
x_{k+1}-x_{k}\in\mathfrak{m}\operatorname{End}(A_{q}^{\oplus n})
for
k\geq 0
, the map
p_{q}
reduces to
p
modulo
\mathfrak{m}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Let
P\subseteq A_{q}^{\oplus n}
be a submodule, not necessarily projective. Regard the projection map
\pi:B\widehat{\otimes}A\to A
. Then
\pi(P)\subseteq A^{\oplus n}
is an
A
-submodule of
A^{\oplus n}
. To avoid confusion, let us write
\mu_{q}
for the deformed product of
A_{q}
and
\mu
for the product of
A
. In these terms, we have for
a\in A
and
x\in B\widehat{\otimes}A^{\oplus n}
that
\mu(a,\pi(x))=\pi(\mu_{q}(a,x))
. Now if
x\in P
, then
\mu_{q}(a,x)\in P
and we get
\mu(a,\pi(x))=\pi(\mu_{q}(a,x))\in\pi(P)
. This shows that
\pi(P)
is an
A
-submodule. With this observation in mind, we can make the following statement:

Lemma 7.32
.

Let
P\subseteq A_{q}^{\oplus n}
be a projective module. Then the
A
-module
\pi(P)
is projective.

Proof.

Write
A_{q}^{\oplus n}=P\oplus Q
for some
A_{q}
-module
Q
. As kernels of projections, both are automatically closed with respect to the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology, in particular pseudoclosed. According to
Proposition 2.29
, we get that
A^{\oplus n}=\pi(P)\oplus\pi(Q)
as vector spaces. As observed before, both
\pi(P)
and
\pi(Q)
are actually
A
-submodules of
A^{\oplus n}
. We conclude that
\pi(P)
is a projective
A
-module. This finishes the proof.
∎

The endomorphism space
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(A_{q}^{\oplus n},A_{q}^{\oplus n})
is the same as
A_{q}^{\oplus n}=B\widehat{\otimes}A^{\oplus n}
as
B
-module. If
A_{q}^{\oplus n}=P_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus P_{k}
is a decomposition into
A_{q}
-submodules, then every hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P_{i},P_{j})
can be naturally interpreted as
B
-linear subspace of
B\widehat{\otimes}A^{\oplus n}
. In fact,
B\widehat{\otimes}A^{\oplus n}
is their direct sum.

Lemma 7.33
.

Let
A_{q}^{\oplus n}=P_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus P_{k}
be a decomposition as
A_{q}
-module. Then
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P_{i},P_{j})\subseteq B\widehat{\otimes}A^{\oplus n}
is pseudoclosed and quasi-flat, and we have
\pi(\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P_{i},P_{j}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\pi(P_{i}),\pi(P_{j}))
.

Proof.

We have
A_{q}^{\oplus n^{2}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(A_{q}^{\oplus n},A_{q}^{\oplus n})=\bigoplus_{i,j}\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P_{i},P_{j})
. By
Proposition 2.29
, we conclude that every
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P_{i},P_{j})
is quasi-flat and pseudoclosed.

It remains to show that
\pi(\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P_{i},P_{j}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\pi(P_{i}),\pi(P_{j}))\subseteq A_{q}^{\oplus n^{2}}
. Pick any
A_{q}
-linear morphism
\varphi:P_{i}\to P_{j}
. We claim that
\pi(\varphi):\pi(P_{i})\to\pi(P_{j})
is an
A
-module map. Indeed, for
a\in A
and
x\in P_{i}
we have

\pi(\varphi)(\mu(a,\pi(x)))=\pi(\varphi(\mu_{q}(a,x)))=\pi(\mu_{q}(a,\varphi(x)))=\mu(a,\pi(\varphi(x)))=\mu(a,\pi(\varphi)(x)).

Conversely, let
\varphi:\pi(P_{i})\to\pi(P_{j})
be an
A
-module morphism. We shall construct an
A_{q}
-module map
\varphi_{q}:P_{i}\to P_{j}
such that
\pi(\varphi_{q})=\varphi
. The idea is to view the composition
\tilde{\varphi}:A^{\oplus n}\twoheadrightarrow\pi(P_{i})\to\pi(P_{j})\hookrightarrow A^{\oplus n}
. We can write
\tilde{\varphi}
as an element of
A^{\oplus n^{2}}
. In particular, we obtain a map of
A_{q}
-modules
\varphi_{q}:P_{i}\hookrightarrow A_{q}^{\oplus n^{2}}\to A_{q}^{\oplus n^{2}}\twoheadrightarrow P_{j}
with
\pi(\varphi_{q})=\varphi
. This shows
\pi(\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P_{i},P_{j}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\pi(P_{i}),\pi(P_{j}))
and finishes the proof.
∎

Let us set up more terminology. We denote by
\operatorname{Proj}A
the category of finitely generated projective
A
-modules and by
\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}
the category of finitely generated
A_{q}
-modules. Both are ordinary unital
\mathbb{C}
-linear categories. We use the terminology of
A_{\infty}
-deformations for these two categories, even though the only product of both is an ordinary associative composition with different sign rule. In terms of
Definition 2.41
, we shall prove that
\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}
is a loose object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{Proj}A
.

The first step is to define the mapping
F:\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}\to\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{Proj}A
as given by
F(P)=P/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot P)
. Obviously, the quotient
P/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot P)
carries a natural action of
A=A_{q}/\mathfrak{m}A_{q}
, rendering it an
A
-module. This mapping
F
is the generalization of the mapping
P\mapsto\pi(P)
to projectives not presented as submodules of free modules. We shall now prove that
F(P)
is actually projective:

Lemma 7.34
.

Let
P\in\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}
. Then the
A
-module
F(P)=P/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot P)
is projective.

Proof.

It suffices to regard the case of a submodule
P\subseteq A_{q}^{\oplus n}
. We claim that
P/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot P)\cong\pi(P)
as
A
-modules. Regard the
A
-linear projection map
\pi:P\to\pi(P)
. The map is surjective. Its kernel is
P\cap\mathfrak{m}A^{\oplus n}
. Since
P
is a direct summand of
B\widehat{\otimes}A^{\oplus n}
as
B
-module,
P\subseteq B\widehat{\otimes}A^{\oplus n}
is quasi-flat and pseudoclosed. In particular we have
P\cap\mathfrak{m}A^{\oplus n}=\mathfrak{m}P=\mathfrak{m}\cdot P
. We obtain an isomorphism of
A
-modules
P/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot P)\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi(P)
. By
Lemma 7.32
,
\pi(P)
is a projective
A
-module and we finish the proof by concluding that
P/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot P)\in\operatorname{Proj}A
.
∎

Proposition 7.35
.

The category
\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}
is an essentially surjective loose object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{Proj}A
over
B
via the mapping
F:\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}\to\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{Proj}A
.

Proof.

The proof consists of two steps: First we show that
F
is essentially surjective. Second, we show that
\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}
becomes an object cloning deformation of
\operatorname{Proj}A
.

To see that
F
is essentially surjective, let
P\in\operatorname{Proj}A
be any finitely generated projective
A
module. There exists an isomorphic module
P^{\prime}
and a decomposition
A^{\oplus n}=P^{\prime}\oplus Q
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and some module
Q
. Pick a lift
A_{q}^{\oplus n}=P^{\prime}_{q}\oplus Q_{q}
. Then
P^{\prime}_{q}/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot P^{\prime}_{q})\cong P
, since
\mathfrak{m}P^{\prime}_{q}=\mathfrak{m}\cdot P^{\prime}_{q}
. This shows that
P
is reached by
F
up to isomorphism. In other words,
F
is essentially surjective.

To see that
\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}
is a loose object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{Proj}A
, we have to provide linear isomorphisms

\psi_{P,Q}:\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q)/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(F(P),F(Q))\quad\text{for }P,Q\in\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}.

Let
\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q)
. Then
\varphi
descends to a map
F(P)\to F(Q)
. We define
\psi_{P,Q}(\varphi)
as this descended map. We need to check that
\psi_{P,Q}
is an isomorphism. For this, we note that
\psi_{P,Q}
is defined purely in terms of the module structure of
P
and
Q
. It therefore suffices to show bijectivity of
\psi_{P,Q}
only in case
P
and
Q
are embedded as submodules of a free module
A_{q}^{\oplus n}
. In this case, the hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q)
has an interpretation as pseudoclosed quasi-flat
B
-submodule of
B\widehat{\otimes}A^{\oplus n^{2}}
. The map
\psi_{P,Q}
is in this case the map
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q)/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q))\to\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\pi(P),\pi(Q))
simply induced from the projection to zeroth order
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\pi(P),\pi(Q))
. The map
\psi_{P,Q}
is surjective since
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\pi(P),\pi(Q))=\pi(\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q))
by
Lemma 7.33
, and injective since
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,Q)
is quasi-flat. This shows that
\psi_{P,Q}
is an isomorphism.

Finally, we have to check that composition in the category
\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}
reduces to composition in
\operatorname{Proj}A
via
\{\psi_{P,Q}\}_{P,Q}
once
\mathfrak{m}
is divided out. This is however immediate, since composition commutes with descending to the quotient by
\mathfrak{m}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.36
.

The correspondence between projectives of
A_{q}
and
A
can typically be made more explicit. Assume for instance, an element
v\in A
is an idempotent in the sense that
v^{2}=v
. Then
Av
is a projective of
A
since
Av
is the image of the
A
-linear idempotent map
(-)v:A\to A
. Moreover, if
v^{2}=v
within
A_{q}
, then
A_{q}v
is a projective of
A_{q}
, since it is the image of the idempotent
A_{q}
-linear map
(-)v:A_{q}\to A_{q}
.

The
A
-projective
F(A_{q}v)=A_{q}v/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot A_{q}v)
can be naturally identified with
Av
via the map
\varphi:A_{q}v/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot A_{q}v)\xrightarrow{\sim}Av
given by
\varphi(a)=\pi(a)v
for
a\in A_{q}v\subseteq B\widehat{\otimes}A
, where
\pi:B\widehat{\otimes}A\to A
is the standard projection. Since
\mu_{q}
is a deformation of
\mu
, we have
\varphi(\mu_{q}(a,b))=\pi(\mu_{q}(a,b))v=\mu(\pi(a),\pi(b))v
and conclude that
\varphi
is
A
-linear. The map
\varphi
is surjective since for
a\in A
we have
\varphi(a)=av
. The map
\varphi
is injective, since
\pi(a)v=0
for
a\in A_{q}v
implies
\pi(\mu_{q}(a,v))=0
, hence
\mu_{q}(a,v)\in\mathfrak{m}\cap A_{q}v\subseteq\mathfrak{m}\cdot A_{q}v
, since
A_{q}v\subseteq B\widehat{\otimes}A
is quasi-flat.

Hom spaces between projectives can also be identified. Let
v,w
be two idempotents of
A
still idempotent in
A_{q}
. Then we have the natural identification

\frac{\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(A_{q}v,A_{q}w)}{m\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(A_{q}v,A_{q}w)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(F(A_{q}v),F(A_{q}w))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(Av,Aw).

7.5
Deformed matrix factorizations

In this section, we define deformed categories of matrix factorizations. There are several issues on the road to their definition. In order to satisfy our demand to serve as mirror model in the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction, the definition furthermore needs to deviate from what one expects. The first step in this section is to explain these issues. We then provide a successful construction of the deformed category of matrix factorizations for any deformed Landau-Ginzburg model. We finish this section with an explanation how this category becomes an deformation of the ordinary category of matrix factorizations.

Our starting point is a pair
(A,\ell)
consisting of an associative algebra
A
with a central element
\ell\in A
. Recall from
section 4.4
that the category of matrix factorizations
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
is a dg category which has as objects pairs of finitely generated projective
A
-modules
M,N
together with
A
-module morphisms
f:M\to N
and
g:N\to M
such that
f\circ g=\ell\mathrm{id}_{N}
and
g\circ f=\ell\mathrm{id}_{M}
.

The starting point for the deformed setup is a pair
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
of a deformation
A_{q}
of
A
and a deformed central element
\ell_{q}\in Z(A_{q})
. More precisely,
A_{q}=(B\widehat{\otimes}A,\mu_{q})
shall be an associative deformation of the algebra
A
over a deformation base
B
, in the sense that
\mu_{q}:(B\widehat{\otimes}A)\otimes(B\widehat{\otimes}A)\to B\widehat{\otimes}A
is a
B
-linear associative product that reduces to the product
\mu:A\otimes A\to A
once
\mathfrak{m}
is divided out. The element
\ell_{q}\in Z(A_{q})
is required to be a deformation of
\ell
in the sense that
\ell_{q}-\ell\in\mathfrak{m}A
. We fix this terminology as follows:

Definition 7.37
.

A
Landau-Ginzburg model

(A,\ell)
is a pair of an associative algebra and a central element. A
deformation

(A_{q},\ell_{q})

of

(A,\ell)
consists of an algebra deformation
A_{q}=(B\widehat{\otimes}A,\mu_{q})
of
A
together with a central element
\ell_{q}\in Z(A_{q})
which is a deformation of
\ell
. Disregarding the reference to
(A,\ell)
, we may call
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
a
deformed Landau-Ginzburg model
.

The following is a naive candidate for a deformed matrix factorization category: Objects are projective
A_{q}
-modules
M,N
together with
A_{q}
-module maps
f:M\to N
and
g:N\to M
such that
f\circ g=\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{N}
and
g\circ f=\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{M}
. The hom space of two such matrix factorizations
\mathord{\hbox to78.49pt{\vbox to15.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.96312pt\lower-7.77745pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

and
\mathord{\hbox to84.16pt{\vbox to16.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.79652pt\lower-8.06413pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
