\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
would be defined as
\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(M,M^{\prime})\oplus\ldots
similar to the classical case. The differential
\mu^{1}
on this category would be given by commuting a morphism with
f,g
and
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
as in the classical case. The product
\mu^{2}
would be given by standard matrix composition. This defines a dg category and a naive candidate for a deformed category of matrix factorizations.

Let us now sketch the issues associated with this naive definition. The leading question is how to interpret this category as a deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
, both on object and morphism level.

The first issue consists of matching projective modules of
A_{q}
with projective modules of
A
. Even more, we also need that the hom spaces between projective modules of the two kinds match. We have resolved this in
section 7.4
.

The second issue consists of matching the factorization morphisms
f,g
for
\ell_{q}
with factorization morphisms for
\ell
. In fact, multiple matrix factorizations of
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
reduce to the same matrix factorization of
(A,\ell)
. A simple example is to multiply
f
by an element
1+\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon\in\mathfrak{m}
and multiply
g
by
(1+\varepsilon)^{-1}=\sum(-\varepsilon)^{i}
. Conversely, it is unclear whether every matrix factorization of
(A,\ell)
extends to a matrix factorization of
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
. This makes a precise correspondence between matrix factorizations of
(A,\ell)
and of
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
impossible. We resolve this by building
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
as an object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
.

The third issue consists of liberalizing the category
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
enough so that it can serve as codomain of the deformed mirror functor. As we shall see, our deformed mirror functor does not map to objects
(M,N,f,g)
where all compositions
f\circ g
and
g\circ f
are equal to each other. Instead, the compositions will only agree with the predefined central element
\ell_{q}
on zeroth order and differ per object. This phenomenon is inevitable when starting form a curved
A_{\infty}
-deformation. It requires us to admit objects very liberally into the category
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
.

We resolve the third issue as follows: The objects of
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
are pairs
(M,N,f,g)
of projective modules and module maps, but
f\circ g
and
g\circ f
need only equal
\ell_{q}
up to zeroth order. The difference of
f\circ g
and
\ell_{q}
, and of
g\circ f
and
\ell_{q}
, serves as curvature of
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
. Since our deformed mirror functor typically requires the codomain to carry curvature as well, this resolves the third issue sufficiently.

At this point, we stress the crucial importance of
A_{q}
being a deformation of
A
in the sense of
Definition 6.1
. Intuitively, if
A_{q}
is smaller than
B\widehat{\otimes}A
, then its modules have smaller hom spaces as well, which breaks all chances to make the category of matrix factorizations of
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
a deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
.

Definition 7.38
.

Let
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
be a deformed Landau-Ginzburg model. A
deformed matrix factorization
of
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
consists of two finitely generated projective
A_{q}
-modules
P,Q
together with
A_{q}
-module maps
f:P\to Q
and
g:Q\to P
such that
f\circ g-\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{Q}\in\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(Q,Q)
and
g\circ f-\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{P}\in\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(P,P)
.

Example 7.39
.

Let
A=\mathbb{C}[X,Y]
and regard the trivial deformation
A_{q}=(A\llbracket q\rrbracket,\mu)
. Regard the central element
\ell=XY
and the deformed central element
\ell_{q}=XY+q
. The object
(A,A,X,Y)
is a matrix factorization of
(A,\ell)
. Both
(A_{q},A_{q},X,Y)
and
(A_{q},A_{q},X+5q,Y+qX)
are deformed matrix factorizations of
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
. Both however do not factor to
\ell_{q}
precisely, but only on zeroth order. In fact, there are no single elements
f,g\in A_{q}
such that
fg=XY+q
and
f-X\in(q)
and
g-Y\in(q)
. To see this, assume
f=X+qz
and
g=Y+qw
, then
fg=XY+q(zY+Xw)+q^{2}zw
. In order for this to be equal to
\ell_{q}=XY+q
, we need that
zY+Xw
is
1
on first order, which is impossible. This shows that matrix factorizations need not have strict lifts, but deformed matrix factorizations are rather abundant.

Remark 7.40
.

As in the classical case, the pair of modules
(P,Q)
can also be described as a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded module
M=P\oplus Q[1]
(where both graded parts are projective). The pair of morphisms
(f,g)
can be described as an odd morphism
\delta:P\oplus Q[1]\to P\oplus Q[1]
. We shall liberally switch between the two kinds of notation, identifying

\mathord{\hbox to74.29pt{\vbox to15.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.98051pt\lower-7.77745pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-25.6475pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.60483pt}{-2.44444pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.76 L 23.07 2.76}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{16.87183pt}{1.9919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 -2.76 L 0.55 -2.76}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{-1.9919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad\left(P\oplus Q[1],\begin{pmatrix}0&g\\
f&0\end{pmatrix}\right).

We are now ready to define
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
. As in the classical case, if
(M,\delta)
is a deformed matrix factorization, we denote by
\tilde{\delta}
the tweaked differential given by
\tilde{\delta}(m)=(-1)^{|m|}\delta(m)
.

Definition 7.41
.

Let
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
be a deformed Landau-Ginzburg model. The
deformed category of matrix factorizations

\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is defined as follows:

•

Objects are the deformed matrix factorizations
(M,\delta)
of
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
.

•

Hom spaces are given by
\operatorname{Hom}((M,\delta_{M}),(N,\delta_{N}))=\operatorname{Hom}_{A_{q}}(M,N)
, naturally
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded.

•

The curvature of an object
(M,\delta)
is given by
\mu^{0}_{(M,\delta)}=\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{M}-\delta^{2}
.

•

The differential is given by
\mu^{1}(f)=\tilde{\delta}_{N}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ\tilde{\delta}_{M}
for
f\in\operatorname{Hom}((M,\delta_{M}),(N,\delta_{N}))
.

•

The product is given by
\mu^{2}(f,g)=(-1)^{\|f\||g|}f\circ g
.

Remark 7.42
.

In writing
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
, we have abused notation: The category
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is not the same as (classical) matrix factorizations of the pair
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
.

We aim at showing that
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is an object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
. To make this true, we provide a map
\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})\to\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
. The construction of this map is easy and consists of taking the leading term of any matrix factorization:

Definition 7.43
.

Let
(A_{q},\ell_{q})
be a deformed Landau-Ginzburg model. Let
(M,\delta)
be a deformed matrix factorization of
(A,\ell)
. Then the
leading term
of
(M,\delta)
is the matrix factorization
O(M,\delta)
of
(A,\ell)
given by

O(M,\delta)=(M/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot M),\pi(\delta)).

In this definition,
\pi(\delta)
denotes the induced map
M/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot M)\to M/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot M)
and is automatically an
A
-module map. The quotient
M/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot M)
is understood to be performed in even and odd degree separately. We are now ready to show that
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is an object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
.

Proposition 7.44
.

The category
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is a loose object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
along the map
O:\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})\to\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps: The first step is to show that
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
satisfies the (curved)
A_{\infty}
-axioms, merely regarding its hom spaces as
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector spaces instead of
B
-modules. The second step is to investigate the
B
-linear structure on its hom spaces and the shape of the products. The third step is to draw the conclusion that
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is indeed an object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
.

For the first step, we check all curved
A_{\infty}
-relations for
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
one after another. The first relation reads

\mu^{1}(\mu^{0}_{(M,\delta)})=\tilde{\delta}\circ(\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{M}-\delta^{2})-(\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{M}-\delta^{2})\circ\tilde{\delta}=0.

Note we have used that
\delta^{2}=-\tilde{\delta}^{2}
and
\ell_{q}
is central. The second relation reads

\displaystyle\mu^{1}(\mu^{1}(f))+(-1)^{\|f\|}\mu^{2}(\mu^{0},f)+\mu^{2}(f,\mu^{0})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\delta}\circ(\tilde{\delta}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ\tilde{\delta})

\displaystyle\quad-(-1)^{|f|+1}(\tilde{\delta}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ\tilde{\delta})\circ\tilde{\delta}

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{\|f\|+|f|}(\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{M}-\delta^{2})\circ f+f\circ(\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{M}-\delta^{2})

\displaystyle=-\delta^{2}\circ f+f\circ\delta^{2}-\ell_{q}f+f\ell_{q}+\delta^{2}\circ f-f\circ\delta^{2}=0.

We have used again that
\ell_{q}
is central. The third relation is analogous to the classical case and reads

\displaystyle\mu^{1}(\mu^{2}(f,g))+(-1)^{\|g\|}\mu^{2}(\mu^{1}(f),g)+\mu^{2}(f,\mu^{1}(g))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\|f\||g|}(\tilde{\delta}\circ f\circ g-(-1)^{|fg|}f\circ g\circ\tilde{\delta})

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{|f||g|+\|g\|}(\tilde{\delta}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ\tilde{\delta})\circ g

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{\|f\|\|g\|}f\circ(\tilde{\delta}\circ g-(-1)^{|g|}g\circ\tilde{\delta})=0.

The fourth relation is associativity and all other relations vanish.

For the second part of the proof, we give
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
the structure of loose object-cloning deformation. This entails providing for every two deformed matrix factorizations
(M,\delta_{M}),(N,\delta_{N})
a linear
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded isomorphism

\psi_{(M,\delta_{M}),(N,\delta_{N})}:\frac{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})}((M,\delta_{M}),(N,\delta_{N}))}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})}((M,\delta_{M}),(N,\delta_{N}))}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)}(O(M,\delta_{M}),O(N,\delta_{N})).

The hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})}((M,\delta_{M}),(N,\delta_{N}))
merely consists of the direct sum of four hom spaces from the category
\operatorname{Proj}A_{q}
. Thanks to
Proposition 7.35
, every of these four hom spaces, quotiented by
\mathfrak{m}
, comes with a natural isomorphism to the corresponding hom space from the category
\operatorname{Proj}A
. We now construct the isomorphism
\psi_{(M,\delta_{M}),(N,\delta_{N})}
by simply combining these four isomorphisms on their respective domains.

For the third part of the proof, we explain why
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is a loose object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
via
O
. Indeed, composition in
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is merely given by matrix composition and we have seen in
Proposition 7.35
that it is compatible via
\psi
with composition in
\operatorname{Proj}A
. Similarly, the differential in
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is defined via commuting with
\delta
, which reduces via
\psi
to commuting with the induced
\delta
in
\operatorname{Proj}A
and hence to the differential of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
. Finally, the curvature in
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
is infinitesimal and reduces to zero after dividing out
\mathfrak{m}
. This proves
\operatorname{MF}(A_{q},\ell_{q})
a loose object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(A,\ell)
via
O
and finishes the proof.
∎

7.6
Deformed mirror functor

In this section, we construct our deformed mirror functor. The idea is to simply repeat the Cho-Hong-Lau construction using the deformed category
\mathcal{C}_{q}
as input instead of
\mathcal{C}
. As we have seen in
section 7.3
, we obtain a deformed Landau-Ginzburg model
(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
. The target of the mirror functor is then the deformed category of matrix factorizations
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
. We deploy notation and assumptions from
Convention 7.22
. As in the classical case, we construct functors in the general case and the case of slow growth in parallel.

The first step of this section is to define the deformed functors
\widehat{F}_{q}:\mathcal{C}_{q}\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
and
F_{q}:\mathcal{C}_{q}\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
on object level. The second step is to define them on morphism level. Finally we check that
\widehat{F}_{q}
and
F_{q}
satisfy the deformed
A_{\infty}
-functor relations and their leading terms are the classical Cho-Hong-Lau functors
\widehat{F}
and
F
.

An important assumption in this section is that
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
be a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
, or
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})
be a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W)
, in the sense of
Definition 6.1
. As such, these algebras come with
B
-linear algebra isomorphisms

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi}_{\operatorname{Jac}}:\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}(B\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W),\mu_{\operatorname{Jac},q}),

\displaystyle\varphi_{\operatorname{Jac}}:\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}(B\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W),\mu_{\operatorname{Jac},q}).

We fix these isomorphisms and will always view
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
and
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})
as deformations of
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
and
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W)
.

Remark 7.45
.

By
Remark 6.6
, the isomorphisms
\widehat{\varphi}_{\operatorname{Jac}}
and
\varphi_{\operatorname{Jac}}
can be assumed to be unital and be
\mathbb{C}Q^{\mathbb{L}}_{0}
-bimodule morphisms. For instance, any vertex element
L_{i}\in\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
is mapped to the vertex
L_{i}\in\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
, without getting deformed. We also have
L_{i}^{2}=L_{i}
in
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
and
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})
, since the same holds in
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
and
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W)
. The two module maps
(-)L_{i}:\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})\to\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
and
(-)L_{i}:\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})\to\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})
are therefore idempotents. We conclude that
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})L_{i}
and
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})L_{i}
are projectives.

We start with the explicit description of
\widehat{F}_{q}
and
F_{q}
on object level.

\displaystyle\widehat{F}_{q}(X)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})L_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(L_{i},X),\quad\delta(m)=\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{\|m\|}\mu_{q}^{k}(m,b,\ldots,b)\right),

\displaystyle F_{q}(X)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})L_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(L_{i},X),\quad\delta(m)=\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{\|m\|}\mu_{q}^{k}(m,b,\ldots,b)\right).

Note that each map
\delta
is well-defined since
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth.

Lemma 7.46
.

For
X\in\mathcal{C}
the object
\widehat{F}_{q}(X)
is indeed a deformed matrix factorization of
(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
. If
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth, then
F_{q}(X)
is a deformed matrix factorization of
(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
.

Proof.

We merely check the case of
\widehat{F}_{q}(X)
. It is our task to show that
\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{\widehat{F}_{q}(X)}-\delta^{2}
is infinitesimal. Calculating in
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},X)
, we find

\displaystyle\ell_{q}m-\delta^{2}(m)

\displaystyle=\ell_{q}m-\delta^{2}(m)

\displaystyle=\ell_{q}m+\sum_{i,j\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{i+1}(\mu_{q}^{j+1}(m,b\ldots,b),b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle=\ell_{q}m-\sum_{i,j,k\geq 0}\mu_{q}(m,\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{i},\mu_{q}(\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{j}),\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{k})-(-1)^{\|m\|}\sum_{i\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{i+2}(\mu_{q}^{0},m,b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|m|}\sum_{i\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{i+2}(\mu_{q}^{0},m,b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{m}\cdot(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},X)).

Here we have used the curved
A_{\infty}
-relation for
\mu_{q}
and
\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{l}(b,\ldots,b)=\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}
. We conclude that
\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{\widehat{F}_{q}(X)}-\delta^{2}
is infinitesimal and
\widehat{F}_{q}(X)
is a deformed matrix factorization. The analogous calculations show that
F_{q}(X)
is a matrix factorization when
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of bounded growth.
∎

Remark 7.47
.

Explicitly, the curvature of the object
\widehat{F}_{q}(X)
is the endomorphism of
\widehat{F}_{q}(X)
given by

\mu^{0}_{\operatorname{MF},\widehat{F}_{q}(X)}(m)=\ell_{q}m-\delta^{2}(m)=(-1)^{|m|}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{l+2}(\mu_{q}^{0},m,b,\ldots,b).

We now define the deformed CHL functor in analogy to the classical CHL functor:

Definition 7.48
.

The
deformed CHL functor

\widehat{F}_{q}
is the mapping

\displaystyle\widehat{F}_{q}:\mathcal{C}_{q}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q}),

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\xmapsto{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}\widehat{F}_{q}(X),

\displaystyle\widehat{F}_{q}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})(m)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|)\|m\|+1}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{k+l+1}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},m,b,\ldots,b)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{for }m_{i}:X_{i}\to X_{i+1},\quad m\in\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1}).

In case
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth, the functor
F_{q}:\mathcal{C}_{q}\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
is defined analogously.

Remark 7.49
.

Note that we put
F_{q}^{0}=0
, as opposed to
F^{0}_{q}\coloneqq\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{l+1}(-,b,\ldots,b)
.

We shall now prove that
\widehat{F}_{q}
and
F_{q}
are actually functors. We shall also show that their leading terms are the classical functors
\widehat{F}
and
F
. The categories
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
and
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
are only object-cloning deformations of
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W),\ell)
and
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W),\ell)
, so making the statement on leading terms rigorous we need to provide an identification map on object level. In
Definition 7.43
, we have already provided such identification maps

\displaystyle O:\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W),\ell),

\displaystyle O:\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W),\ell).

The map
O
sends
\widehat{F}_{q}(X)
and
F_{q}(X)
to

\displaystyle O(\widehat{F}_{q}(X))

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})L_{i}}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})L_{i}}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(L_{i},X),\quad\delta(m)=\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{\|m\|}(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mu_{q}^{k}(m,b,\ldots,b))\right),

\displaystyle O(F_{q}(X))

\displaystyle=\left(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})L_{i}}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})L_{i}}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(L_{i},X),\quad\delta(m)=\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{\|m\|}(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mu_{q}^{k}(m,b,\ldots,b))\right).

Here
\pi
denotes the projection from the Jacobi algebra to its quotient by
\mathfrak{m}
. Meanwhile, the object
\widehat{F}(X)
is given by a sum of projectives of the form
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)L_{i}
. As discussed in
Remark 7.36
, the difference is entirely cosmetic and we shall naturally identify

\displaystyle O(\widehat{F}_{q}(X))\xrightarrow{\sim}\widehat{F}(X),

(7.16)

\displaystyle O(F_{q}(X))\xrightarrow{\sim}F(X).

A similar statement holds for the hom spaces. Regard a hom space between two image objects
\widehat{F}_{q}(X)
and
\widehat{F}_{q}(Y)
, divide out
\mathfrak{m}
, and identify the quotient via the map
\psi
from
section 7.4
. According to
Remark 7.36
, the result can further be identified naturally with the hom space in
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W),\ell)
or
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W),\ell)
:

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{Hom}(\widehat{F}_{q}(X),\widehat{F}_{q}(Y))}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Hom}(\widehat{F}_{q}(X),\widehat{F}_{q}(Y))}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[~~\sim~~]{\psi}\operatorname{Hom}\left(\frac{\widehat{F}_{q}(X)}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot\widehat{F}_{q}(X)},\frac{\widehat{F}_{q}(Y)}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot\widehat{F}_{q}(Y)}\right)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}(\widehat{F}(X),\widehat{F}(Y)),

(7.17)

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{Hom}(F_{q}(X),F_{q}(Y))}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot\operatorname{Hom}(F_{q}(X),F_{q}(Y))}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[~~\sim~~]{\psi}\operatorname{Hom}\left(\frac{F_{q}(X)}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot F_{q}(X)},\frac{F_{q}(Y)}{\mathfrak{m}\cdot F_{q}(Y)}\right)\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}(F(X),F(Y)).

We claim that with respect to this identification, the leading terms of the two functors
\widehat{F}_{q}
and
F_{q}
are the classical Cho-Hong-Lau functors
\widehat{F}
and
F
:

Theorem 7.50
.

Assume
Convention 7.22
and that
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W)
. Then the mapping
\widehat{F}_{q}
defines a functor of loose object-cloning
A_{\infty}
-deformations

\widehat{F}_{q}:\mathcal{C}_{q}\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q}),\ell_{q}).

The leading term of
\widehat{F}_{q}
via the identifications (
7.16
) and (
7.17
) is the classical Cho-Hong-Lau functor
\widehat{F}
.

Assume instead
Convention 7.22
, that
\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth and that
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q})
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W)
. Then the mapping
F_{q}
defines a functor of loose object-cloning
A_{\infty}
-deformations

F_{q}:\mathcal{C}_{q}\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q}),\ell_{q}).

The leading term of
F_{q}
via the identifications (
7.16
) and (
7.17
) is the classical Cho-Hong-Lau functor
F
.

Proof.

First we check the
A_{\infty}
-functor relations, then we comment on the leading term. To start with, for the functor relations it is our task to check that

\sum_{0\leq j\leq i\leq k}(-1)^{\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{j}\|}\widehat{F}_{q}(m_{k},\ldots,\mu_{q}(m_{i},\ldots,m_{j+1}),\ldots,m_{1})\\
=\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{MF}}(\widehat{F}_{q}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{i+1}),\widehat{F}_{q}(m_{i},\ldots,m_{1}))+\mu^{1}_{\operatorname{MF}}(\widehat{F}_{q}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})).

Checking these relations is similar to the classical case
Lemma 7.20
. The calculation does not use explicitly that
\delta^{2}
vanishes, therefore remains intact. However, due to the possible curvature of
\mathcal{C}_{q}
a few new terms appear on one side of the
A_{\infty}
-functor equation. We shall check these terms in more detail.

Assume there are
k\geq 1
input morphisms
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
. Comparing with the calculation in
Lemma 7.20
, the new terms on the left-hand side of the functor relation are

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq n\leq k}(-1)^{\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{n}\|}\widehat{F}_{q}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{n+1},\mu^{0}_{q},m_{n},\ldots,m_{1})(m)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n\leq k\\
0\leq l\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{n}\|+\|m\|(\|m_{1}\|+\ldots+\|m_{k}\|+1)+1}\mu_{q}^{k+l+1}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{n+1},\mu^{0}_{q},m_{n},\ldots,m_{1},m,b,\ldots,b).

There are no new terms on the right-hand side of the functor relation. When applying the curved
A_{\infty}
-relation as in the proof of
Lemma 7.20
, the terms on the left-hand side disappear and the terms
\mu_{q}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},m,b,\ldots,\mu_{q}^{\geq 0}(b,\ldots,b),\ldots,b)
come in, which still vanish since
\sum\mu_{q}(b,\ldots,b)=\ell_{q}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{L}_{q}}
. This proves the functor relations for
k\geq 1
.

Also for
k=0
the functor relation is still satisfied:

\widehat{F}_{q}^{1}(\mu^{0}_{X})(m)=(-1)^{\|m\|+1}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{q}^{l+2}(\mu^{0}_{q},m,b,\ldots,b)=\mu^{0}_{\widehat{F}_{q}(X)}.

The computations for
F_{q}
are analogous. This finishes the checks of the functor relations.

Finally, let us now comment on the leading terms of
\widehat{F}_{q}
and
F_{q}
. Indeed, every functor component
\widehat{F}_{q}^{k}
or
F_{q}^{k}
is constructed via the deformed products of
\mathcal{C}_{q}
.

We need to check the
\operatorname{Jac}(\widehat{Q^{\mathbb{L}}},W_{q})
-linear map
\widehat{F}_{q}^{k}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1}):\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1})\to\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{k+1})
. Up to terms of
\mathfrak{m}\cdot\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{k+1})
, this map is equal to

\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu^{k+l+1}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},-,b,\ldots,b):\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1})\to\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{k+1}).

Passing along
\psi
gives

\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu^{k+l+1}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},-,b,\ldots,b):\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1})/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1}))\to\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{k+1})/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{k+1})).

This map is not the same as
\widehat{F}^{k}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})
yet. But now identify
\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1})/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\widehat{F}(X_{1})
and similarly for
X_{k+1}
. Recalling
Remark 7.36
, the induced map
\widehat{F}(X_{1})\to\widehat{F}(X_{k+1})
is merely given by the composition
\widehat{F}(X_{1})\to\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1})/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{1}))\to\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{k+1})/(\mathfrak{m}\cdot\widehat{F}_{q}(X_{k+1}))\to\widehat{F}(X_{k+1})
, yielding the map

\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu^{k+l+1}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{k},-,b,\ldots,b):\widehat{F}(X_{1})\to\widehat{F}(X_{k+1}).

This is precisely
\widehat{F}^{k}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1})
. We have shown that the leading term of
\widehat{F}_{q}
is
\widehat{F}
. The same argument holds for
F_{q}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

8
Deformed mirror symmetry

In this section, we collect all preliminary results and prove deformed mirror symmetry, our main theorem. The procedure is as follows: We start with a geometrically consistent dimer
Q
under
Convention 5.1
. By
section 5
, the deformed category of zigzag curves
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
is deformed cyclic. By
section 7
, we obtain an algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
plus central element
\ell_{q}\in\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
together with a deformed functor

F_{q}:\quad\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q}).

Because of the slow growth requirements, this functor is restricted to the domain category
\operatorname{Gtl}_{Q}
instead of
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. By
section 6
, the algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
. The necessary requirement for this last step is that
\check{Q}
is cancellation consistent and of bounded type. Finally, we interpret the category
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
as a deformation of the classical mirror
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
, and the functor
F_{q}
as a deformation of the classical
A_{\infty}
mirror quasi-isomorphism

F:\quad\operatorname{Gtl}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell).

In particular, we conclude that
F_{q}
itself is a quasi-isomorphism.

8.1
Mirror symmetry by Cho-Hong-Lau

In this section, we recollect how a gentle algebra as specific instance of the Cho-Hong-Lau construction yields mirror symmetry for punctured surfaces. The first step in this section is to provide some details on this specific instance. Second, we read off the specific properties for the Cho-Hong-Lau construction. Third, we realize that the resulting mirror functor indeed recovers mirror symmetry for punctured surfaces. This section is an integrated summary of
[
17
, Chapter 10]
.

Remark 8.1
.

Cho, Hong and Lau depart from a folklore version of wrapped Fukaya category, where the complete set of products is unclear. We have opted in the present paper to use the very rigorous description of
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q
and
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
from
[
24
]
. In particular, we restrict to the case that
Q
is geometrically consistent or a standard sphere dimer.

Our starting point is a dimer
Q
whose zigzag paths are equipped with a specific choice of spin structure, which we have codified in
Convention 5.1
. We shall start describing the specific instance of the Cho-Hong-Lau construction needed for mirror symmetry. The first step is to choose
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q
. The subcategory of reference objects, denoted
\mathbb{L}
in
section 7
, is the category of zigzag paths
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q
.

As a second step, we describe the CHL basis that needs to be chosen. The right choice of basis elements
\{X_{e}\}
for
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}}(L_{1},L_{2})
is the collection of all transversal intersection points of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
which are odd as morphisms
L_{1}\to L_{2}
. In other words, every single transversal intersection points between two arbitrary zigzag curves appears as basis element for precisely one hom space, namely the one in which it is odd. The set of transversal intersection points between zigzag curves in
Q
is precisely the same as the set of arcs
Q_{1}
. We shall therefore fix notation as follows:

Definition 8.2
.

For every arc
a\in Q_{1}
, we denote by
X_{a}
the odd morphism in
\mathbb{L}
which is located at the midpoint of
a
. Similarly, we denote by
Y_{a}
the even morphism in
\mathbb{L}
which is located at the midpoint of
a
.

Remark 8.3
.

Visually speaking, we have the correspondence
odd morphisms
(transversal only)
\longleftrightarrow
arrows
a\in Q_{1}
\longleftrightarrow
even morphisms
(transversal only)

Gadget

General

Specific

Category

Table 8.1
:
For every gadget involved in the Cho-Hong-Lau construction, this overview exhibits the general definition and its specific shape in the case of the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
.

With this in mind, let us describe the specific instance of the Cho-Hong-Lau construction which yields mirror symmetry for punctured surfaces. The specific instance consists of a specific pair
(\mathbb{L},\mathcal{C})
, a specific choice of CHL basis and a specific choice of odd pairing. These data are given as follows:

•

The category
\mathcal{C}
is the derived category
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q
of the gentle algebra
\operatorname{Gtl}Q
.

•

The subcategory
\mathbb{L}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is the subcategory
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q
of zigzag paths.

•

The CHL basis consists of all odd cohomology basis elements
X_{e}
between zigzag paths, all even cohomology basis elements
Y_{e}
between zigzag paths, and the co-identity elements
\mathrm{id}^{*}_{L_{i}}
.

•

The odd pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
on
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}
is defined by enforcing the pairing identities (
7.1
). Explicitly, one sets
\langle-,-\rangle
to zero on all pairs of basis elements except

\langle X_{e},Y_{f}\rangle=\delta_{ef},\quad\langle\mathrm{id}^{*}_{L_{i}},\mathrm{id}_{L_{j}}\rangle=\delta_{ij}.

Application of the Cho-Hong-Lau construction to the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
yields precisely the dual dimer
\check{Q}
, which we have recalled in
section 4.5
:

Lemma 8.4
.

The CHL quiver of the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
is the quiver
Q^{\mathbb{L}}=\check{Q}
.

Proof.

By general definition, the vertices of the quiver
Q^{\mathbb{L}}
are the reference objects
L_{i}\in\mathbb{L}
and the arrows from
L_{i}
to
L_{j}
are given by the index set
E_{ij}
. For the specific case of the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
, the reference objects are the zigzag paths of
Q
. Since zigzag paths of
Q
are in correspondence with vertices of
\check{Q}
, this identifies the vertices of
Q^{\mathbb{L}}
and
\check{Q}
. For given vertices
i,j
, the set of arrows
E_{ij}
from
i
to
j
is equal to the set of odd transversal intersection points
L_{i}\to L_{j}
. Every odd transversal intersection is located at the midpoint of an arc
a\in Q_{1}
. The corresponding arc
a\in\check{Q}_{1}
runs from
i
to
j
as well, as illustrated in
Figure 8.2
. We conclude that
Q^{\mathbb{L}}=\check{Q}
.
∎

L_{1}
L_{2}
a
in
Q
in
\check{Q}
a
L_{1}
L_{2}

Figure 8.2
:
This picture depicts the correspondence between odd intersections in
Q
and arrows in
\check{Q}
. The odd basis element
X_{a}:L_{1}\to L_{2}
, given by the intersection of
\tilde{L}_{1}
and
\tilde{L}_{2}
located at the midpoint of the arc
a
, corresponds to an arrow
L_{1}\to L_{2}
in the dual dimer
\check{Q}
.

Lemma 8.5

(
[
17
, Lemma 10.13]
)
.

Application of the Cho-Hong-Lau construction to the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
yields the familiar superpotential

W=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{k}\ldots a_{1}\\
\text{clockwise}\end{subarray}}(a_{k}\ldots a_{1})_{\operatorname{cyc}}~-~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{k}\ldots a_{1}\\
\text{counterclockwise}\end{subarray}}(a_{k}\ldots a_{1})_{\operatorname{cyc}}\in(\mathbb{C}\check{Q})_{\geq 3}.

In particular, application yields the familiar relations
R_{a}=r_{a}^{+}-r_{a}^{-}
and the familiar Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W)=\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
.

Proof.

It is our task to determine the superpotential
W
. After that, the conclusion on the relations
R_{a}
and the Jacobi algebra is immediate.

The general superpotential is given by
W=\langle\mu(b,\ldots,b),b\rangle
. Specifically for the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
this description boils down to calculating the products

\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}})

The result of such a product is a linear combination of the even basis elements
Y_{e}
and possibly an identity. It is our task to extract the coefficient of every
Y_{e}
in the product. We claim that this coefficient is precisely

\langle\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}),X_{e}\rangle=\begin{cases}+1&\text{if }e_{k}\ldots e_{1}e\text{ is a clockwise polygon in }\check{Q}\\
-1&\text{if }e_{k}\ldots e_{1}e\text{ is a counterclockwise polygon in }\check{Q}\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}.

(8.1)

To compute the product, we use our explicit description of the minimal model
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}
, see
section 5.3
. Recall that
section 5.3
actually describes the deformed version
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}
. The
A_{\infty}
-structure
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}
is obtained from the deformed
A_{\infty}
-structure of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
by extracting the leading terms of every product. Spelling this out, the higher products in
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}
are simply determined by only those CR, ID, DS and DW disks that do not cover any punctures.

With this in mind, we are ready to calculate the products (
8.1
). Let
D
be a (CR, ID, DS or DW) disk contributing to the product. Note that all input morphisms
X_{e_{i}}
are odd and the output is not an identity, so
D
is necessarily a CR disk.

e
e_{1}
e_{k}
\ldots

(a)

The two disks contributing to
\partial_{e}W

(b)

Checkerboard coloring

Figure 8.3
:
One can put a checkerboard coloring on the area of the dimer
Q
. The checkerboard coloring makes it easy to check that there are only two disks which contribute to
\partial_{e}W
.

We claim that the disk
D
precisely bounds the interior of an (elementary) polygon, as depicted in
3(a)
. Indeed, the zigzag curves of
Q
split
D
into pieces of two types: the first type comprises an interior of a polygon of
Q
, and the second type comprises a polygonal neighborhood of a puncture bounded by neighboring zigzag curves. Two pieces of
D
bounding each other are of opposite type. An example of this checkerboard coloring is depicted in
3(b)
. Since the specific disk
D
is supposed to cover no puncture, it only consists of pieces of the first type. In fact, there it consists of only one such piece, since any second bordering piece would be of the second type. This shows that
D
precisely fits a polygon of
Q
.

Let us conclude the product formula (
8.1
). If
e_{k}\ldots e_{1}e
is a clockwise polygon in
\check{Q}
, then the same path forms a clockwise polygon in
Q
. As we have just seen, there is a single disk
D
contributing the product, and we conclude

\mu(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}})=Y_{e}.

The sign is positive because all inputs of
D
are odd and run clockwise with the disk, the output is odd, and no
\#
signs appear on the zigzag curve segments. If
e_{k}\ldots e_{1}e
is a counterclockwise polygon in
\check{Q}
, then the path
e_{1}\ldots e_{k}e
is a counterclockwise polygon in
Q
. As we have just seen, there is a single disk
D
contributing to the product, and we conclude

\mu(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}})=-Y_{e}.

The sign is negative because
D
has
k
odd inputs running counterclockwise with
D
, while there is also a
\#
-sign in every of the
k+1
corners of the polygon.

If
e_{k}\ldots e_{1}e
is a path in
\check{Q}
that is not a polygon, there is no disk
D
with inputs
X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k}}
and output
Y_{e}
at all. In summary, we have shown (
8.1
). Ultimately, the superpotential is given by

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\langle\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(b,\ldots,b),b\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{k},\ldots,e_{1},e}e_{k}\ldots e_{1}e\langle\mu(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}),Y_{e}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{k}\ldots a_{1}\\
\text{clockwise}\end{subarray}}(a_{k}\ldots a_{1})_{\operatorname{cyc}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{k}\ldots a_{1}\\
\text{counterclockwise}\end{subarray}}(a_{k}\ldots a_{1})_{\operatorname{cyc}}.

This finishes the calculation of the superpotential
W
. The final statement on
R_{a}
and
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W)
is now immediate: By
Lemma 7.16
, we have
R_{a}=\partial_{a}W
which simplifies to
R_{a}=r_{a}^{+}-r_{a}^{-}
. In particular, we have
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W)=\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

As preparation for the proof of
Lemma 8.7
, let us fix some terminology regarding the identity location of a zigzag path. Let
L_{i}\in\mathbb{L}
be one of the zigzag paths of
Q
. The identity location of
L_{i}
is a certain arc
a_{0}
. This arc borders precisely two polygons in
Q
. One of the two is clockwise, the other counterclockwise. The
special polygon

P_{i}
of
L_{i}
is the one lying on the left or right side of the arc
a_{0}
, depending on whether where
L_{i}
turns left or right at the head of
a_{0}
, respectively. If
Q
has no punctures of valence 2, this condition can also be expressed as follows:
P
is the polygon which has the identity arc
a_{0}
and its successor within
L_{i}
as part of the boundary.

In case
P_{i}
is clockwise, we denote by
p_{1},\ldots,p_{l}
be the boundary arcs of
P_{i}
, in clockwise order and ending with the identity arc
p_{l}=a_{0}
. In case
P_{i}
is counterclockwise, let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{l}
be the boundary arcs of
P_{i}
, in clockwise order and starting with
p_{1}=a_{0}
. We may call
p_{1},\ldots,p_{l}
the
special polygon sequence
of
L_{i}
. Note that
p_{l}\ldots p_{1}
is only a path in
Q
if
P_{i}
is clockwise, while it is always a path in
\check{Q}
.

Lemma 8.7

(
[
17
, Lemma 10.21]
)
.

Application of the Cho-Hong-Lau construction to the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
yields the familiar potential
\ell=\sum\ell_{v}\in\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
. Altogether, application yields the familiar Landau-Ginzburg model
(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
.

Proof.

The general potential
\ell=\sum_{i\in Q^{\mathbb{L}}_{0}}\ell_{i}
is given by

\ell_{i}=\langle\mu(b,\ldots,b),\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}}\rangle.

Specifically for the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
this description boils down to extracting the identities from the products

\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}).

Denote by
p_{1},\ldots,p_{l}
the special polygon sequence of
L_{i}
, defined in
Remark 8.6
. We claim the coefficient of
\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}}
in the product
\mu(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}})
is precisely

\langle\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}),\mathrm{id}^{*}_{L_{i}}\rangle=\begin{cases}+1&\text{if }e_{k}\ldots e_{1}=p_{l}\ldots p_{1}\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

(8.2)

As in the proof of
Lemma 8.5
, we use our explicit description of the minimal model
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}
. Again, the only disks that count are those not covering any punctures. Let
D
be a (CR, ID, DS or DW) disk contributing to the product (
8.2
). Note that all input morphisms
X_{e_{i}}
are odd and the output is an identity, so
D
is necessarily a CR or ID disk. Similar to the case of the products presented in the proof of
Lemma 8.5
, we conclude that
D
precisely bounds a certain polygon
P
. More precisely, the length
l
of the polygon is equal to the number
k
of inputs of
D
, and the boundary arcs of
P
are equal to
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
in this order.

Let us analyze the properties of
D
. By assumption, the output of
D
is the identity of
L_{i}
. This identity is therefore located on one of the boundary arcs of
P
. Since every single odd intersection located at arcs of the polygon is used as input of
D
, the identity location necessarily of
L_{i}
necessarily lies infinitesimally close to one of the inputs of
D
. We conclude that
D
is necessarily an ID disk.

We claim that
P
is the special polygon
P_{i}
of
L_{i}
. To show this, assume
P
is clockwise. By the definition of ID disks, the input lying close to the output precedes the output. In other words, the identity arc is
e_{k}
and the zigzag path
L_{i}
is the one turning right at the head of
e_{k}
. Assume now
P
is counterclockwise. By definition of ID disks, the input lying close to the output succeeds the output. In other words, the identity arc is
e_{1}
and the zigzag path
L_{i}
is the one turning left at the head of
e_{1}
. In both cases we conclude that
P
is precisely the special polygon
P_{i}
.

We are ready to conclude the product formula (
8.2
). In case
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}=p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
within
Q
is the boundary of the special polygon
P_{i}
, then there is a single disk contributing to the product as we have just seen. We conclude

\langle\mu(X_{e_{k}},\ldots X_{e_{1}}),\mathrm{id}^{*}_{L_{i}}\rangle=+1.

The sign is always positive. Indeed, if
D
lies in a clockwise polygon, then all of its
k
inputs are odd but clockwise with
D
and no
\#
signs appear on the boundary of
D
. If
D
lies in a counterclockwise polygon, then all of its
k
inputs are odd and counterclockwise, while there is also a
\#
-sign in every of the
k
corners of the polygon

In case
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
is not
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
, then there is no disk contributing to the product as we have just seen. We conclude

\langle\mu(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}),\mathrm{id}^{*}_{L_{i}}\rangle=0.

Ultimately, the potential reads

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\check{Q}_{0}}\langle\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(b,\ldots,b),\mathrm{id}^{*}_{L_{i}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\check{Q}_{0}}\ell_{i}\in\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}.

Here for every
i
the letter
\ell_{i}
denotes an arbitrary boundary cycle of a polygon starting at vertex
i\in\check{Q}_{0}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.8

(
[
17
, Proposition 10.30]
)
.

Application of the Cho-Hong-Lau construction to the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
yields the familiar matrix factorizations

F(a)=\mathord{\hbox to145.2pt{\vbox to16.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 66.67155pt\lower-8.33301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-63.33855pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.60483pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
