{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 -2.76 L 0.55 -2.76}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{-1.9919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\text{ for }a\in\operatorname{Ob}\operatorname{Gtl}Q=Q_{1}.

Proof.

By definition, the matrix factorization
F(a)
is given by
(M,\delta)
with module given by

M=\bigoplus_{L_{i}\in\check{Q}_{0}}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q})L_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(L_{i},a).

and differential given by

\delta(m)=(-1)^{\|m\|}\sum\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}\mu(m,b,\ldots,b).

Let us evaluate the module. As seen earlier, there are precisely two indices
i,j
whose hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(L_{i},a)
is non-empty. These indices correspond to the two zigzag paths that cross the arc
a
. Of course, the two zigzag paths leaving
a
might actually be equal, in which case we set
i=j
. If we set
i
to be the zigzag path with the even intersection and
j
the zigzag path with the odd intersection, we have
i=h(a)
and
j=t(a)
. This already gives the shape

(M,\delta)=\mathord{\hbox to142.14pt{\vbox to16.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.60796pt\lower-8.33301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-60.27495pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.60483pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.76 L 23.07 2.76}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{16.87183pt}{1.9919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 -2.76 L 0.55 -2.76}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{-1.9919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

It remains to evaluate the differential
\delta
. Denote the even intersection point by
p\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(L_{h(a)},a)
and the odd intersection point by
q\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(L_{t(a)},a)
. As we have seen earlier, the two products
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(p,b,\ldots,b)
and
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(q,b,\ldots,b)
are computed by MT and MD disks, respectively. For the present non-deformed case, this description boils down to the equations

\displaystyle\delta(h(a)\otimes p)=(-1)^{\|p\|}\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(p,b,\ldots,b)=(-1)^{1+1}a\otimes q,

\displaystyle\delta(t(a)\otimes q)=(-1)^{\|q\|}\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(q,b,\ldots,b)=+\bar{a}\otimes p.

This shows that the module map
(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q})h(a)\to(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q})t(a)
is given by left multiplication with
a
and the module map
(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q})t(a)\to(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q})h(a)
is given by left multiplication with
\bar{a}
.
∎

We are now ready to grasp the mirror functor associated with the specific case of the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
. Indeed, we have computed the specific Landau-Ginzburg model and the mirror objects. The functor itself is not a quasi-equivalence, but it becomes one if we restrict to domain
\operatorname{Gtl}Q\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q
and codomain
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)\subseteq\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
.

Corollary 8.9

(
[
17
, Proposition 10.32]
)
.

If
\check{Q}
is zigzag consistent, the CHL functor
F:\operatorname{Gtl}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

On the level of objects,
F
maps an arc
a\in Q_{1}
to the matrix factorization
F(a)
which is bijective on the level of objects. On the level of hom spaces, we are forced to cheat a little and believe the following fact: Let
L_{1},\ldots,L_{N+1}
be a sequence of
N+1\geq 1
zigzag paths and let
a,b\in Q_{1}
be arcs. Let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{N}
be odd morphisms with
h_{i}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}}(L_{i},L_{i+1})
not containing co-identities, let
m\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(L_{N+1},a)
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(a,b)
be further morphisms. Then we shall assume without proof that the product

\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(\alpha,m,h_{N},\ldots,h_{1})

is computed by smooth immersed disks with Abouzaid sign. A good illustration of these disks can be found in
[
17
, Figure 20]
. This illustration makes it easy to check that
F^{1}
sends an angle
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Gtl}Q}(a,b)
to its associated morphism of matrix factorizations

\pm\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{opp}_{1}\\
\mathrm{opp}_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\text{ or }\pm\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{opp}_{2}&0\\
0&\mathrm{opp}_{1}\end{pmatrix},

depending on whether
\alpha
is odd or even. It was shown in
[
12
]
that these morphisms of matrix factorizations provide a basis for
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)}(F(a),F(b))
. This proves the functor
F
a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

The original mirror functor for punctured surfaces
[
12
]
was defined in a nonconstructive and nonunique way. Its properties known precisely are its values on objects and the first component
F^{1}
. All higher components
F^{\geq 2}
are defined nonconstructively. In contrast, the CHL functor
F:\operatorname{Gtl}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
has explicitly defined higher components, at least if one counts the involved higher products of
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q
as explicit. The CHL functor should therefore be viewed as a modern constructive incarnation of the original mirror functor.

Remark 8.10
.

Cho, Hong and Lau use a different convention for Fukaya categories: In their convention, a disk
D
contributing to a product
\mu(h_{N},\ldots,h_{1})
is supposed to hit the intersection points
h_{1},\ldots,h_{N}
in counterclockwise order. We have decided to stick with the original definition of gentle algebras and let products be given by counting disks hitting the intersection points in clockwise order.

Correspondingly, the conventions for the dual dimer
\check{Q}
also differ: In their convention, the dual dimer
\check{Q}
is obtained from
Q
by flipping over the clockwise faces. In our convention, the dual dimer
\check{Q}
is obtained by flipping over the counterclockwise faces instead. The results differ by a flip of orientation and an inversion of the arrows.

8.2
Midpoint polygons

In this section, we introduce an auxiliary tool for the description of the deformed superpotential and deformed potential in
section 8.4
and
8.5
. The deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction namely expresses superpotential and potential in term of the products on
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
which are in turn enumerated in terms of CR, ID, DS and DW disks. In the present section, we overhaul this description and provide one single type of disks, which we call midpoint polygons.

Definition 8.11
.

A
midpoint polygon
is an immersion of the standard polygon
D:P_{N}\to|Q|
with
N\geq 1
, such that

•

The boundary of
P_{N}
is mapped to (nonempty) zigzag curve segments.

•

The corners are convex and lie on intersections points of zigzag curves.

•

The corners point into the interior of a polygon.

The map
D
itself is taken only up to reparametrization. The corners
h_{1},\ldots,h_{N}
of
D
lie on intersection points of zigzag curves, in other words on the midpoints of arcs
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\in Q_{1}
. We refer to the sequence of arcs as the
arc sequence
of
D
. The midpoint polygon
starts
at arc
a_{1}
and
ends
at arc
a_{N}
. It starts
at the left/right side
of
a_{1}
if the interior of
D
at
a_{1}
lies at the left/right of the arc
a_{1}
in the arc’s natural orientation. It ends
at the left/right side
of
a_{N}
if the interior of
D
lies at the left/right of the arc
a_{N}
in the arc’s natural orientation. A
arc crossing
of
D
is the datum of a single (indexed) arc crossed by one of the zigzag segments of
D
, not counting the corner arcs. Arc crossings are always supposed to come with the datum of their location on the boundary of
D
.

Midpoint polygons are our most general container format for enumerating products in
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
. Midpoint polygons with specific properties or specific additional data can be used to enumerate specific products. By requiring that the corners point into the interior of a polygon, we have incarnated the fact that we only regard disks with odd inputs.

Definition 8.12
.

Let
D
be a midpoint polygon with sides lengths
n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}
. Then the
sign
of
D
is
|D|=\sum(n_{i}-1)/2\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. The
deformation parameter

\operatorname{Punc}(D)\in\mathbb{C}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
is the product of the punctures covered by
D
, counting multiplicities. Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
be the arc sequence of
D
. Then the
path recording
of
D
is the path
\operatorname{Arcs}(D)=e_{k}\ldots e_{1}\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}
.

A sample midpoint polygon is depicted in
4(a)
.

e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}

(a)

Midpoint polygon

n_{i}=3
+
+
-
+
n_{i}=3
-
-
+
-

(b)

Lengths and signs of zigzag segments

Figure 8.4
:
These pictures illustrate midpoint polygons and how we measure the lengths of their boundary segments. In the midpoint polygon on the left, the intersection points are located at arcs
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
,
e_{3}
of
Q
. To this midpoint polygon
D
, we assign the path recording
\operatorname{Arcs}(D)=e_{3}e_{2}e_{1}
and the sign
|D|=3\cdot(7-1)/2=1\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

8.3
Cyclicity and slow growth

In this section, we check that
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
is deformed cyclic and of slow growth. These two properties are technical requirements for the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction according to
Theorem 7.50
. We check the cyclicity property directly in terms of midpoint polygons. By contrast, the slow growth property makes reference also to products between arcs and zigzag paths in
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
and must therefore be dealt with from scratch. We do not prove slow growth with respect to the entire category, but restrict to arcs and zigzag paths.

Lemma 8.13
.

The
A_{\infty}
-deformation
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
is deformed cyclic on odd sequences containing no co-identities.

Proof.

Rotating a midpoint polygon contributing to
\langle\mu_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}}(X_{e_{k-1}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}),Y_{e_{k}}\rangle
gives a midpoint polygon contributing to
\langle\mu_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}}(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{2}}),X_{e_{1}}\rangle
. Their Abouzaid signs and deformation parameters agree. We conclude that both pairings agree, which finishes the proof.
∎

Let us now check the slow growth requirement property. The requirement is slightly problematic in that we are supposed to evaluate products of the form
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},b,\ldots,b)
. Here
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
are morphisms between arbitrary objects of
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
and we do not have these products under control. However, for our purposes it suffices to construct the mirror functor merely on the subcategory
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. Inspecting the construction of the functor
F_{q}
and the proof of
Lemma 7.46
and
Theorem 7.50
, we conclude that for constructing the functor
F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\mathbb{L}},W_{q}),\ell_{q})
it suffices to check the slow growth property of
Definition 7.23
only for morphisms with
m_{1}:L\to a_{1}
and
m_{i}:a_{i-1}\to a_{i}
with
L\in\mathbb{L}_{q}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. We record this as follows:

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
k\geq 0
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. Let
L\in\mathbb{L}_{q}
and
m_{1}:L\to a_{1}
and
m_{i}:a_{i-1}\to a_{i}
for
i=2,\ldots,k
. Then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an
l_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall l\geq l_{0}:\quad\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}^{k+l}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},b,\ldots,b)\in\mathfrak{m}^{n}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{L},a_{k}).

Proof.

The intuition is that the number of
b
insertions is a lower bound for the size of a disk contributing to the product. In other words, the number of punctures covered by a disk is at least as large as the number of
b
insertions, up to a multiplicative constant. If one is willing to assume that the product
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},b,\ldots,b)
is computed by counting disks (which we have not shown), this argument should suffice.

Otherwise, the property can be checked rigorously as follows: According to the minimal model calculation procedure detailed in
[
23
]
, the product can be computed by summing over Kadeishvili trees. In
[
24
]
, we also provide a description of the applicable codifferential
h_{q}
for hom spaces between zigzag paths and for hom spaces from zigzag paths to arcs.

The idea is to measure subtrees containing purely
b
inputs separately. Thanks to the dedicated subdisk construction in
[
24
]
, we see that result components of
h
-trees only consisting of
b
inputs immediately lie in order
\geq n
as soon as they have a certain amount of
K\in\mathbb{N}
inputs. The
h
-trees consisting only of
b
inputs only yield
\beta
(A) result components of order
\geq 1
. Apart from the fact that this already establishes the claim in case
k=0
, this observation is important for what follows.

If
m
is any morphism between zigzag paths or arcs, then we call the maximum length of angles contained in
m
simply the length of
m
. Let
I
be the sum of the lengths of
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
. Let
F
the maximum length of a full turn around a puncture in
Q
.

Pick a result component of the product
\mu(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},b,\ldots,b)
of order
<n
. Let
T
be the Kadeishvili
\pi
-tree which the result component is derived from. We call a node of
T
pure if its subtree purely consumes
b
inputs (instead of
m_{i}
) and if it is maximal with this property
T
(in that its parent does not have this property). We view a subtree at a pure node of
T
as an indecomposable unit. We call all nodes of
T
typical that are non-leaf and not contained in a pure subtree.

Regard a typical node
N\in T
. We claim the result at
N
has length at most one less than its total inputs including outer
\delta
insertions and excluding inner
\delta
inputs and direct
b
inputs. Indeed, the result component at the node is a morphism from a zigzag path to an arc by construction. When
m
is an
H
or
R
basis morphism from a zigzag path to an arc, it can be checked easily that the following products vanish:

h_{q}\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m,\alpha_{3}/\alpha_{4}),~h_{q}\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m,\beta\text{(A)}),~\pi_{q}\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m,\alpha_{3}/\alpha_{4}),~\pi_{q}\mu^{2}_{\operatorname{Add}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m,\beta\text{(A)}).

Therefore the node
N
is necessarily decorated with
h_{q}\mu^{\geq 3}_{\operatorname{Add}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}
or
\pi_{q}\mu^{\geq 3}_{\operatorname{Add}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}
. A first-out or final-out disk definitely reduces the length of the inputs. This shows the claim.

According to the claim just proven, the total length of the output of a typical node
N\in T
is at most
I+nF-s
, where
s
is the number of typical nodes in the subtree at
N
. This bounds the number of typical nodes in
T
by
I+nF
. The subtree at every pure node has at most
K
nodes by assumption. Since there are at most
n
pure nodes, the tree
T
in total has at most
I+nF+nK
nodes. This bounds the total number of nodes in
T
.

Regard a typical node
N
. Among the child nodes of
N
, there are at most
k
typical children and at most
n
pure children. Moreover,
N
may have at most
n
direct outer
\delta~
insertions. This gives rise to already
2n+k
inputs of the
h_{q}\mu_{\operatorname{Add}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}
at
N
. Every string of direct
b
and
\delta
insertions between these
2n+k
inputs is limited to at most
F
items, since otherwise no discrete immersed disk can be made. This means that
N
consumes at most
(2n+k+1)F
many direct
b
inputs. This number is bounded.

The total number of direct
b
inputs at typical nodes is therefore bounded by
(2n+k+1)F(I+nF)
. The total number of
b
inputs used for pure trees is at most
nK
. Therefore the total number of
b
inputs in the tree
T
is bounded by
(2n+k+1)F(I+nF)+nK
.

We have shown that the product
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m_{k},\ldots,m_{1},b,\ldots,b)
can only have nonzero result components of order
<n
if the number of
b
insertions is bounded. In consequence, once the number of
b
insertions exceeds this bound, all result components are necessarily of order
\geq n
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.15
.

The construction of the deformed CHL functor
F_{q}:\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}Q,\ell_{q})
along the procedure of
section 7
is impossible in the specific case of the pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q)
. Indeed, we need to restrict the domain of the functor to the subcategory
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
due to the slow-growth requirements. Let us explain this in a slightly more elaborate fashion.

If the slow-growth property held for the entire category
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
, then we could build a CHL functor
F_{q}:\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
and restrict afterwards to the subcategory
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. The construction and subsequent restriction would be analogous to the process described in
section 8.1
in case of the undeformed pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q)
.

In reality, the slow-growth property is not guaranteed for the entire category
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. For this reason, we cannot define the deformed mirror functor with domain
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. Fortunately, we have proven a restricted slow-growth property
Lemma 8.14
. With this help, the construction of the deformed mirror functor succeeds if from its very beginning we restrict the domain of the functor to consist only of the subcategory
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
.

8.4
Deformed superpotential

In this section, we start applying the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction to the specific pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q)
. The result is a deformed superpotential
W_{q}\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
, which we describe explicitly in terms of midpoint polygons. After that, we describe the resulting deformed Jacobi algebra. In order to obtain a mirror functor according to
Theorem 7.50
, we are required to show that the deformed Jacobi algebra is a deformation of the classical Jacobi algebra. In the present section, we invoke the flatness result
Theorem 6.77
to show that this is the case. Recall that we work under
Convention 5.1
.

Lemma 8.16
.

The deformed superpotential
W_{q}\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
can be expressed as

W_{q}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\text{ clockwise}\\
\text{midpoint polygon}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|D|}\operatorname{Punc}(D)\operatorname{Arcs}(D)~-~\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\text{ counterclockwise}\\
\text{midpoint polygon}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|D|}\operatorname{Punc}(D)\operatorname{Arcs}(D).

Proof.

Let us digest the statement. It is our task to evaluate the products
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}}(X_{e_{k-1}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}})
and extract the coefficient of
Y_{e_{k}}
. By our explicit description of the products from
section 5.3
, these products boil down to counting CR, ID, DS and DW disks. Since ID disks have identity outputs and DS and DW disks are already irrelevant, we are left with the task of counting CR disks.

Let
D
be a CR disk with inputs
X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k-1}}
and output
Y_{e_{k}}
. Since
D
is CR and its inputs contain no co-identities, all of the zigzag segments of
D
are actually non-empty. This immediately renders
D
a midpoint polygon. More precisely, we associate with
D
the midpoint polygon given by the one with the same shape as
D
and arc sequence
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
. Note that this sets up a bijection

\displaystyle\{\text{CR disks with inputs }X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k-1}}\text{ and output }Y_{e_{k}}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\longleftrightarrow\quad\{\text{midpoint polygons with arc sequence }e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\}.

We can therefore write

\displaystyle\langle\mu(X_{e_{k-1}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}}),Y_{e_{k}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{CR disks }D\\
\text{with inputs }X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k-1}}\\
\text{and output }Y_{e_{k}}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\operatorname{Abou}(D)}\operatorname{Punc}(D)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{midpoint polygons }D\\
\text{with arc sequence }e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\operatorname{Abou}(D)}\operatorname{Punc}(D).

Here we have already anticipated that the Abouzaid sign of a CR disk
D
with inputs
X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k-1}}
and output
Y_{e_{k}}
is equal to the Abouzaid sign attached to its associated midpoint polygon by
Definition 8.12
.

In the remainder of the proof, we check that these two signs are indeed equal. Regard one of the CR disks. Its boundary cuts a number of angles of clockwise and counterclockwise polygons of
Q
. Let
n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}
denote the lengths of the zigzag segments, as depicted in
4(b)
. The
\#
signs alternate along the zigzag segments and the individual lengths
n_{i}
are all odd. At every corner
X_{e_{i}}
, the two neighboring signs are equal and in fact determined by whether
D
is clockwise or counterclockwise.

If
D
is clockwise, the sign at every corner is
0\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
and it alternates
n_{i}
times until the next corner. This gives a sign contribution of
(n_{i}-1)/2
. The total sign becomes

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{n_{i}-1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}.

(8.3)

Since all zigzag curves lie clockwise with
D
, this is already the Abouzaid sign of
D
. If
D
is counterclockwise, the sign at every corner is
1\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. In comparison with (
8.3
), we incur a sign flip for all
n_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}
angles that
L
runs through. Since every
n_{i}
is odd, this changes the sign by
k\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. Since all zigzag paths are counterclockwise relative to
D
, the Abouzaid sign also incurs an increase by
k-1\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. This makes the sign precisely opposite to (
8.3
) and finishes the proof.
∎

The deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction proceeds by defining the deformed Jacobi algebra. The construction comes in two variants, depending on whether the category of reference objects
\mathbb{L}
is of slow growth with respect to
\mathcal{C}
or not. In the case of the specific pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q)
, we have seen in
Lemma 8.14
that
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth, at least in a way sufficient for the Cho-Hong-Lau construction. We can therefore apply the slow growth variant of the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction and obtain a deformed Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(\check{Q},W_{q})
according to
Definition 7.25
. We shall abbreviate this algebra by
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
:

Definition 8.17
.

We denote the
deformed Jacobi algebra
by

\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}=\operatorname{Jac}(\check{Q},W_{q})=\frac{\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket}{~\overline{(\partial_{e}W_{q})_{e\in\check{Q}_{1}}}~}.

Remark 8.18
.

Recall that the closure refers to the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic topology on
\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
.

Let us now explain that the deformed Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
is a deformation of the classical Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
in the sense of
Definition 6.1
. We have investigated this question in detail during
section 6
in the general context of superpotential deformations of CY3 Jacobi algebras. We record the specific case of
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
as follows:

Corollary 8.19
.

If
\check{Q}
is cancellation consistent and of bounded type, then
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
.

Proof.

The goal is to invoke
Theorem 6.77
. We have to check two conditions. First,
W_{q}
and therefore also
W^{\prime}=W_{q}-W
is indeed cyclic as shown in
Lemma 8.13
or equivalently
8.16
. Second,
W_{q}
indeed lies in
\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
instead of only
\widehat{\mathbb{C}\check{Q}}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
, since
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth by
Lemma 8.14
.
Finally, we conclude that
Theorem 6.77
applies and
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.20
.

The dual dimer
\check{Q}_{M}
of the standard sphere dimer
Q_{M}
is cancellation consistent. The dimer
\check{Q}_{M}
is also of bounded type because its two polygons have the same length, namely
M
. Therefore
Corollary 8.19
particularly applies to the case
Q=Q_{M}
.

8.5
Deformed potential

In this section, we compute the deformed potential. More precisely, we evaluate the definition of the deformed potential from the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction in the case of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
. The result is an element
\ell_{q}\in\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
. We describe this element in terms of midpoint polygons. Recall that we work under
Convention 5.1
.

Recall that the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction in general requires us to compute a deformed potential
\ell_{q}\in B\widehat{\otimes}Q^{\mathbb{L}}
, given by counting identity outputs of products of the form
\mu(X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}})
. More precisely, the definition reads

\ell_{q}=\sum_{i\in Q^{\mathbb{L}}_{0}}\langle\mu(b,\ldots,b),\mathrm{id}^{*}_{i}\rangle.

It is our task to evaluate this deformed potential for the specific pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q)
. In this specific case, the products
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}}(b,\ldots,b)
are given by an enumeration of CR, ID, DS and DW disks. As announced, DS and DW disks are irrelevant. In fact, the enumeration boils down to counting midpoint polygons of specific type.

Regard a midpoint polygon. Its boundary crosses many arcs. Indeed, it consists of segments of zigzag curves, which run between midpoints of arcs. The longer the segments, the more arcs are crossed. A midpoint polygon may or may not cross arcs that are identity arcs
a_{0}
of zigzag paths. If a midpoint polygon starts at, ends at or crosses an arc that is the identity arc
a_{0}
of a zigzag path, then the midpoint polygon together with the datum of this crossing determines an identity contribution to the product of the intersection points associated with the polygon’s corners. To make this precise, we set up the following terminology:

Definition 8.21
.

Let
L
be a zigzag path in
Q
with identity location
a_{0}
. An
L
-polygon
is one of the following:

•

If
L
turns left at the head of
a_{0}
: a midpoint polygon starting at the left side of
a_{0}
.

•

If
L
turns right at the head of
a_{0}
: a midpoint polygon ending on the right side of
a_{0}
.

•

A midpoint polygon
D
whose segment between the last and first arc is an
L
segment, together with the datum of a crossing of this
L
-segment with the arc
a_{0}\in\,L
.

L
e_{2}
e_{3}
a_{0}=e_{1}

(a)

First type

L
a_{0}=e_{3}
e_{1}
e_{2}

(b)

Second type

a_{0}
L
e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}

(c)

Third type

Figure 8.5
:
These pictures illustrate
L
-polygons of all three types. According to the definition, the midpoint polygon underlying an
L
-polygon of the first type starts on the left side of
a_{0}
. In the first picture, we have depicted this by writing
e_{1}=a_{0}
. The midpoint polygon underlying an
L
-polygon of the second type ends on the right side of
a_{0}
. We have depicted the ending in the second picture as
e_{3}=a_{0}
. The midpoint polygon underlying an
L
-polygon of the third type requires that the zigzag segment between the first and the last arc is
L
and comes with the datum of a crossing with
a_{0}
. In the third picture, we have indicated this crossing by a thick cross.

We have illustrated
L
-polygons in
Figure 8.5
. With this in mind, we can express the potential
\ell_{q}\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
in terms of
L
-polygons:

Lemma 8.22
.

The deformed potential
\ell_{q}=\sum_{i\in\check{Q}_{0}}\ell_{q,i}\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
can be expressed as

\ell_{q,i}=\sum_{L_{i}\text{-polygons }D}(-1)^{|D|}\operatorname{Punc}(D)\operatorname{Arcs}(D).

Proof.

It is our task to evaluate identity terms in the products
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}}(b,\ldots,b)
and match them with
L
-polygons of the three types.

According to the description recapitulated in
section 5.3
, the products
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}}(b,\ldots,b)
are computed by enumerating CR and ID disks. More precisely, a counterclockwise CR disk with inputs
X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k}}
and output
\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}}
corresponds precisely to an
L_{i}
-polygon with arc sequence
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
of the first type. A clockwise CR disk with inputs
X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k}}
and output
\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}}
corresponds precisely to an
L_{i}
-polygon with arc sequence
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
of the second type. An ID disk with inputs
X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k}}
and output
\mathrm{id}_{L_{i}}
corresponds precisely to an
L_{i}
-polygon with arc sequence
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
of the third type. This already matches all identity terms in the products
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}}(b,\ldots,b)
with
L
-polygons.

It remains to check the signs. More precisely, we have to check that the Abouzaid sign of a CR or ID disk contributing to
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}}(b,\ldots,b)
is equal to the sign
|D|\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
of its associated midpoint polygon
D
. To see that both are equal, we distinguish whether
D
is clockwise or counterclockwise. If the disk is clockwise, its Abouzaid sign is simply the sum of the
\#
signs along
D
, which by the proof of
Lemma 8.16
is equal to
|D|
. If
D
is counterclockwise and has
k
corners, its Abouzaid sign is the sum of the
\#
signs plus
k
, which by the proof of
Lemma 8.16
is still
|D|
. This shows that the Abouzaid sign of a disk is equal to the sign
|D|
of the associated midpoint polygon
D
. This proves the desired formula for
\ell_{q,i}
.
∎

The deformed potential
\ell_{q}\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
depends on the choice of identity locations. However, we claim that it becomes independent of this choice when we pass to the deformed Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
:

b_{1}
\ldots
\ldots
b_{2}
\ldots
-
\ldots
=~~b_{2}\partial_{b_{2}}W_{q}

Figure 8.6
:
The difference between two choices of
\ell_{q}
is a relation.

Lemma 8.23
.

The deformed potential
\ell_{q}\in\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}=\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket/\overline{(\partial_{a}W_{q})}
is independent of the choice of identity locations.

Proof.

The idea is to describe the difference of two possible choices explicitly in terms of the relations
\partial_{a}W_{q}
. Denote by
L_{1},\ldots,L_{N}
the zigzag paths of
Q
. Recall that the element
\ell_{q}
is the sum of elements
\ell_{q,i}
over all
1\leq i\leq N
. The identity location on a certain zigzag path
L_{i}
only impacts the element
\ell_{q,i}
and no other summands. It therefore suffices to study only a single element
\ell_{q,i}
. It also suffices to assume that the two choices of identity locations to be studied are arcs which are neighbors along
L_{i}
. The strategy is to enumerate
L_{i}
-polygons with respect to the two choices.

Let us denote the two neighboring arcs are
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
, such that
h(b_{1})=t(b_{2})
and
L_{i}
turns left at the head of
b_{1}
and right at the head of
b_{2}
. The situation is depicted in
Figure 8.6
. We claim that
L_{i}
-polygons with respect to
a_{0}=b_{1}
and
L_{i}
-polygons with respect to
a_{0}=b_{2}
are almost the same. Indeed, the typical
L_{i}
-polygon with respect to
a_{0}=b_{1}
has a very long
\tilde{L}_{i}
-segment and many crossings with
b_{1}
. Since
b_{2}
is the neighbor of
b_{1}
, all these
L_{i}
-polygons can be interpreted alternatively as
L_{i}
-polygons with respect to
a_{0}=b_{2}
. The only difference lies in the corner cases.

To be very precise, the only
L_{i}
-polygons with respect to
a_{0}=b_{1}
which cannot be interpreted as
L_{i}
-polygons with respect to
a_{0}=b_{2}
are those which start at
b_{1}
and end at
b_{2}
, with just the shortest possible
\tilde{L}_{i}
-segment in between, consisting of a single angle. The only
L_{i}
-polygons with respect to
a_{0}=b_{2}
which cannot be interpreted as
L_{i}
-polygons with respect to
a_{0}=b_{1}
are those which end at
b_{2}
. Let us denote by
\ell_{q,i}^{(b_{1})}
and
\ell_{q,i}^{(b_{2})}
the two potentials in
\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
obtained from the two choices of identity locations. In these terms, we conclude

\displaystyle\ell_{q,i}^{(b_{2})}-\ell_{q,i}^{(b_{1})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{clockwise}\\
\text{midpoint polygons }D\\
\text{ending at }b_{2}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Punc}(D)\operatorname{Arcs}(D)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{counterclockwise}\\
\text{midpoint polygons }D\\
\text{ending at }b_{2}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Punc}(D)\operatorname{Arcs}(D)

\displaystyle=b_{2}\partial_{b_{2}}W_{q}.

This shows that the difference of
\ell_{q,i}^{(b_{1})}
and
\ell_{q,i}^{(b_{2})}
is a relation in the deformed Jacobi algebra. In conclusion, the potential is independent of the choice of identity locations.
∎

8.6
Deformed mirror objects

In this section, we compute the deformed mirror objects. More precisely, we evaluate the definition of the deformed mirror functor for the arc objects
a\in\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. We describe these objects
F_{q}(a)\in\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
in terms of midpoint polygons. Recall that we work under
Convention 5.1
. As we have seen in
section 8.1
, the mirror functor
F:\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
sends the arcs
a\in Q_{1}
to very explicit matrix factorizations
F(a)
. We provide in this section an explicit computation of the deformed matrix factorizations
F_{q}(a)
, which is in fact likewise explicit:

arc

{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 -2.76 L 0.55 -2.76}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{-1.9919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.82774pt}{-11.20267pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
\xmapsto{~~~F_{q}~~~}

To compute the object
F_{q}(X)
for
X\in\mathcal{C}_{q}
, we are in general required to find for every
L_{i}\in\mathbb{L}
the hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{q}}(L_{i},X)
. We also need to compute the products
\mu_{q}(m,b,\ldots,b)
where
m\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{q}}(L_{i},X)
is a morphism from one of the reference objects to
X
.

For the specific instance
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
of the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction, we have described the hom spaces and the relevant products already
section 5.4
. Computing the mirror objects
F_{q}(a)
for
a\in\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
now boils down to evaluating the products
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m,b,\ldots,b)
, where
m\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(L,a)
is an even or odd intersection point between
L
and
a
. Recall that the products for even
m
are easy to express, while the products for odd
m
are computed by MD disks. We shall simplify the description of these products here in terms of midpoint polygons.

Definition 8.24
.

Let
a\in Q_{1}
be an arc. Define the
deformed complement
of
a
as

\bar{a}_{q}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{clockwise}\\
\text{midpoint polygons }D\\
\text{ending at }a\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Punc}(D)~\partial_{a}\operatorname{Arcs}(D)\in\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}.

Corollary 8.25
.

The deformed mirror objects are the deformed matrix factorizations given by

F_{q}(a)=\mathord{\hbox to129.86pt{\vbox to30.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.4688pt\lower-17.39676pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.89296pt}{5.5249pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 -2.76 L 0.55 -2.76}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{-1.9919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.82774pt}{-11.20267pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

Proof.

It is our task to evaluate the products
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m,b,\ldots,b)
. Here
m
is an even or odd intersection point between a zigzag path and an arc. We shall regard the two cases separately and explain the relevant products.

First of all, fix an arc
a\in Q_{1}
. Let
L
be the zigzag path turning left at the head of
a
and
L^{\prime}
the zigzag path turning right at the head of
a
. Let
m:L\to a
be the odd intersection point and
m^{*}:L^{\prime}\to a
the even intersection point. Regard the odd morphism
X_{a}:L\to L^{\prime}
of zigzag paths. According to
Lemma 5.20
, the product
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m^{*},X_{a})
is equal to
-m
and it is the only nonvanishing term in
\mu(m^{*},b,\ldots,b)
. We conclude

\delta(m^{*})=(-1)^{\|m^{*}\|}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}^{l+1}(m^{*},b,\ldots,b)=am.

Regard now the odd intersection point
m:L\to a
and regard a sequence
X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{N}}
of odd basis morphisms with
X_{e_{i}}:L_{i}\to L_{i+1}
and
L_{N+1}=L
. According to
Lemma 5.20
, we can describe the product
\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m,X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}})
by means of MD disks with output
m^{*}
:

\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}(m,X_{e_{k}},\ldots,X_{e_{1}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\text{ MD disk}\\
\text{with inputs }X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k}},m\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Punc}(D)m^{*}.

Unraveling the definition, an MD disk with inputs
X_{e_{1}},\ldots,X_{e_{k}},m
is the same as a midpoint polygon with arc sequence
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k},a
. We can therefore write

\delta(m)=(-1)^{\|m\|}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}^{l+1}(m,b,\ldots,b)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{clockwise}\\
\text{midpoint polygons }D\\
\text{ending at }a\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Punc}(D)~\partial_{a}\operatorname{Arcs}(D)m^{*}=\bar{a}_{q}m^{*}.

In summary, we have computed the map
\delta
both on the odd hom space
\operatorname{Hom}(L,a)
and the even hom space
\operatorname{Hom}(L^{\prime},a)
. This finishes the proof.
∎

m
out
\mu^{0}_{q}
b
b
b
b

Figure 8.7
:
The picture depicts the products
\mu(\mu^{0}_{q},m,b,\ldots,b)
, which determine the curvature of
F_{q}(a)
. The products can be interpreted as smooth immersed disks between odd intersections of zigzag curves, with a small embayment given by intermediately tracing an arc. The longer a disk, the more arcs can be embayed and the more it contributes to curvature of mirror objects. The less punctures a disk is supposed to cover, the shorter it is, which shows nicely why the classical mirror objects
F(a)
are curvature-free.

Remark 8.26
.

The mirror objects
F_{q}(a)\in\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
have curvature. Explicitly, their curvature reads

\mu^{0}_{\operatorname{MF},F_{q}(a)}(m)=\ell_{q}m-\delta^{2}(m)=(-1)^{|m|}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}^{l+2}(\mu_{\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q}^{0},m,b,\ldots,b).

In
Figure 8.7
, we have depicted how to interpret this curvature geometrically.

8.7
Main result

In this section, we state our main result. It consists of a wide range of deformed mirror equivalences for punctured surfaces. The A-side is the deformed gentle algebra
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
and the B-side is the deformed category of matrix factorizations
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
. When dividing out the maximal ideal, one recovers the classical mirror symmetry originally described in
[
12
]
. More specifically, one recovers the classical mirror functor of Cho, Hong and Lau defined in
[
17
, Chapter 10]
.

Remark 8.27
.

For sake of completeness, we provide here a brief overview of the setting. The starting point is a dimer
Q
which is geometrically consistent or a standard sphere dimer
Q_{M}
with
M\geq 3
. We assume that the dual dimer
\check{Q}
is zigzag consistent and of bounded type. The bounded type requirement for dimers is detailed in
section 6.10
. For instance, a cancellation consistent dimer on a torus or without triangles is automatically of bounded type.

Our mirror functor is a specific instance of the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction of
section 7
. The input pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q)
for this specific instance consists of derived category
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
of the deformed gentle algebra together with the minimal model
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
of the deformed category of zigzag paths. In
section 8.4
, we have started evaluating the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction for this specific instance and obtained an explicit description of the deformed superpotential
W_{q}
and deformed Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
. In
section 8.5
, we have obtained an explicit description of the deformed potential
\ell_{q}
. The general deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction of
section 7.6
together with
Remark 8.15
about the restriction to the subcategory
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\subseteq\,\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
provides us with a functor

F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\,\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q}).

In
section 8.6
, we have computed explicitly the mirror objects
F_{q}(a)
for
a\in\check{Q}_{1}
. We denote by
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
the subcategory consisting of these objects:

\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})=\{F_{q}(a)\}_{a\in\,\check{Q}_{1}}\subseteq\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q}).

Remark 8.28
.

In our main result, we wish to present a mirror functor of
A_{\infty}
-deformations. At the present point, both the Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
and the category
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
however do not come with a canonical identification as completed tensor product of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
and
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
with
B
. We amend this by the following procedure:

•

The algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
in the sense that there exists a deformation
\mu_{\operatorname{Jac},q}
of the product of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
and a
\mathbb{C}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket
-linear algebra isomorphism

\varphi_{\operatorname{Jac},q}:\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathbb{C}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\mu_{\operatorname{Jac},q}).

This identification is not canonical and depends on choice. For sake of the construction, we shall fix one identification and view
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
as
(\mathbb{C}\llbracket Q_{0}\rrbracket\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\mu_{\operatorname{Jac},q})
.

•

The category
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
is by construction only a loose object-cloning deformation of the category
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
. This means that its objects are not the same as the objects of
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
and its hom spaces cannot be naturally identified with the completed tensor product of the hom spaces of
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
. Instead, such identification depends on choice. We have elaborated on this phenomenon in
section 7.6
. We amend this by regarding the subcategory
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
instead of
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
.

•

The category
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
is by construction only a loose deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
. This entails that the objects
F_{q}(a)
are in correspondence with the objects
F(a)
, but the hom spaces have not been identified. We amend this by noting that the specific projective modules contained in
F_{q}(a)
are of the form
(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q})L_{i}
. These projective modules come with natural isomorphisms

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}}((\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q})L_{i},(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q})L_{j})\xrightarrow{\sim}L_{i}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q})L_{j}\xrightarrow{\sim}B\widehat{\otimes}L_{i}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q})L_{j}.

We can therefore identify the hom spaces of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
as

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})}(F_{q}(a),F_{q}(b))\xrightarrow{\sim}B\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)}(F(a),F(b)).

(8.4)

This way, we can view
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
as an actual
A_{\infty}
-deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
.

•

The mirror functor
F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
is by construction only a functor of loose
A_{\infty}
-deformations. This means that it does not have a naturally defined leading term. However, once we interpret
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
as an actual
A_{\infty}
-deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
, the functor
F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
becomes a functor of actual
A_{\infty}\,
-deformations and does have a well-defined leading term.

In
Theorem 8.29
we present the main result. It is a specific instance of
Theorem 7.50
and yields a wide range of deformed mirror equivalences. In the statement of the result, we have already applied the reinterpretation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
as actual
A_{\infty}
-deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
, according to
Remark 8.28
.

Theorem 8.29
.

Let
Q
be a geometrically consistent dimer or standard sphere dimer
Q_{M}
with
M\geq 3
. Assume that the dual dimer
\check{Q}
is zigzag consistent and of bounded type. Denote by
(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
the deformed Landau-Ginzburg model associated with
Q
. Then:

1.

The algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
.

2.

The category
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
is an
A_{\infty}
-deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
.

3.

The deformed Cho-Hong-Lau functor provides a quasi-isomorphism of deformed
A_{\infty}
-categories

F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\xrightarrow{~~~\sim~~~}\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q}).

4.

The leading term of
F_{q}
is the classical Cho-Hong-Lau functor
F:\operatorname{Gtl}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
.

Proof.

This is essentially a restatement of
Theorem 7.50
, applied to the specific pair
(\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q},\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q)
under the help of
Remark 8.15
. Although we have already worked with the functor
F_{q}
in
section 8.6
, we shall recapitulate here why the conditions of
Theorem 7.50
are satisfied. After that, we comment on the first, second, fourth and third claimed statement, in this order.

Let us explain that all requirements of
Theorem 7.50
are satisfied. To start with, we briefly trace the requirements according to
Convention 7.22
. The category
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
is strictly unital with the same identities as
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}
. It comes with a non-degenerate odd pairing and a choice of CHL basis. As shown in
Lemma 8.13
, the deformed
A_{\infty}
-structure is cyclic on the odd part. As we explained in
Remark 8.15
, we restrict the construction of the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau functor to the domain
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. We have seen in
section 5
that the hom spaces between zigzag paths and arcs are finite-dimensional. This establishes all requirements of
Convention 7.22
, adapted to the restriction of the functor to
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
.

The other requirements of
Theorem 7.50
are that
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth and that
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
. In
Lemma 8.14
we have indeed verified that
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth, at least to an extent sufficient for application of the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau construction when restricting the functor to
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. In
Corollary 8.19
, we have verified that
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
is a deformation of
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
. This already proves the first claimed statement.

We see that all requirements of
Theorem 7.50
are satisfied, adapted to the restriction of the functor to
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
. Invoke the slow growth version of
Theorem 7.50
and conclude that the large category
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
is a loose object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
. Furthermore, the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau functor defines a functor of loose object-cloning deformations

F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q}).

Note that the functor is restricted to
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\subseteq\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
because we have only partially verified that
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
is of slow growth. We now explain the second, third and fourth claimed statements of the theorem.

For the second statement of the theorem, we explain why
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
is a deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
. Indeed, the subcategory
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
consists of the objects
F_{q}(a)
. As such, it is a loose object-cloning deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
via the object-cloning map

\displaystyle O:\operatorname{Ob}(\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}\operatorname{Ob}(\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)),

\displaystyle F_{q}(a)

\displaystyle\xmapsto{~~~\phantom{\sim}~~~}F(a).

Since the object-cloning map
O
is in fact a bijection, we can call
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
simply a loose deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}Q,\ell)
instead of a loose object-cloning deformation. Upon the further identification (
8.4
), we can naturally view
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
as an actual deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
.

For the fourth statement, regard the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau functor
F_{q}
provided to us by
Theorem 7.50
. We merely restrict the codomain of
F_{q}
to the subcategory
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}Q,\ell_{q})\subseteq\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}Q,\ell_{q})
:

F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q}).

This restriction is a bijection on object level. In
Theorem 7.50
, we have investigated its leading term. By definition, the leading term entails dividing out
\mathfrak{m}
on hom spaces on both sides. We have shown the leading term is
F
when the following subsequent identification is applied:

\frac{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})}(F_{q}(a),F_{q}(b))}{(Q_{0})\cdot\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})}(F_{q}(a),F_{q}(b))}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)}(F(a),F(b)),

(8.5)

Under the present interpretation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
as actual deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
, we can go a step further. Indeed, passing
F_{q}
to the quotient by
\mathfrak{m}
and subsequently applying (
8.5
) is equivalent to interpreting
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
as actual deformation of
\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
by means of (
8.4
) and taking the leading term of the functor of actual
A_{\infty}
-deformations
F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
. This shows that
F
is the leading term of the functor
F_{q}
of actual deformed
A_{\infty}
-categories. This proves the fourth statement.

For the third statement, recall from
Corollary 8.9
that the classical Cho-Hong-Lau functor
F:\operatorname{Gtl}Q\to\operatorname{mf}(\operatorname{Jac}Q,\ell)
is a quasi-isomorphism. Since
F
is the leading term of
F_{q}
, the functor
F_{q}
is then also a quasi-isomorphism in the sense of
[
23
]
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.30
.

The classical mirror functor
F:\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q},\ell)
is not quasi-fully-faithful on the entire category
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}Q
. For instance, a narrow loop around a puncture is mapped to the zero object, because the loop does not intersect any zigzag curves. While we have formally not verified the slow growth condition for
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
with respect to the entire category
\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
, suppose we obtain a deformed Cho-Hong-Lau functor
F_{q}:\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
. This functor would then not be quasi-fully-faithful either, in the sense of
[
23
]
.

Remark 8.31
.

We can build another functor
\tilde{F}_{q}:\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{H}\operatorname{Tw}\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
by extending the deformed Cho-Hong-Lau functor
F_{q}:\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q\to\operatorname{MF}(\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q},\ell_{q})
to the deformed twisted completion and passing to the deformed minimal model, in the sense of
[
23
]
. In contrast to
F_{q}
, the functor
\tilde{F}_{q}
is quasi-fully-faithful.

Appendix A
Examples

In this section, we present two examples of deformed mirror symmetry. We consider the 3-punctured sphere and a 4-punctured torus. In both cases we determine the deformed Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
and the deformed potential
\ell_{q}
explicitly.

A.1
3-punctured sphere

In this section, we exhibit mirror symmetry and deformed mirror symmetry for the 3-punctured sphere. We start by explaining the dimer involved together with its zigzag curve. Then we turn to the Jacobi algebra and the mirror objects. We explain all midpoint polygons and compute the deformed Landau-Ginzburg model and the deformed matrix factorizations.

\check{Q}

The deformed superpotential

The deformed gentle algebra
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
has three deformation parameters
q_{a}
,
q_{b}
and
q_{c}
. To compute the deformed superpotential, we have to enumerate all midpoint polygons in
Q
. There are in total 12 midpoint polygons contributing to
W_{q}
, depicted in
Figure A.2
and
A.3
. They fall into the following four groups:
Three of them are 3-gons on the front side and have arc sequences
abc
,
bca
and
cab
(sign
+1
). They are depicted in
2(a)
.
Three of them are 3-gons on the rear side and have arc sequences
cba
,
bac
and
acb
(sign
-1
). They are depicted in
2(b)
.
Three of them are monogons mainly lying on the front side with arc sequences
a
,
b
,
c
(sign
+1
,
q
-parameters
q_{a}
,
q_{b}
,
q_{c}
, respectively). They are depicted in
3(a)
,
3(b)
and
3(c)
.
Three of them are monogons mainly lying on the rear side with arc sequences
a
,
b
,
c
(sign
-1
,
q
-parameters
q_{a}
,
q_{b}
,
q_{c}
, respectively). They are depicted in
3(d)
,
3(e)
and
3(f)
.
We conclude that the deformed terms cancel each other and we are left with

W_{q}=W=(abc)_{\operatorname{cyc}}-(cba)_{\operatorname{cyc}}\in\mathbb{C}\langle a,b,c\rangle.

The deformed Jacobi algebra is
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}=\mathbb{C}[a,b,c]\llbracket q_{a},q_{b},q_{c}\rrbracket
.

The deformed potential

The expression for the deformed potential depends on the choice of identity location for the zigzag path
L
. Let us choose the identity to lie on the copy of
a
which turns right at head and tail of
a
. This choice is depicted in
Figure A.4
. The deformed potential
\ell_{q}
is determined by enumerating
L
-polygons. The specific choice of
a_{0}
gives the following four
L
-polygons. We list these polygons here and recapitulate in technical terms from which part of the minimal model
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
they come from:

•

One
L
-polygon is a 3-gon lying on the front side and ending at the right side of
a
. The underlying midpoint polygon is the same as the one depicted in
2(a)
. Its contribution to
\ell_{q}
is
abc
. In terms of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
, it concerns an ID disk.

•

One
L
-polygon is a monogon mainly lying on the front side and ending at the right side of
a
. The underlying midpoint polygon is the same as the one depicted in
3(a)
. Its contribution to
\ell_{q}
is
-q_{a}a
. In terms of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
, it concerns an ID disk.

•

One
L
-polygon is a monogon mainly lying on the rear side and having a crossing with
a_{0}
. The underlying midpoint polygon is the same as the one depicted in
3(e)
. Its contribution to
\ell_{q}
is
-q_{b}b
. In terms of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
, it concerns a CR disk.

•

One
L
-polygon is a monogon mainly lying on the front side and having a crossing with
a_{0}
. The underlying midpoint polygon is the same as the one depicted in
3(c)
. Its contribution to
\ell_{q}
is
-q_{c}c
. In terms of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
, it concerns a CR disk.

The total deformed potential amounts to

\ell_{q}=abc-q_{a}a-q_{b}b-q_{c}c\in\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}.

We immediately see that
\ell_{q}\in\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}=\mathbb{C}[a,b,c]\llbracket q_{a},q_{b},q_{c}\rrbracket
is still central.

The deformed mirror objects

The deformed mirror objects
F_{q}(a)
,
F_{q}(b)
,
F_{q}(c)
can be determined by enumerating clockwise midpoint polygons ending at
a
,
b
and
c
, respectively. For the arc
a
, there are two such midpoint polygons. The first is a 3-gon and has
\partial_{a}\operatorname{Arcs}(D)=bc
, sign
+1
and no
q
-parameters. The second is a monogon and has
\partial_{a}\operatorname{Arcs}(D)=1
, sign
-1
and
q
-parameter
q_{a}
. Both add up to a contribution of
bc-q_{a}
. The deformed mirror object is therefore

Table A.5
:
The deformed mirror objects

A.2
4-punctured torus

In this section, we exhibit mirror symmetry and deformed mirror symmetry for a 4-punctured torus dimer. We start by explaining the dimer involved together with its zigzag curve. Then we turn to the Jacobi algebra and the mirror objects. We explain all midpoint polygons and compute the deformed Landau-Ginzburg model and the deformed matrix factorizations. Centrality of the deformed Landau-Ginzburg potential obtained is not obvious, and we provide a manual check.

The dimer and its mirror

The torus dimer we regard is depicted in
6(a)
. The dimer has four punctures
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
,
q_{3}
,
q_{4}
, eight arcs
a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}
and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{4}
and four elementary polygons. It has four zigzag paths and is geometrically consistent. The associated zigzag curves are depicted in
6(c)
. The figure also depicts a few sample midpoint polygons of different sizes (without indication which is the first and the last midpoint).

The mirror dimer
\check{Q}
is a 4-punctured torus as well, depicted in
6(b)
. In the numbering of the figure, the correspondence between the punctures
1,2,3,4
of
\check{Q}
and zigzag paths of
Q
is as follows:

\displaystyle 1\in\check{Q}_{0}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow L_{1}=\ldots,b_{1},a_{2},b_{4},a_{3},\ldots

\displaystyle 2\in\,\check{Q}_{0}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow L_{2}=\ldots,b_{2},a_{3},b_{3},a_{2},\ldots

\displaystyle 3\in\,\check{Q}_{0}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow L_{3}=\ldots,a_{1},b_{2},a_{4},b_{3},\ldots

\displaystyle 4\in\,\check{Q}_{0}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow L_{4}=\ldots,a_{1},b_{4},a_{4},b_{1},\ldots

For instance, the two associated zigzag curves
\tilde{L}_{1}
and
\tilde{L}_{2}
are depicted in
8(a)
. The superpotential
W\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}
equals

W=(b_{1}a_{4}b_{2}a_{2})_{\operatorname{cyc}}+(b_{4}a_{1}b_{3}a_{3})_{\operatorname{cyc}}-(b_{2}a_{3}b_{1}a_{1})_{\operatorname{cyc}}-(b_{3}a_{2}b_{4}a_{4})_{\operatorname{cyc}}.

The Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}=\mathbb{C}\check{Q}/(\partial_{a}W)_{a\in Q_{1}}
is a noncommutative quiver algebra with relations. The dimer
\check{Q}
is zigzag consistent and therefore
\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
is CY3. Its 12 relations read

\displaystyle a_{4}b_{2}a_{2}

\displaystyle=a_{1}b_{2}a_{3},

\displaystyle a_{2}b_{1}a_{4}

\displaystyle=a_{3}b_{1}a_{1},

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=\ldots.

The potential
\ell\in\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
reads

\ell=(b_{1}a_{4}b_{2}a_{2})_{\operatorname{cyc}}.

The mirror objects are the eight matrix factorizations

\displaystyle M_{a_{1}}=\mathord{\hbox to108.11pt{\vbox to31.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.51888pt\lower-17.30234pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-42.18587pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(c)

A few midpoint polygons

Figure A.6
:
Illustration of 4-punctured torus

The deformed superpotential

The deformed gentle algebra
\operatorname{Gtl}_{q}Q
has four deformation parameters
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
,
q_{3}
,
q_{4}
. To compute the deformed superpotential
W_{q}
, we have to enumerate all midpoint polygons in
Q
. As can be seen from
6(c)
, there are infinitely many midpoint polygons. All midpoint polygons are rectangular, in particular for the sign
|D|
we have
|D|=0\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
for every midpoint polygon
D
. The midpoint polygons can be classified into 64 different types according to their arc sequence, or 16 if one takes arc sequences up to cyclic permutation. All 16 types are listed in
Table A.7
.

The midpoint polygons of any type are a family indexed by natural numbers
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, standing for the width and height of the polygon. More precisely, define side lengths of a midpoint polygon to be the number of angles cut by the zigzag segments. In these terms, every family comes with a minimally small midpoint polygon and all larger midpoint polygons in the family are derived from this minimal version by extending side lengths by multiples of
4
. For every of the 16 types, we have indicated in
Table A.7
the
q
-parameters of the midpoint polygon of size
(k,l)
. This way, we have efficiently enumerated all midpoint polygons and their properties in 16 types and two parameters.

We use the following abbreviations:

q=q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4},~q_{14}=q_{1}q_{4},~q_{23}=q_{2}q_{3}.

With this in mind, the deformed superpotential can be expressed as a sum over sixteen terms, eight of which actually cancel out due to symmetry of the 4-punctured torus:

\displaystyle W_{q}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{8 clockwise}\\
\text{types}\end{subarray}}\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\operatorname{Punc}(D)\operatorname{Arcs}(D)_{\operatorname{cyc}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{8 counterclockwise}\\
\text{types}\end{subarray}}\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\operatorname{Punc}(D)\operatorname{Arcs}(D)_{\operatorname{cyc}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}q_{23}^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}-\sum_{k,l\geq 0}q(q_{14}q)^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)(b_{2}a_{2}b_{1}a_{4})_{\operatorname{cyc}}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}q(q_{23}q)^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}-\sum_{k,l\geq 0}q_{14}^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)(b_{3}a_{2}b_{4}a_{4})_{\operatorname{cyc}}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}q_{23}^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}-\sum_{k,l\geq 0}q(q_{14}q)^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)(b_{3}a_{3}b_{4}a_{1})_{\operatorname{cyc}}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}q(q_{23}q)^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}-\sum_{k,l\geq 0}q_{14}^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)(b_{2}a_{3}b_{1}a_{1})_{\operatorname{cyc}}.

The deformed Jacobi algebra is
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}=\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}\rrbracket/\overline{(\partial_{a}W_{q})_{a\in\check{Q}_{1}}}
. Here
\overline{(\partial_{a}W_{q})_{a\in\check{Q}_{1}}}
denotes the closure of the ideal generated by the derivatives
\partial_{a}W_{q}\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}\rrbracket
with respect to the
(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4})
-adic topology.

Enumeration of midpoint polygons

The deformed potential

The deformed potential
\ell_{q}
as element of
\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}\rrbracket
is the sum of four potentials, one for each of the four zigzag paths:

\ell_{q}=\ell_{q,1}+\ell_{q,2}+\ell_{q,3}+\ell_{q,4}.

The expression for the deformed potential
\ell_{q,i}
as element of
\mathbb{C}\check{Q}\llbracket q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}\rrbracket
depends on the choice of identity location for the zigzag path
L_{i}
. For instance, let us choose the identity of the zigzag path
L_{1}=\ldots,a_{2},b_{1},a_{3},b_{4},\ldots
to be the arc
a_{2}
. Then the summands
\ell_{q,1}
and
\ell_{q,2}
read

\displaystyle\ell_{q,1}=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}lq_{23}^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}b_{1}a_{4}b_{2}a_{2}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(l+1)q(q_{14}q)^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)b_{1}a_{4}b_{2}a_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}lq_{4}(q_{23}q)^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(l+1)q_{4}q_{14}^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)b_{1}a_{1}b_{3}a_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}lq_{3}q_{23}^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(l+1)q_{3}(q_{14}q)^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)b_{4}a_{1}b_{2}a_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}lq(q_{23}q)^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(l+1)q_{14}^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)b_{4}a_{4}b_{3}a_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(l+1)q_{2}q_{23}^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}lq_{2}(q_{14}q)^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)b_{1}a_{4}b_{3}a_{3}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(l+1)q(q_{23}q)^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}lq_{14}^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)b_{1}a_{1}b_{2}a_{3}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}lq_{23}^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(l+1)q(q_{14}q)^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)b_{4}a_{1}b_{3}a_{3}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}lq_{1}(q_{23}q)^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(l+1)q_{1}q_{14}^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)b_{4}a_{4}b_{2}a_{3}

and

\displaystyle\ell_{q,2}=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(k+1)q_{23}^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}kq(q_{14}q)^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)a_{2}b_{1}a_{4}b_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(k+1)q_{4}(q_{23}q)^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}kq_{4}q_{14}^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)a_{2}b_{1}a_{1}b_{3}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(k+1)q_{3}(q_{23})^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}kq_{3}(q_{14}q)^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)a_{2}b_{4}a_{1}b_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(k+1)q(q_{23}q)^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}kq_{14}^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)a_{2}b_{4}a_{4}b_{3}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}k(q_{23})^{l}(q_{14})^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(k+1)q(q_{14}q)^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)a_{3}b_{4}a_{1}b_{3}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(k+1)q_{1}(q_{23}q)^{l}q_{14}^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}kq_{1}q_{14}^{l}(q_{23}q)^{k}q^{2kl}\right)a_{3}b_{4}a_{4}b_{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}kq_{2}q_{23}^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(k+1)q_{2}(q_{14}q)^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)a_{3}b_{1}a_{4}b_{3}

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k,l\geq 0}(k+1)q(q_{23}q)^{l}(q_{14}q)^{k}q^{2kl}+\sum_{k,l\geq 0}kq_{14}^{l}q_{23}^{k}q^{2kl}\right)a_{3}b_{1}a_{1}b_{2}.

Centrality

The general mirror construction guarantees that the deformed potential
\ell_{q}
is central in the deformed Jacobi algebra
\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
. In what follows, we verify centrality manually in the case of the 4-punctured torus. Our starting point is the explicit description of
W_{q}
and
\ell_{q}
in terms of midpoint polygons. It turns out that centrality is not as obvious as in the classical case of
\ell\in\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
. The checks we present incarnate particular cases of the
A_{\infty}
-relations for
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
, in fact going beyond the transversal case. Checking centrality therefore presents evidence that the description of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
in terms of CR, ID, DS and DW disks is accurate and is therefore evidence for the correctness of
[
24
]
.

To get started, recall that proving
\ell_{q}
central entails finding for every arc
a\in\check{Q}_{1}
an element
x\in\overline{(\partial_{a}W_{q})}
such that
a\ell_{q}+x=\ell_{q}a
within
\mathbb{C}\check{Q}
. In the classical case of
\ell\in\operatorname{Jac}\check{Q}
, this element
x
can be described as a sequence of F-term flips of the path
\ell a\in\mathbb{C}\check{Q}
, see
section 6.10
. In the deformed case of
\ell_{q}\in\operatorname{Jac}_{q}\check{Q}
, the potential
\ell_{q}
is much more complicated and the element
x
can only be obtained by inspecting and Koszul dualizing the
A_{\infty}
-relations of
\operatorname{H}\mathbb{L}_{q}
.
